Tensor Topic Modeling Via HOSVD
Yating Liu, Claire Donnat

Abstract
By representing documents as mixtures of topics, topic modeling has allowed the successful analysis of datasets across a wide spectrum of applications ranging from ecology to genetics. An important body of recent work has demonstrated the computational and statistical efficiency of probabilistic Latent Semantic Indexing (pLSI)– a type of topic modeling— in estimating both the topic matrix (corresponding to distributions over word frequencies), and the topic assignment matrix. However, these methods are not easily extendable to the incorporation of additional temporal, spatial, or document-specific information, thereby potentially neglecting useful information in the analysis of spatial or longitudinal datasets that can be represented as tensors. Consequently, in this paper, we propose using a modified higher-order singular value decomposition (HOSVD) to estimate topic models based on a Tucker decomposition, thus accommodating the complexity of tensor data. Our method exploits the strength of tensor decomposition in reducing data to lower-dimensional spaces and successfully recovers lower-rank topic and cluster structures, as well as a core tensor that highlights interactions among latent factors. We further characterize explicitly the convergence rate of our method in entry-wise ℓ 1 \ell_{1} norm. Experiments on synthetic data demonstrate the statistical efficiency of our method and its ability to better capture patterns across multiple dimensions. Additionally, our approach also performs well when applied to large datasets of research abstracts and in the analysis of vaginal microbiome data. Keywords: Tucker Decomposition, Topic Modeling, Higher-order Singular Value Decomposition, pLSI.



1
Introduction

Consider a corpus of
N
documents, each consisting of
M
words taking values in a dictionary of size
R
. From a mathematical perspective, this data can be summarized in a document-term matrix
\mathbf{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times R}
whose
(i,r)^{th}
entry records the number of times word
r
has occurred in document
i
. The objective of topic modeling is to extract a set of
K
topics from the matrix
\mathbf{Y}
(usually encoded as a matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times R}
, where rows correspond to different topics), as well as a topic assignment matrix
\mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times K}
that captures the topic proportions present in each document. In this setting, topics are understood as distinct distributions over word frequencies. It is customary to assume that each row of the matrix
\mathbf{Y}
follows a multinomial distribution:

\mathbf{Y}_{i\cdot}\sim\text{Multinomial}(M,\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbf{W}_{ik}\mathbf{A}_{kr}\}_{r=1}^{R})

(1)

where
\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbf{W}_{ik}\mathbf{A}_{kr}\}_{r=1}^{R}
is a
R
-dimensional vector representing the expected frequency of each word
r
in document
i
as a mixture of
K
different distributions (or topics), whose weights are denoted as
\mathbf{W}_{ik}
. The rows of
\mathbf{Y}
are here sampled independently of one another, with no explicit relationship between documents.

Since its introduction, topic modeling has proven to be a useful unsupervised learning technique for extracting latent structure from document corpora and allowing the subsequent clustering of documents according to their topic proportions
[
32
,
59
,
61
,
65
]
. Beyond the text analysis sphere, topic modeling has been successfully applied to the analysis of count data across multiple applications, including image processing
[
34
,
50
,
54
]
, social network analyses
[
11
,
15
]
, as well as genetics
[
29
,
42
,
60
,
35
]
, and microbiome studies
[
40
,
47
,
51
]
where topics are interpreted as networks of interacting genes or communities of co-abundant bacteria, respectively.

From a methods perspective, topic modeling approaches can broadly be classified as belonging to one of two classes: Bayesian approaches, mostly expanding on the work by Blei et al.
[
9
]
, and frequentist approaches. Amongst the latter, the probabilistic Latent Semantic Indexing (pLSI) model
[
22
]
has recently benefited from an increased amount of interest
[
28
,
31
]
in the statistics community, as it allows the development of fast, statistically sound methods for inferring both
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{W}
.

However, in many applications, the data often includes additional dimensions, such as temporal or spatial attributes. In these settings, the data is best represented as a tensor rather than a matrix to reflect the natural structure of the data.
Instances of this setting occur in a wide number of applications, including:

•

Example 1 (text data): analysis of scientific reviews:
Consider the analysis of a set of reviews written by
N^{(1)}
reviewers on
N^{(2)}
academic papers. In this setting, it might be desirable to simultaneously cluster reviewers according to their
reviewer types
(e.g. favorable or unfavorable to a given research topic, focused on theoretical considerations or the applicability of the method, etc.), and papers according to their topic proportions
[
64
]
.

•

Example 2 (marketing): “Market-Basket” analysis:
Consider a set of
N^{(1)}
customers (the “documents”), and their purchases of various items (the “words”) through time (e.g. months or seasons). Tensor topic modeling can here be used to analyze purchasing patterns over time, identifying latent purchasing behaviors that correlate product categories with customer segments and temporal/seasonal variations (see Section
4
).

•

Example 3 (biology): longitudinal microbiome studies:
topic modeling has been proven to be a valuable tool for the analysis of microbiome samples
[
12
,
39
,
40
]
. In this setting, microbime samples from a given patient can be interpreted as documents, with words corresponding to bacteria types, and topics corresponding to communities of co-abundant bacteria
[
47
]
. In many settings, it is not unusual to have samples from the same subject collected across time
[
18
,
51
,
46
]
, or across different sampling sites (e.g. vaginal, saliva samples and gut samples, or samples from different parts of the gastro-intestinal tract
[
49
]
). In this case, inferring clusters of patients, time points, as well as communities of bacteria, and understanding the interactions between these latent structures is important for gaining deeper biological insights. (see Section
4
).

In this paper, we thus propose the first adaptation of pLSI to
t
ensor
t
opic
m
odeling (
TTM
), enabling us to appropriately leverage tensors’ inherent structure to efficiently estimate latent factors across all dimensions of the input tensor matrix. We formalize the description of our method for the analysis of text documents as described in Example 1 due to its broader ease of interpretation, but, as we will show in Section
4
, our method is broadly applicable to the analysis of tensor count data at large. Let us therefore suppose that we observe
N^{(1)}\times N^{(2)}
documents written by
N^{(1)}
reviewers for
N^{(2)}
papers using a vocabulary of
R
words. For each
i\in[N^{(1)}]
and
j\in[N^{(2)}]
, let
M_{ij}
denote the length of document (or review)
(i,j)
. For all
i\in[N^{(1)}]
,
j\in[N^{(2)}]
and
r\in[R]
, we observe the empirical frequency of each word in each document, defined as:

\mathcal{Y}_{ijr}=\frac{\text{number of times word }r\text{ has appeared in reviewer $i$'s review of paper $j$}}{M_{ij}}.

By analogy with the original topic model framework (Equation (
1
)), we assume that each document
(i,j)
is sampled independently, so that the entries
\{M_{ij}\mathcal{Y}_{ij\star}\}_{i\in[N^{(1)}],j\in[N^{(2)}]}
are sampled independently as:

\displaystyle M_{ij}\mathcal{Y}_{ij\star}\sim\text{Multinomial}\left(M_{ij},\mathcal{D}_{ij\star}\right)

(2)

for some tensor
\mathcal{D}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(1)}\times N^{(2)}\times R}
representing the expected word frequencies in each document:
\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{Y})=\mathcal{D}
. Letting
\mathcal{Z}:=\mathcal{Y}-\mathcal{D}
, the observed word frequencies
\mathcal{Y}
can thus be written in a “signal plus noise” form:

\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{D}+\mathcal{Z}.

(3)

In the context of tensor topic modeling, each entry of
\mathcal{D}
is assumed to be non-negative and to sum up to 1 along the “vocabulary axis”– that is, for each document
(i,j)
,
\sum_{r=1}^{R}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}=1
.
As an analogue of the matrix pLSI model, tensor topic modeling (TTM) imposes a low-rank structure on
\mathcal{D}
. In this paper, we propose studying TTM under a nonnegative Tucker decomposition (NTD) framework
[
30
]
, which decomposes the tensor
\mathcal{D}
as the product of a smaller core tensor
\mathcal{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{(1)}\times K^{(2)}\times K^{(3)}}
and three factor matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(1)}\times K^{(1)}}
,
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(2)}\times K^{(2)}}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times K^{(3)}}
. In this setting, the order-3 tensor
\mathcal{D}
is said to admit a rank-
(K^{(1)},K^{(2)},K^{(3)})
Tucker decomposition if it can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{G}\cdot(\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)}),

(4)

where
\cdot
denote the tensor multiplication.
In element-wise form, this model is equivalent to assuming that each entry
\mathcal{D}_{ijr}
can be re-written as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{ijr}=\sum_{k^{(1)}=1}^{K^{(1)}}\sum_{k^{(2)}=1}^{K^{(2)}}\sum_{k^{(3)}=1}^{K^{(3)}}\mathcal{G}_{k^{(1)}k^{(2)}k^{(3)}}\mathbf{A}^{(1)}_{ik^{(1)}}\mathbf{A}^{(2)}_{jk^{(2)}}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{rk^{(3)}}

(5)

Entries of the latent factors and the core tensor of
\mathcal{D}
are also assumed to be nonnegative, and can be interpreted either as probabilities or mixed membership proportions.
In particular, under model (
5
), each reviewer
i
is assumed to belong to a mixture of reviewer types, as indicated by the row
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}_{i\star}
– thereby allowing a mixed membership assignment of reviewers to clusters. Similarly,
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}_{jk^{(2)}}
represents paper
j
’s soft membership assignment to
paper category

k^{(2)}
.
Finally, each column
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{\star k^{(3)}}
corresponds to a probability distribution on the word frequencies, thereby defining
topic

k^{(3)}
. We thus refer to the latent space
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
as the
reviewer types
, the latent space
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
as the
paper category
space, and
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
as the latent
topic
space for words, as is usually done in matrix topic models
[
9
]
.
By contrast, the core tensor
\mathcal{G}
captures the interactions between latent topics and clusters, so that
\mathcal{G}_{k^{(1)}k^{(2)}k^{(3)}}
can be understood as the probability of observing topic
k^{(3)}
in a review authored by an author of type
k^{(1)}
in a paper of type
k^{(2)}
. Therefore, (
5
) can be understood as enforcing the following law of total probabilities for any word
r
in document
(i,j)
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\text{word }r|\text{ reviewer }i,\text{ paper }j\right)=\sum_{k^{(1)},k^{(2)},k^{(3)}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\text{topic }k^{(3)}|\text{ reviewer type }k^{(1)},\text{ paper category }k^{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{P}\left(\text{reviewer type }k^{(1)}|\text{ reviewer }i\right)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{P}\left(\text{paper category }k^{(2)}|\text{ paper }j\right)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{P}\left(\text{word }r|\text{ topic }k^{(3)}\right)

These components satisfy:

\begin{split}\sum_{k^{(1)}=1}^{K^{(1)}}\mathbf{A}^{(1)}_{ik^{(1)}}=1,\sum_{k^{(2)}=1}^{K^{(2)}}\mathbf{A}^{(2)}_{jk^{(2)}}&=1,\sum_{r=1}^{R}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{rk^{(3)}}=1\\
\sum_{k^{(3)}=1}^{K^{(3)}}\mathcal{G}_{k^{(1)}k^{(2)}k^{(3)}}&=1,\text{ and }\sum_{r=1}^{R}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}=1.\end{split}

(6)

In real applications, the size of the core
\mathcal{G}
is less than any of the original dimensions of the tensor:
\max\left(K^{(1)},K^{(2)},K^{(3)}\right)\ll\min\left(N^{(1)},N^{(2)},R\right)
. For technical reasons, we assume throughout this paper that
K^{(a)}
for
a\in[3]
is fixed while
N^{(1)},N^{(2)},R
and the document length
M_{ij}
vary.
Since the nonnegative Tucker decomposition introduced in (
4
) may not be unique, we introduce the following additional separability conditions to ensure identifiability.

Definition 1.1

(Anchor document assumption)
.

We call document
i^{*}
an anchor document for a given cluster
k\in[K]
if document
i^{*}
belongs solely to cluster
k
(i.e.,

\mathbb{P}[\text{cluster }k|\text{document }i^{*}]=1
).

Adapting this definition to the context of tensor topic modeling, we will assume in the rest of this paper that there exists for each reviewer cluster
k^{(1)}
an “anchor reviewer”
i^{*}
such that:

\mathbf{A}^{(1)}_{i^{*},k^{(1)}}=1,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\forall k^{\prime}\neq k^{(1)},\quad\mathbf{A}^{(1)}_{i^{*},k^{\prime}}=0.

Similarly, we assume that for each paper category
k^{(2)}
, there exists an “anchor paper”
j^{*}
such that:

\mathbf{A}^{(2)}_{j^{*},k^{(2)}}=1,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\forall k^{\prime}\neq k^{(2)},\quad\mathbf{A}^{(2)}_{j^{*},k^{\prime}}=0.

Definition 1.2

(Anchor word assumption)
.

We call word
r^{*}\in[R]
an anchor word for topic
k^{(3)}\in[K^{(3)}]
if the expected frequency of word
j
in topic
k^{(3)}
is nonzero, but its expected frequency is zero in all other topics.

In this paper, we assume that every topic
k^{(3)}
has at least one anchor word:

\forall k^{(3)}\in[K^{(3)}],\exists r^{*}:\quad\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{r^{*},k^{(3)}}>0,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\forall k^{\prime}\neq k^{(3)},\quad\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{r^{*},k^{\prime}}=0.

(7)

Remark 1.1
.

These conditions are sufficient, but not necessary for recovering the latent structure in the tensor-pLSI setting considered in this paper. In fact, we provide in Appendix
F
a discussion on extending the method to settings where the anchor word assumption is not satisfied. However, we note that these assumptions also enhance the interpretability of the model. In essence, these conditions imply the existence of pure words as well as archetypal reviewers and papers. Unlike centroids, archetypes highlight extremes rather than averages and are often used in machine learning and statistics to provide greater interpretability
[
16
,
38
]
. This is particularly relevant in our context, since, usually, to understand or represent topics or clusters in mixed membership settings, it is rather natural to turn to their “purest”/ ”most extremes” representants rather than to average members of that group.

1.1
Our contributions

In this paper, we propose a novel estimation procedure to recover the core tensor
\mathcal{G}
, along with the three factor matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
in (
4
). We further characterize the statistical efficiency of our method by providing theoretical guarantees under the anchor word, anchor reviewer and anchor paper assumptions.

More specifically, inspired by the use of Singular Value Decomposition as the cornerstone step in the estimation of matrix topic models
[
28
]
, we propose to use higher-order SVD (HOSVD)
[
58
]
for tensor topic modeling. Since
\mathcal{D}
is assumed to be low-rank, HOSVD is indeed a powerful and robust tool for dimensionality reduction that can (a) be rigorously analyzed under the anchor assumptions (Assumptions
1.2
and
1.1
); (b) is more interpretable than alternative tensor decompositions (e.g. the CP decomposition) by allowing to model the interactions between all latent components; and (c) has the potential to be computationally fast and efficient. We summarize our main contributions below:

•

We propose a new HOSVD-based method for estimating the four tensor topic components of (
4
): the core tensor
\mathcal{G}
and three factor matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
(Section
2
).

•

We characterize the statistical efficiency of our procedure by deriving an error bound in terms of the entry-wise
\ell_{1}
loss in Theorem
2.4
. Our method relies on the anchor word and anchor document assumptions (Assumptions
1.2
and
1.1
). Under mild assumptions on the smallest frequency of the word, our error bound is shown to be valid for all parameter regimes for which
K^{(a)}
for
a\in[3]
are considered fixed.

•

Our proposed procedure has further been shown to be adaptive to the column-wise
\ell_{q}
-sparsity assumption (Assumption
2.4
) in Section
2
. This sparsity assumption, motivated by Zipf’s law
[
66
]
, aligns with the natural distribution of word frequencies in topics, where only a few words appear frequently, and most others are rare
[
52
]
.

•

We highlight the performance of our method on an extensive set of synthetic (Section
3
) and real-world experiments (Section
4
), highlighting the increased accuracy and interpretability of our proposed approach.

We note that theoretical guarantees for the parameters in matrix topic modeling have already been established in the literature
[
28
,
31
]
. Consistent estimates of the three-factor matrices in TTM (Tensor Topic Modeling) can be obtained using similar proof techniques by flattening the tensor into matrices across three different dimensions. However, flattening the tensor is not sufficient to provide a consistent and efficient estimate for the core tensor. To the best of our knowledge, our estimating procedure is the first to leverage the advantages of HOSVD and offer a consistent estimate of the core tensor in Tucker-based TTM.

1.2
Notations

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
[k]
denote the index set
\{1,\dots,k\}
. We use
\mathbf{1}_{d}
to denote the vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with all entries equal to 1. For a general vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
\|\mathbf{v}\|_{r}
denote the vector
\ell_{r}
norm, for
r=0,1,\dots,\infty
, and let
\text{diag}(\mathbf{v})
denote the
d\times d
diagonal matrix with diagonal entries equal to entries of
\mathbf{v}
. For any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, let
a\vee b:=\max(a,b)
and
a\wedge b:=\min(a,b)
.

Let
\mathbf{I}_{m}
denote the
m\times m
identity matrix. For a general matrix
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
and
r=0,1,\dots,\infty
, let
\|\mathbf{Q}\|_{r}
denote the vector
\ell_{r}
-norm of
\mathbf{Q}
if one treats
\mathbf{Q}
as a vector. Let
\|\mathbf{Q}\|_{F}
and
\|\mathbf{Q}\|_{\text{op}}
denote the Frobenius (i.e.
\|\mathbf{Q}\|_{F}=\|\mathbf{Q}\|_{2}
) and operator norms of
\mathbf{Q}
respectively. For any index
j\in[m]
and
i\in[n]
, let
\mathbf{Q}_{ji}
,
\mathbf{Q}_{j,i}
or
\mathbf{Q}(j,i)
denote the
(j,i)
-entry of
\mathbf{Q}
. Also, let
\mathbf{Q}_{j\star}
and
\mathbf{Q}_{\star i}
denote the
j^{\text{th}}
row and
i^{th}
column of
\mathbf{Q}
respectively. For an integer
k\leq m\wedge n
, let
\sigma_{k}(\mathbf{Q})
denote the
k^{\text{th}}
largest singular value of
\mathbf{Q}
, and if
\mathbf{Q}
is a square matrix then, if applicable, let
\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{Q})
denote the
k^{\text{th}}
largest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{Q}
.
We write the
Kronecker product
between two matrices
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
and
\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}\times m_{2}}
as:
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}m_{1}\times n_{2}m_{2}}
with
(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})_{ik,jl}=\mathbf{A}_{ij}\mathbf{B}_{kl}
. For any matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
, the vectorized
\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}n_{2}}
-vector
\text{vec}(\mathbf{A})
is obtained by stacking the columns of
\mathbf{A}
. Conversely, a vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}n_{2}}
can be written in matrix form
\mathbf{U}=\operatorname{mat}(\mathbf{u})=\operatorname{vec}^{-1}(\mathbf{u})\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
.

We define the unfolding operations of tensors along each dimensions as follows. The mode-1 matricization of a tensor
\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}\times n_{3}}
matrix
\mathbf{X}^{(1)}
with entries:
\mathbf{X}^{(1)}(i,j_{k})
for
j_{k}=n_{3}(j-1)+k
. We also write
(jk)=j_{k}
. The mode-2 and mode-3 matricization (defined as
\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathcal{X})
and
\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathcal{X})
, respectively)
can be defined in a similar manner as the matrices
\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}\times n_{1}\times n_{3}}
matrix
\mathbf{X}^{(2)}
and
\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{3}\times n_{1}\times n_{2}}
matrix
\mathbf{X}^{(3)}
such that:

\mathbf{X}^{(2)}_{j,(ik)}=\mathcal{X}_{ijk}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{X}^{(3)}_{k,(ij)}=\mathcal{X}_{ijk}.

Throughout this paper, we denote matrices with simple bold capital letters (e.g.
\textbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
), vectors with bold lower-case letters (e.g.
\textbf{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
), and 3-way tensors using italics (e.g.
\mathcal{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}\times n_{3}}
). Let
C,c>0
denote absolute constants that may depend on
K^{(a)}
; we assume that
K^{(a)}
for
a\in[3]
are fixed, unobserved constants. Let
C^{*},c^{*}>0
denote numerical constants that do not depend on the unobserved quantities like
K^{(a)}
for
a\in[3]
. The constants
C,c,C^{*},c^{*}
may change from line to line. For the simplicity of notation, we write
K^{(1,2,3)}=K^{(1)}K^{(2)}K^{(3)}
, and
N^{(1,2)}=N^{(1)}N^{(2)}
.

2
Tensor Topic Modeling

Recall that
\mathcal{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(1)}\times N^{(2)}\times R}
denotes the observed corpus tensor and by assumption, under model (
2
),
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Y}]=\mathcal{D}
. In our paper, for ease of presentation, we assume that the document lengths are all equal:
M_{11}=\dots=M_{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}=M
. Our results also hold if we assume the document lengths satisfy
\max_{i,j\in[N^{(1)}]\times[N^{(2)}]}M_{ij}\leq C^{*}\min_{i,j\in[N^{(1)}]\times[N^{(2)}]}M_{ij}
, in which case
M=\frac{1}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N^{(1)},N^{(2)}}M_{ij}
denotes the average document length. Before introducing our method, we begin with some preliminary definitions and properties of tensor matricization, an operation that is central to our proposed method.

Mode-
a
matricization of
\mathcal{D}
: As discussed in Section
1.1
, estimating the three factor matrices can be achieved by flattening (or
matricizing
) the input tensor
\mathcal{Y}
across each of the different modes. Specifically, for
a\in[3]
, we define the mode-
a
matricization of a tensor
\mathcal{D}
with rank
(K^{(1)},K^{(2)},K^{(3)})

Tucker-decomposition
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{G}\cdot(\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)})
as:

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{(1)}:

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{D})=\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{G})(\mathbf{A}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(3)})^{\top}=\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\mathbf{W}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(1)}\times(N^{(2)}R)}

(D.1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{(2)}:

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathcal{D})=\mathbf{A}^{(2)}\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathcal{G})(\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(3)})^{\top}=\mathbf{A}^{(2)}\mathbf{W}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(2)}\times(N^{(1)}R)}

(D.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{(3)}:

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathcal{D})=\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathcal{G})(\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(2)})^{\top}=\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\mathbf{W}^{(3)}\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times(N^{(1)}N^{(2)})}

(D.3)

where
\mathbf{W}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{(1)}\times(N^{(2)}R)}
,
\mathbf{W}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{(2)}\times(N^{(1)}R)}
and
\mathbf{W}^{(3)}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{(3)}\times(N^{(1)}N^{(2)})}
. By direct application of Bayes’ theorem, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}^{(1)}_{k^{(1)}(jr)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k^{(2)}=1}^{K^{(2)}}\sum_{k^{(3)}=1}^{K^{(3)}}\mathcal{G}_{k^{(1)}k^{(2)}k^{(3)}}\mathbf{A}^{(2)}_{jk^{(2)}}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{rk^{(3)}}=\mathbb{P}\left(\text{word }r|\text{ reviewer type }k^{(1)},\text{ paper }j\right)

(W.1)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}^{(2)}_{k^{(2)}(ir)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k^{(1)}=1}^{K^{(1)}}\sum_{k^{(3)}=1}^{K^{(3)}}\mathcal{G}_{k^{(1)}k^{(2)}k^{(3)}}\mathbf{A}^{(1)}_{ik^{(1)}}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{rk^{(3)}}=\mathbb{P}\left(\text{word }r|\text{ paper category }k^{(2)},\text{ reviewer }i\right)

(W.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}^{(3)}_{k^{(3)}(ij)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k^{(1)}=1}^{K^{(1)}}\sum_{k^{(2)}=1}^{K^{(2)}}\mathcal{G}_{k^{(1)}k^{(2)}k^{(3)}}\mathbf{A}^{(1)}_{ik^{(1)}}\mathbf{A}^{(2)}_{jk^{(2)}}=\mathbb{P}\left(\text{topic }k^{(3)}|\text{ reviewer }i,\text{ paper }j\right)

(W.3)

Since each of the entries of the matrix
\mathbf{W}^{(a)},a\in[3]
can be interpreted as probabilities, the following constraints must hold:

\sum_{r=1}^{R}\mathbf{W}^{(1)}_{k^{(1)}(jr)}=1,\quad\sum_{r=1}^{R}\mathbf{W}^{(2)}_{k^{(2)}(ir)}=1,\text{ and }\sum_{k^{(3)}=1}^{K^{(3)}}\mathbf{W}^{(3)}_{k^{(3)}(ij)}=1

(8)

Using matricization on the tensor
\mathcal{D}
, the estimation of the factor matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}
is similar to the traditional pLSI setting, which we detail in Section
2.1
. However, estimating the core tensor
\mathcal{G}
is more difficult, as is establishing its consistency. To see this, note that a matricization along any axis
a
of the tensor
\mathcal{D}
would yield, in vector form:

\text{vec}\left(\mathbf{D}^{(a)}\right)=\left(\mathbf{A}^{(\backslash a)\top}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(a)\top}\right)\text{vec}\left(\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathcal{G})\right)

where
\mathbf{A}^{(\backslash a)}:=\mathbf{A}^{(b)}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(c)}
for
b,c\neq a
and
b<c
. However, unlike for the factor matrices, we cannot rely on any anchor assumption to reliably estimate
\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathcal{G})
. A solution would be to formulate the recovery of
\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathcal{G})
as a regression problem, using the estimated factor matrices in lieu of
\mathbf{A}^{(\backslash a)}
. However, this would yield an error bound in the estimation of
\mathcal{G}
that increases with the error between
\mathbf{A}:=\left(\mathbf{A}^{(\backslash a)\top}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(a)\top}\right)
and its estimate
\hat{A}:=\left(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(\backslash a)\top}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(a)\top}\right)
. Indeed, for any (compatible) matrices
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
,

\|\hat{A}\otimes\hat{B}-A\otimes B\|_{1}\leq\|\hat{A}-A\|_{1}\|\hat{B}\|_{1}+\|\hat{B}-B\|_{1}\|A\|_{1},

and in our case,
\|\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\|_{1}=N^{(1)},\|\mathbf{A}^{(2)}\|_{1}=N^{(2)},\|\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\|_{1}=K^{(3)}
. Thus, this discrepancy scales with
N_{R}=\max\left(N^{(1)},N^{(2)},R\right)
. Additionally, this method is computationally slow due to the vectorization of the tensor data. To address these challenges, we propose an alternative algorithm in Section
2.3
that constructs an estimate of
\mathcal{G}
and establishes its consistency.

2.1
Oracle procedure for the estimation of the factor matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)}

We first consider the oracle case in which the underlying frequency matrix
\mathcal{D}
is observed, and derive an oracle procedure to estimate the factor matrices. In the next subsection, we will show how to modify the oracle procedure to deal with stochastic noise (the
\mathcal{Z}
in Equation (
3
)), which gives our final method for estimating factor matrices.

As discussed in the Introduction, in this paper, we propose a procedure to estimate the factor matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
, and
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
based on the traditional pLSI framework. We begin by introducing a few preliminary concepts that are central to our estimation procedure.

Definition 2.1

(Ideal Simplex)
.

Let
\{\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
denote a set of
K
vertices in
\mathbb{R}^{p}.
Let
\{\mathbf{y}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be a set of
n
data points in the convex hull of the vertices
\{\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\}_{k=1}^{K}
:

\forall i\in[n],\qquad\mathbf{y}_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{ik}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k},\qquad\text{ with weights $\omega_{ik}\geqslant 0$ for all $k\in[K]$ and $\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{ik}=1$}.

The set
\{\mathbf{y}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is known as an “ideal simplex” if for each
k\in[K]
, there exists an index
i\in[p]
such that
\omega_{ik}=1
and
\omega_{ik^{\prime}}=0
for all
k^{\prime}\neq k
.

For points lying on an ideal simplex
\{\mathbf{y}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
, the
K
vertices
\{\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
can be identified using a “vertex hunting” algorithm. This algorithm returns data points corresponding to the vertices of the simplex
\{\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}\}_{k\in[K]}
.

Definition 2.2

(Vertex hunting)
.

Given
K
and a point cloud
\{\mathbf{y}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
belonging to the ideal vertex defined above, a vertex hunting algorithm
\mathcal{V}(\cdot)
is a function that returns a subset
K
of points
\{\boldsymbol{\nu}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\nu}_{K}\}\subset\{\mathbf{y}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
corresponding to the vertices.

2.1.1
Estimating
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}

Under the anchor-reviewer and anchor-document assumptions, the matricizations
\mathbf{D}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
form an ideal simplex (Definition
2.1
), and the matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
satisfy the condition of
\{\omega_{ik}\}_{i\in[n],k\in[K]}
defined in its definition
2.1
, since:

\mathbf{D}^{(1)}=\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\mathbf{W}^{(1)}\quad\text{with }\mathbf{A}^{(1)}_{ik}=\mathbb{P}[\text{reviewer type }k|\text{ reviewer }i],\quad\sum_{k=1}^{K^{(1)}}\mathbf{A}^{(1)}_{ik}=1,

\mathbf{D}^{(2)}=\mathbf{A}^{(2)}\mathbf{W}^{(2)}\quad\text{with }\mathbf{A}^{(2)}_{jk}=\mathbb{P}[\text{paper category }k|\text{ document }j],\quad\sum_{k=1}^{K^{(2)}}\mathbf{A}^{(2)}_{jk}=1.

To obtain estimates for
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
, a simple procedure consists in first, recovering vertices from the point clouds
\mathbf{D}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
respectively, yielding two sets of vertices
\mathbf{H}^{(a)},a\in\{1,2\}
. We note that there exists several methods for inferring vertices from point clouds. Options include for instance the successive projection algorithm (SPA) (see
[
31
]
).
The second step of the algorithm is to express each of the observations as a convex combination of the vertices:
\mathbf{D}^{(a)}=\mathbf{A}^{(a)}\mathbf{H}^{(a)}
for
a\in\{1,2\}
, and obtain estimates of
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}
by simply right multiplying
\mathbf{D}^{(a)}
by the inverse of
\mathbf{H}^{(a)}
. However, as shown in Appendix
E
, the output matrix
\mathbf{H}^{(a)}
of vertices from the point cloud
\{\mathbf{D}^{(a)}_{i\star}\}_{i\in[N^{(a)}]}
is not invertible.
We would thus need to estimate
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}
by regressing
\mathbf{D}^{(a)}
unto
\mathbf{H}^{(a)}
, which would make the error bounds for
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}
scale linearly with
N^{(a)}
for
a\in\{1,2\}
.

We propose instead building upon the matrix topic modeling given by
[
31
,
28
,
52
]
, which provides a more efficient way of solving for
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}
. We start by considering the HOSVD of tensor
\mathcal{D}
. Under the Tucker decomposition framework, each matricization of
\mathcal{D}
along axis
a
admits a singular value decomposition (SVD) of the form:

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{(a)}=\mathbf{\mathbf{\Xi}}^{(a)}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{(a)}\mathbf{B}^{(a)\top}

A key observation is that, if
\lambda_{K^{(a)}}\left(\mathbf{D}^{(a)}\right)>0
, then the matrix
\mathbf{\mathbf{\Xi}}^{(a)}
can be represented as

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}=\mathbf{A}^{(a)}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}

(9)

where
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
is unique and non-singular (proof in Appendix
E
). If the matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
satisfies the anchor-reviewer (and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
the anchor-paper) assumption, the rows of the matrix
\mathbf{\mathbf{\Xi}}^{(a)},a\in\{1,2\}
form an ideal simplex in
\mathbb{R}^{K^{(a)}}
with vertices given by the row of the matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
. The matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}
for
a\in\{1,2\}
are the corresponding weights, and, since
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
is invertible,
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}
can be estimated as:
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}=\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)})^{-1}
.

2.1.2
Estimating
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}

For
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
, unlike for the first two dimensions, the anchor-word assumption does not naturally imply that the matricization
\mathbf{D}^{(3)}
is an ideal simplex. Inspired by
[
28
]
, our procedure uses the SCORE normalization, which was originally introduced to deal with degree heterogeneity in network analysis in
[
26
]
, to reduce the word point cloud
\mathbf{D}^{(3)}
to an ideal simplex.

Definition 2.3
.

[SCORE normalization
s(\mathbf{\Xi})
] Given a
n\times K
matrix
\mathbf{\Xi}
where the first column is strictly positive, the SCORE normalization
s(\mathbf{\Xi}):\mathbb{R}^{n\times K}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n\times K-1}
is the procedure that normalizes each row of the matrix
\mathbf{\Xi}
by its first coordinate. The resulting matrix is defined as
\mathbf{S}:=s(\mathbf{\Xi})=[\mathbf{S}_{1\star},\dots,\mathbf{S}_{n\star}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(K-1)}
such that for all
i\in[n]
:

\mathbf{S}_{ik}=\mathbf{\Xi}_{i(k+1)}/\mathbf{\Xi}_{i1}\text{ for }k=1,\dots,K-1.

Under the anchor-word assumption, we can indeed show that the rows of
\mathbf{S}
form an ideal simplex (Lemma
F.2
in the Appendix, similar to
[
28
]
). As for the first two dimensions, we can thus employ a vertex-hunting procedure to recover the vertices of the simplex. Expressing each row of
s(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)})
as a convex combination of these vertices (whose weights are denoted by the matrix
\mathbf{\Omega}^{(3)}
), the matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
can then be recovered as:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Omega}^{(3)}:=\left[\text{diag}(\mathbf{\Xi}_{\star 1}^{(3)})\right]^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}[\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})]

(10)

The detailed proof is provided by Lemma
F.2
in the Appendix.

2.1.3
Estimating the Latent Factors

We summarize the estimation for all three latent components as follows.

Oracle Procedure:

Let
\mathbf{D}:=\mathbf{D}^{(a)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
denote a matrix associated with the factor index
a\in[3]
, where the dimensions
n,p
may vary depending on the choice of
a
.

1.

(SVD) Compute the singular value decomposition (SVD) of
\mathbf{D}
to obtain the first
K
left singular vectors. Denote the resulting matrix as
\mathbf{\Xi}=[\Xi_{\star 1},\dots,\Xi_{\star K}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times K}
.

2.

(Post-SVD preprocessing) Define the matrix
S
as:

\mathbf{S}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{\Xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\tilde{K}}\text{ for }\tilde{K}:=K&\text{ if }a\in\{1,2\}\\
s(\mathbf{\Xi})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\tilde{K}}\text{ for }\tilde{K}:=K-1&\text{ if }a=3\end{cases}

where
s
is defined in Definition
2.3
.

3.

(VH algorithm) Apply a vertex hunting algorithm on the rows of
\mathbf{S}
to obtain a vertex set
\{\mathbf{V}_{1\star},\dots,\mathbf{V}_{K\star}\}\in\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{K}}
. Define
\mathbf{V}=[\mathbf{V}_{1\star},\dots,\mathbf{V}_{K\star}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times\tilde{K}}

4.

(Recovery) Recover the weight matrix
\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times K}
by solving the linear equation
\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}\mathbf{V}^{*}=\mathbf{S}^{*}
where

\mathbf{V}^{*}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{V}\\
[\mathbf{1}_{K},\mathbf{V}_{\star 1},\dots\mathbf{V}_{\star\tilde{K}}]\end{cases}\mathbf{S}^{*}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{S}&\text{ if }a\in\{1,2\}\\
[\mathbf{1}_{n},\mathbf{S}_{\star 1},\dots\mathbf{S}_{\star\tilde{K}}]&\text{ if }a=3\end{cases}

5.

(Output) Compute
\mathbf{A}
as

\mathbf{A}^{(a)}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}&\text{ if }a\in\{1,2\}\\
\text{ColNorm}(\text{diag}(\mathbf{\Xi}_{\star 1})\mathbf{\Omega}^{*})&\text{ if }a=3\end{cases}

where ColNorm(
\mathbf{A}
) for matrix
\mathbf{A}
is to normalize each column of matrix
\mathbf{A}
to have unit
l_{1}
norm

Remark 2.1
.

Our oracle procedure differs from that of
[
28
]
and
[
31
]
in the first step of the singular value decomposition (SVD). Indeed, we perform an SVD on
\mathbf{D}
instead of
\mathbf{L}^{1/2}\mathbf{D}
where
\mathbf{L}
is defined differently in
[
28
]
and
[
31
]
to adapt to different noise levels. We prefer not preconditioning the SVD as in
[
28
]
as this preconditioning yields a point cloud that can be more easily stretched and destabilized by low-frequency words. As shown in
[
52
]
, the robustness of the SVD preconditioning requires indeed that the frequencies of the words in
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
have the same order. To increase the signal-to-noise ratio in the procedure, an alternative is to truncate or threshold words altogether as in
[
52
]
, which is easy to adapt in our oracle procedure (see Section
2.4
).

Additionally, considering
\mathbf{D}
instead of
\mathbf{L}^{1/2}\mathbf{D}
simplifies some parts of our theoretical analysis. In particular, the estimation of the core tensor via HOSVD based on
\mathbf{D}
(as opposed to
\mathbf{L}^{1/2}\mathbf{D}
with different
\mathbf{L}
s) is easier to analyze (See Lemma
E.4
).

2.2
Estimation procedure in the presence of noise

We now extend the oracle procedure described in the previous subsection to real-world settings, in which we observe a noisy version of
\mathbf{D}^{(a)}
,
\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}:=\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathcal{Y})
.

In this case, the procedure for recovering the latent factors is essentially the same as in the oracle case, replacing the first step by an eigendecomposition of the matrix
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
to obtain a matrix
\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times K}
corresponding to its leading eigenvectors
K
, where
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
is defined as

\hat{\mathbf{Q}}:=\begin{cases}\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{Y}^{\top}&\text{ if }a\in\{1,2\}\\
\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{Y}^{\top}-\frac{1}{M}\text{diag}(\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{1}_{p})&\text{ if }a=3\end{cases}

(11)

Furthermore, we choose to use the Successive Projection (SP)
[
4
]
as our vertex hunting algorithm in all the experiments, but other choices like Sketched Vertex Search (SVS)
[
27
]
and Archetypal Analysis (AA)
[
25
]
can also be applied. The difference between those vertex hunting algorithms has been studied in
[
28
,
52
]
.

Remark 2.2
.

When
a=3
, adjusting the matrix
\mathbf{Y}^{(3)}\mathbf{Y}^{(3)\top}
by subtracting
\frac{1}{M}\text{diag}(\mathbf{Y}^{(3)}\mathbf{1}_{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}})
helps center the variance of the multinomial distribution. This adjustment leads to a more accurate SVD, thereby improving the match between the true and estimated singular vectors.

To ensure that the SCORE normalization is well-conditioned during the estimation procedure, the leading eigenvectors obtained should be strictly positive. According to Perron’s theorem, the signs of the leading singular vectors are necessarily positive in the oracle procedure. However, in the noisy case,
\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{\star 1}
may contain negative entries. Therefore, any word
r\in[R]
for which
\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r1}
is negative should have the corresponding rows in
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
clipped to zero after Step 2. Subsequent steps are performed only with
\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r1}>0
for
r\in[R]
. In our theoretical analysis, this situation is unlikely to occur with high probability (see Lemma
E.5
in the Appendix).

2.3
Estimating the core tensor
\mathcal{G}

For a tensor
\mathcal{D}
with rank
r(\mathcal{D})=\left(K^{(1)},K^{(2)},K^{(3)}\right)
,
its higher-order singular value decomposition (HO-SVD) is defined as the decomposition

\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{S}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}\right).

The left singular vectors
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}
and
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}
can be obtained from the SVDs of the matricizations
\mathbf{D}^{(1)},\mathbf{D}^{(2)}
and
\mathbf{D}^{(3)}
respectively.
The
K^{(1)}\times K^{(2)}\times K^{(3)}
HO-SVD core tensor
\mathcal{S}
can thus be obtained through the following equation:

\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{D}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)\top},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)\top},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)\top}\right)

due to the orthogonality of left singular vectors.

However, in topic modeling, such a simple relationship between the core tensor
\mathcal{G}
and the three mode matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}
for
a\in[3]
does not exist. Mode matrices in topic modeling are indeed neither non-singular nor orthogonal. While
\mathcal{G}
can be estimated using a constrained least square regression, as highlighted in the introduction, this naive approach does not yield good estimates.

However, as detailed in Equation (
9
), the singular vectors
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}
of the HO-SVD of
\mathcal{D}
can be decomposed as
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}=\mathbf{A}^{(a)}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
. Thus, if
\lambda_{K^{(a)}}(\mathbf{D}^{(a)})>0
, then the matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
is invertible and
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}=\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}\left(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}\right)^{-1}
.

This allows us to rewrite the tensor topic model of (
4
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\cdot\left(\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)})^{-1},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)})^{-1},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)})^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{\mathcal{G}\cdot\left((\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)})^{-1},(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)})^{-1},(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)})^{-1}\right)\right\}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}\right)

Right multiplying the two sides of the previous equation by the singular vectors, we see that:

\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{S}\cdot\left(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)},\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)},\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\right).

For
a\in\{1,2\}
, the matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
corresponds simply to the vertices extracted by the vertex hunting procedure:
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}=\mathbf{V}^{(a)}=\mathcal{V}(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)})
. For
a=3
, Lemma
F.2
shows that:

\left[\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})\right]^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}=\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}=[\mathbf{1}_{K^{(3)}},\mathbf{V}^{(3)}]

(12)

We now introduce an explicit procedure to recover
\mathcal{G}
from
\mathcal{S}
and the three mode matrices
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
for
a\in[3]
. This procedure relies on the following lemma.

Lemma 2.1
.

Suppose
\sigma_{K^{(3)}}(\mathbf{A}^{(3)})\geqslant c^{*}
for some constant
c^{*}>0
. Then, there exists a positive vector
\mathbf{q}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{(3)}}
such that
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}=\text{diag}(\mathbf{q}_{0})\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}
and

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\text{diag}(\mathbf{q}_{0})=\text{diag}(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{\star 1})\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(3)}

(13)

where
\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}
is the set of vertices extracted by the vertex hunting procedure.

Lemma
2.1
establishes the connection between equations (
10
) and (
12
), and allows the estimation of
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}
from the estimation procedure for
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
. Since each column of
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
has unit
\ell_{1}-
norm, the right hand size of (
13
) means that
the vector
\mathbf{q}_{0}
can be estimated as the
\ell_{1}
-norm of each column of
\text{diag}(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{\star 1})\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(3)}
. In the oracle procedure, the vector
\mathbf{q}_{0}
corresponds to the vector
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\star 1}^{(3)}
. The entries of
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\star 1}^{(3)}
are all positive if
\lambda_{K^{(a)}}(\mathbf{D}^{(a)})>0
(see the proof of (
68
) in Section
F
). In the noisy setting, our theoretical analysis guarantees that the entries of
\mathbf{q}_{0}
are positive with high probability.

We summarize the full estimation procedure for
\mathcal{G}
in the noisy setting:

[Estimation procedure for
\mathcal{G}
]
Given an input tensor
\mathcal{Y}

1.

(HOSVD) Obtain estimates of the singular matrices
\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(a)}
for all
a\in\{1,2,3\}
, from the spectral decomposition in (
11
).

2.

(HOSVD Core recovery) Estimate the core tensor
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
as
\mathcal{\hat{S}}:=\mathcal{Y}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(1)\top},\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(2)\top},\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(3)\top}\right)

3.

(Mode Recovery) Estimate
\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
from the matrices of vertices of
\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}
such that

\displaystyle\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}=\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(1)},\quad\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)}=\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(2)}\quad\text{and}\quad\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}=\text{diag}(\mathbf{\hat{q}}_{0})\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(3)}

where
\mathbf{\hat{q}}_{0}=\left(\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star 1}\|_{1},\dots\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star K^{(3)}}\|_{1}\right)^{\top}
with
\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}=\text{diag}(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(3)}

4.

(Core Tensor Recovery) Set the core tensor to be:
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}=\mathcal{\hat{\mathbf{S}}}\cdot\left(\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)},\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)},\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\right)
.
Set
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
to be a clipped version of
\mathcal{\tilde{G}}
, so that any negative entries in
\mathcal{\tilde{G}}
are set to zero and then all rows are appropriately re-normalized so that
\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{k^{(1)}k^{(2)}\star}
sums up to 1.

Remark 2.3

(Comparison with HOOI)
.

Suppose the underlying noise tensor
\mathcal{Z}
consists of
i.i.d.
subGaussian entries with mean zero and variance
\sigma
,

[
43
]
and
[
63
]
showed that the method of high-order orthogonal iteration (HOOI)
[
17
]
yields optimal estimates of the singular vectors for the Tucker decomposition when the signal-to-noise ratio is
\gtrsim p^{3/4}
, where
p
is the maximum tensor dimension. In contrast, HOSVD tends to yield sub-optimal results under such conditions. Our method can be easily adapted to work with HOOI by incorporating power iterations into steps 1 and 2 using the data
\mathcal{Y}
with no centering step (described in (
11
)). This adaptation would in principle allow us to leverage the advantages of HOOI and deploy them to our subGaussian setting.

However, under model (
2
), the stochastic noise
\mathcal{Z}
follows a multinomial distribution, and entries are not independent across dimensions
r\in[R]
. We can show that in this scenario, HOOI achieves performance bounds comparable to those of HOSVD (see Lemma
E.4
).

Remark 2.4

(Comparison with truncated HOSVD)
.

Truncated HOSVD and STAT-SVD
[
62
]
are methods designed to identify low-rank structures in tensor data while assuming a certain sparsity structure. Specifically, these methods often rely on mode-wise hard sparsity, requiring at least one row of the mode matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}
to have all entries equal to zero. However, this assumption is not suitable for topic modeling: if a word does not appear in any of the documents, it is absent from the vocabulary and does not affect the model.

As discussed in
[
52
]
, assuming element-wise sparsity or row-wise sparsity in the mode matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
is not appropriate for dealing with the large vocabulary size
R
. Instead, a weak column-wise
\ell_{q}
sparsity assumption (Assumption
2.4
) would be more appropriate. This assumption is based on the empirical observation that word frequency in text data often decreases inversely proportionally with word rank. Similar assumptions are used in other statistical contexts, such as sparse PCA
[
36
]
and sparse covariance estimation
[
10
]
.
Our method is easily adapted to accommodate this weak
\ell_{q}
sparsity. This approach is detailed in Lemma
2.2
and Section
2.4
, showing how our method can effectively handle the sparsity structure relevant to tensor topic modeling.

2.4
Theoretical Results

Given a matrix parameter
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times K}
, we measure the performance of its estimator
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
through the element-wise
\ell_{1}
error (subject to a permutation matrix
\mathbf{\Pi}
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left(\hat{\mathbf{A}},\mathbf{A}\right)\equiv\sum_{k=1}^{K}\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{\star\pi(k)}-\mathbf{A}_{\star k}\|_{1}

(14)

The core tensor is also permuted consistently with the factor matrices. We therefore measure

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{\hat{G}},\mathcal{G}\right)\equiv\sum_{k^{(1)}=1}^{K^{(1)}}\sum_{k^{(2)}=1}^{K^{(2)}}\sum_{k^{(3)}=1}^{K^{(3)}}|\mathcal{\hat{G}}_{\pi^{(1)}(k^{(1)})\pi^{(2)}(k^{(2)})\pi^{(3)}(k^{(3)})}-\mathcal{G}_{k^{(1)}k^{(2)}k^{(3)}}|

(15)

where
\mathbf{\Pi}^{(1)},\mathbf{\Pi}^{(2)},\mathbf{\Pi}^{(3)}
are permutation matrices for the factor matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
respectively.

We begin by stating the assumptions underlying our analysis.

Assumption 2.1
.

For some constant
c^{*}\in(0,1)

\sigma_{K^{(1)}}(\frac{1}{N^{(1)}}\mathbf{A}^{(1)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(1)})\geqslant c^{*},\sigma_{K^{(2)}}(\frac{1}{N^{(2)}}\mathbf{A}^{(2)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(2)})\geqslant c^{*},\text{ and }\sigma_{K^{(3)}}(\mathbf{A}^{(3)})\geqslant c^{*}\sqrt{K^{(3)}}

\sigma_{K^{(a)}}(\frac{1}{K^{(1)}K^{(2)}}\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathcal{G})\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathcal{G})^{\top})\geqslant c^{*}\text{ for all }a\in[3]

Assumption 2.2

(Anchor conditions)
.

For the factor matrices defined in the introduction section, we assume that:

•

\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
has at least one anchor entry for each class
k
. That is, for each class
k
, there exist an index
j
such that
\mathbf{A}_{jk}=1
and
\mathbf{A}_{jk^{\prime}}=0
for all other class
k^{\prime}.

•

\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
has at least one anchor word for each topic
k
. That is, for each topic
k
, there exists a word
r
such that
\mathbf{A}_{rk}>0
but
\mathbf{A}_{rk^{\prime}}=0
for all other topic
k^{\prime}.

Assumption 2.3
.

For some constant
c^{*}>0
, we assume that:

\min_{k,k^{\prime}\in[K^{(3)}]}\left(\mathbf{A}^{(3)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\right)_{kk^{\prime}}\geqslant c^{*}

Assumption
2.1
assumes the factor matrices and the core tensor are well-conditioned. This assumption is commonly assumed in the literature on matrix topic modeling
[
28
,
31
,
52
]
. Since the entries of the four components of Tucker decomposition in topic modeling are all probabilities, we always have
\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{(3)})\leqslant\sqrt{K^{(3)}}
,
\sigma_{1}(\frac{1}{N^{(a)}}\mathbf{A}^{(a)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(a)})\leqslant 1
for
a\in\{1,2\}
, and
\sigma_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathcal{G})^{\top}\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathcal{G}))\leqslant K^{(1)}K^{(2)}
for any
a\in[3]
. Combined with Assumption
2.1
, this implies that the condition numbers of the Tucker components are all bounded by some constant.

Assumption
2.2
requires the lower-dimensional classes or topics from the factor matrices to each have at least one anchor entity. For comparison, identifiability in matrix topic modeling is guaranteed when assuming an anchor condition on only one dimension (e.g. anchor document or anchor word, but not necessarily both). In the tensor case, matricization across a mode does not guarantee identifiability on another, and therefore we have to assume an anchor condition for each target factor matrix. This is thus a much stronger assumption than for matrix topic modeling. However, this assumption is only used to guarantee that there exists a vertex hunting algorithm that recovers the vertices such that
\max_{k\in[K]}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}\|_{2}\leqslant c^{*}\max_{i\in[n]}\|\mathbf{\hat{y}}_{i}-\mathbf{y}_{i}\|_{2}
(up to a label permutation). We refer the reader to the discussion of
[
52
]
for weaker assumptions in Section 2.4, under which our main theorem
2.4
may also hold.

Assumption
2.3
pertains the correlation between topics, effectively assuming that topics are reasonably correlated. We do not perceive this assumption to be restrictive, since any topic in
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
will assign significant weights to common, less informative words. This condition implies a large eigengap, meaning the
K^{(3)}
first largest eigenvalues along the third mode are well-separated from the smaller eigenvalues (due to noise). This makes the model well-conditioned and facilitates the subsequent analysis.

We now state our main theorem.

Theorem 2.4
.

Suppose that the assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
and
2.3
hold. Define
N_{R}=\max\{N^{(1)},N^{(2)},R\}
and
f_{r}=\sum_{k\in[K^{(3)}]}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{rk}
. For some constants
c,c^{*},C>0
, we further assume that the number of words per document
M
is such that
M\geqslant C\log(N_{R})
, and that the word frequencies
f_{r}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{jk}
are such that:

\min_{r\in[R]}f_{r}\geqslant c\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}
.

Then, with probability
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\right)\leq c^{*}N^{(1)}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}}

(16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)}\right)\leq c^{*}N^{(2)}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}}

(17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\right)\leq c^{*}\left(\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}

(18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{\hat{G}},\mathcal{G}\right)\leq c^{*}\max\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}},\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}\right\}

(19)

The proof of this theorem is provided in Appendix
A
. When document lengths
M
is sufficiently large such that

M\gg N^{(1)}N^{(2)}
, the error in estimating
\mathcal{G}
is thus primarily driven by the SVD error associated with the mode matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
. Conversely, if

M\ll N^{(1)}N^{(2)}
,
the estimation error is dominated by the errors in the mode matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
.

We also compare our results in Theorem
2.4
with the existing literature. For the matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
, the rates in Equations (
16
) and (
17
) are consistent with the minimax rates provided in
[
31
]
, up to a logarithmic factor. For the topic-word matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
, our rate only depends on the logarithm of the vocabulary size

R
,
and the rate in (
18
) aligns with the rate derived in
[
52
]
for high-frequency words.

However, our analysis is based on the condition that the lowest frequency of the entire vocabulary is bounded below by
\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}
.
If this condition is not realistic, an alternative is to assume weak
\ell_{q}
sparsity on
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
as in Assumption
2.4
, as discussed in
[
52
]
.

Assumption 2.4

(Column-wise
\ell_{q}
sparsity)
.

Given
k\in[K^{(3)}]
, let the entries of each column
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{\star k}
be ordered as
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{[1]k}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{[R]k}
. For some
q\in(0,1)
and
s_{0}>0
, the columns of
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
satisfy

\max_{k\in[K^{(3)}]}\left\{\max_{r\in[R]}r\left(\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{[r]k}\right)^{q}\right\}\leqslant s_{0}

This assumption imposes a condition on the decay rate of the ordered entries in the columns of
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
. It allows most entries of
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
to be small but nonzero, making it feasible to estimate these low-frequency entries as zero if they fall below a certain threshold. We set this threshold at
\tau=c^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}
for some constant
c^{\prime}>0
(
c^{\prime}=0.005
as suggested in
[
52
]
). Consequently, the estimation procedure previously outlined in Section
2.3
operates only on a subset of the data

\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{J}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(1)}\times N^{(2)}\times|\mathcal{J}|}

where the set of words
\mathcal{J}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}:=\left\{r\in[R]:\frac{1}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}],j\in[N^{(2)}]}\mathcal{Y}_{ijr}\geqslant c^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}\right\}

(20)

For the subset of word frequencies in the matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}\star}
, where
\mathcal{J}^{c}
denotes the set of indices with frequencies below the threshold, we set these entries to zero.

Lemma 2.2

(Sparse tensor topic modeling)
.

Suppose the Assumption
2.1
,
2.2
,
2.3
and
2.4
are satisfied. If
M\geq c\log N_{R}
, and
s_{0}\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{\frac{1-q}{2}}=o(1)
, we then can show that, with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
, the error in the third factor is such that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)})\leq c^{*}\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}+s_{0}\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{\frac{1-q}{2}}

(21)

The first part of the error term in (
21
) accounts for the errors associated with the words within the set
\mathcal{J}
where
\min_{r\in\mathcal{J}}f_{r}\gtrsim\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}
. In the strong sparsity regime where
s_{0}=O(1)
and
0<q<1/2
, this component of the error dominates. The second part of the error term, on the right-hand side of the rate, comes from setting the entries corresponding to the words outside the set
\mathcal{J}
to zero. This simplification is based on the assumption that these entries are below a certain threshold and can be safely approximated as zero. However, in some cases, especially when the number of such entries is large and
1/2<q<1
, this component can dominate the overall error.

Since the estimation methods (e.g., HOSVD, core recovery in Section
2.3
) utilize the subset

\mathcal{J}
with a specified minimum frequency condition, the error rates for
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
,
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
and the core tensor
\mathcal{G}
remain unchanged.

3
Experiments on synthetic data

To validate the performance of our method, we present here the results of extensive experiments on synthetic datasets
1
1

1

The code for our method and all the experiments presented in this section can be found on Github at the following link:
https://github.com/yatingliu2548/tensor-topic-modeling
where the ground truth is known. This allows us in particular to understand the method’s performance as a function of the data dimensions and properties.

Data Generation Procedure.

We adapt the synthetic data generation procedure of
[
28
]
and
[
52
]
to the tensor setting. More specifically, we generate soft cluster assignments along the first and second modes by sampling
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
rowwise from a Dirichlet distribution with parameter
\mathbf{1}_{K^{(a)}},a\in\{1,2\}
. We generate a core matrix
\mathcal{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{(1)}\times K^{(2)}\times K^{(3)}}
by sampling each slice
(k^{(1)},k^{(2)}
from a Dirichlet distribution with parameter
\mathbf{1}_{K^{(3)}}
. Finally, we create the matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
(whose rows indicate the frequencies of the words in each topic) by sampling its entries from a uniform distribution and normalizing the columns appropriately. The data
\mathcal{Y}
is then sampled from a multinomial distribution with parameters
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{G}\cdot(\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)})
as (
2
).

Benchmarks

Throughout this section, we compare the performance of our method against five different benchmarks:

•

Tensor-LDA:
we implement a fully Bayesian counterpart to our method (the extension of LDA for tensor data), where all latent factors and core
\mathcal{G}
are endowed with Dirichlet priors. The posteriors for all latent
\mathbf{A}^{(a)},a\in[3]
and
\mathcal{G}
are fitted using Variational Bayes in Rstan
[
20
]
. We provide further details on the model and implementation of this method in Appendix
K
.

•

Hybrid-LDA:
We also compare our method with a simple refinement of the basic LDA results to our setting: we begin by fitting an LDA model to the matricization of the data along the third mode, thereby allowing us to extract the latent topic matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
and a topic assignment matrix
\mathbf{W}^{(3)}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{(3)}\times N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}
. We subsequently apply a vertex hunting algorithm on the resulting topic assignment matrix
\mathbf{W}^{(3)}
to extract the remaining two factors,
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
, and estimate the core using constrained linear regression. The full estimation procedure is detailed in Appendix
K
.

•

Structural Topic Model (STM)
[
44
]
:
Since the previous method (Hybrid LDA) is oblivious to existing correlations in topics amongst documents, we replace the Latent Dirichlet Allocation step in the previous bullet point by a Structural Topic Model (STM)
[
44
]
. The main difference between the two models lies in the explicit encoding of correlations between documents in STM. To this end, it replaces the Dirichlet prior on the mixture matrix
\mathbf{W}^{(3)}
by a lognormal distribution:

\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\text{lognormal}(\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta},\mathbf{\Sigma})

where
\mathbf{X}
is a matrix of covariates and
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is a regression coefficient. In our case, we take
\mathbf{X}
to be a
N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\times(N^{(1)}+N^{(2)})
matrix such that:

\forall i\in[N^{(1)}],j\in[N^{(2)}],\quad\mathbf{X}_{(ij),l}=\begin{cases}1\quad\text{ if }l=N^{(1)}\\
0\quad\text{ if }l<N^{(1)}\text{ and }l\neq N^{(2)}\\
1\quad\text{ if }l=N^{(1)}+N^{(2)}\\
0\quad\text{ if }l>N^{(1)}\text{ and }l\neq N^{(2)}+N^{(1)}\\
\end{cases}

•

Non-Negative Tucker Decomposition (NTD):
To compare our method against another simple heuristic, we also apply a simple non-negative Tucker decomposition on our data, and normalize the estimated latents and core appropriately so that their rows (or columns when appropriate) lie on the simplex.

•

Topic-Score TTM:
Finally, we further compare our method against a variant using the topic score method of
[
28
]
to compute the topic matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
, but using the same method as we propose to estimate the rest of the latent factors and core. This allows us in particular to assess the efficiency of our proposed normalization (Step 1 of the HOSVD, Equation (
11
))

3.1
Visualizing Topic Interactions

We begin by illustrating the performance of our method on a toy example consisting of a
30\times 10\times 50
tensor
\mathcal{D}
, shown in Figure
21
in Appndix
L
. We take the number of words per document to be identical across documents and set it to
M=100
. Modes 1 and 2 index the documents, while mode 3 indexes the words. We let
\mathcal{Y}
denote the observed tensor, and
\mathcal{D}
to denote the ground-truth one, which is built to exhibit the following structure:

•

First Mode (30 indices, 2 clusters)
: The first 10 indices belong to a first cluster, the last 10 to another, and the middle 10 users have split membership between clusters 1 and 2.

•

Second Mode (10 indices, 2 clusters)
: indices along the second mode are split into two separate clusters, with indices 1 through 5 belonging to cluster 1 and 6 through 10 belonging to cluster 2.

•

Third Mode (50 words, 3 topics)
: the third mode represents the topics.

The results are shown in Figures
1
-
4
, with the associated reconstruction errors
\|\mathcal{D}-\mathcal{\hat{D}}\|_{1}
detailed in the figure captions, where
\mathcal{\hat{D}}=\hat{\mathcal{G}}\cdot(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)},\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(2)},\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)})
represents the reconstructed data tensor using the estimated components. We note that our method yields the smallest reconstruction
\ell_{1}
error.

All algorithms, (ours, NTD, tensor LDA, and hybrid-LDA) successfully identify the two groups along the first mode, as well as the mixed membership of the middle 10 indices.
The results in the second mode are more mixed. Hybrid LDA, shown in Figure
4
, fails to correctly recognize clusters along the second mode. By contrast, Tucker-decomposition-based methods (ours in Figure
1
, Non-negative Tucker Decomposition (NTD) in Figure
2
and Tensor-LDA in Figure
3
) successfully recover the mode-2 clusters. However, TTM-HOSVD and NTD feature stronger membership assignments compared to Tensor-LDA. Moreover, the core tensors derived from our method (TTM-HOSVD) in Figure
1
and Tensor-LDA in Figure
3
reveal clear interactions between clusters along all modes. In particular, these methods show the first cluster of indices along the first mode switches from topic 1 to topic 2 for as the documents to which they correspond switch from cluster 1 to cluster 2 along mode 2.
In contrast, neither NTD nor hybrid-LDA shows such clear interaction patterns in the core.

Figure 1
:
Tucker components
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)},\mathcal{G}
(Left to Right respectively) estimated by our method with ranks
(2,2,3)
for the tensor from Figure
21
. Note that the factor
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
shows a clear clustering pattern. The estimated factor
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
also exhibits two clusters. Factor
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
shows the topics. The core tensor
\mathcal{G}
highlights interactions between the clusters of multiple modes. Each slice in the last subfigure represents the clusters derived from
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
. The reconstruction error is
\|\mathcal{\hat{D}}-\mathcal{D}\|_{1}=22.687

Figure 2
:
Tucker components
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)},\mathcal{G}
(Left to Right respectively) estimated by Nonnegative Tucker decomposition (NTD) with ranks
(2,2,3)
. The reconstruction error is
\|\mathcal{\hat{D}}-\mathcal{D}\|_{1}=91.543
.

Figure 3
:
Tucker components
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)},\mathcal{G}
(Left to Right respectively) estimated by Tensor LDA with ranks
(2,2,3)
. The reconstruction error is
\|\mathcal{\hat{D}}-\mathcal{D}\|_{1}=27.262
.

Figure 4
:
Tucker components
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)},\mathcal{G}
(Left to Right respectively) estimated by Hybrid-LDA with ranks
(2,2,3)
. The reconstruction error is
\|\mathcal{\hat{D}}-\mathcal{D}\|_{1}=82.703
.

Our first experiment highlights the importance of Tucker decomposition in topic modeling for multidimensional data. In particular, this approach allows us to understand interactions among latent factors (e.g. interactions between clusters of indices and topics).
This approach offers a more comprehensive understanding of the latent structure within the data.

Figure
5
further demonstrates that our HOSVD-based method is deterministic, ensuring consistent results across multiple runs. In contrast, tensor LDA, hybrid LDA, and NTD are sensitive to initialization and prone to getting stuck in local optima, often requiring multiple runs with different initializations to find a global solution. More specifically, hybrid LDA exhibits the largest variance in reconstruction error, as the estimation of the matrix

\mathbf{W}^{(3)}
is critical for determining
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
,
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
. As shown in Appendix
L.1
, if the estimation of
\mathbf{W}^{(3)}
is sufficiently accurate, the rest of the procedure to estimate
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
can effectively capture the data patterns. However, since
\mathbf{W}^{(3)}
is nondeterministic in LDA, Hybrid-LDA often exhibits higher variance. The instability of tensor LDA is also evident, with significant variation in the reconstruction error and outlier points in Figure
5
. Finally, while NTD is more stable compared to Bayesian methods, it exhibits the largest average reconstruction error.

Figure 5
:
Boxplot of the reconstruction errors over 30 runs on the same dataset from Figure
21
. The middle line is the median value over 30 runs. The red point refers to the mean value.

We provide the results for STM in Appendix
L.1
, since it is a variation on the Hybrid LDA approach. Overall, STM offers a much less variable alternative to LDA, but still yields a higher reconstruction rate than our method.

3.2
Varying the data dimensions

We now vary the data dimensions and assess the performance of our method and benchmarks in estimating the different components of the model. Figure
6
shows the estimators’ respective errors as a function of the first dimension
N^{(1)}
(with
N^{(2)}
being equal to
N^{(1)}
) for various dictionary sizes, and Figure
7
shows the error as a function of the dictionary size
R
for various dimensions
(N^{(1)},N^{(2)}).
Overall, we observe that our method consistently performs better than all other benchmarks, especially in estimating the latent factors
\mathbf{A}^{(a)},a\in\{1,2,3\}.
The naive NTD approach does not succeed in correctly estimating the latent factors. The performance of the tensor LDA method that we suggest in this paper seem to drop as the size of the dictionary increases. Overall, our method seems to be the most robust to varying dictionary sizes.

N^{(1)}
, compared to various benchmarks.

is here set to 10,000 and

while

.

, compared to various benchmarks.

is here set to 10,000 and

while

.

M
in Figure
8
, Our method and STM seem to have steeper slopes, indicating a more efficient use of the information than in other methods.

4
Real data experiments

In this section, we apply our method to real-world datasets, specifically focusing on text, microbiome as well as marketing data as outlined in the Introduction (Section
1
).

4.1
ArXiv’s paper Analysis

For our first experiment, we consider a corpus of 5,094 research abstracts belonging to (at least) one of four categories: Computer Science, Mathematics, Physics, and Statistics from 2007 to 2024.
2
2

2

The data is available on Kaggle at
https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/Cornell-University/arxiv
after some pre-processing of the data (See detail in Appendix
M.1
). The structure of the obtained tensor is of dimensions (283, 18, 8837) where the first dimension represents sampled articles, the second dimension corresponds to the publication years, and the third dimension represents the vocabulary size. In this study, we aimed to analyzed scholarly articles from the arXiv repository to investigate trends in theoretical and applied research over 18 years. We run our Tensor Topic Modeling method of the dataset with ranks (2,3,4). (As described in
[
28
,
31
,
52
]
, the spectrum of
\mathbf{D}^{(a)}
is useful for estimating
K^{(a)}
. We note that it is often possible to determine the eigenvalue cutoff by inspecting the scree plot of
\mathbf{D}^{(a)}
’s eigenvalues. Figure
27
in the Appendix displays the scree plot for each mode with different values of
K^{(a)}
.)

Analysis and Discussion

We show the top 10 words for each of the four topics. The results are presented in Figure
9(b)
, and
10
. For the topics of Computer Science, Math and Physics, the top 10 most representative words produced by our method agree with 50%, 60%, and 70% of NTD’s most representative words in the corresponding topics, respectively. Upon closer inspection, we find that some of the words produced by our method (such as “quantum”) are more indicative of Physics. In contrast, the Bayesian methods show less agreement with the top 10 words derived from our method. Specifically, most of the top 10 words produced by Hybrid LDA are generic and could easily appear in other categories, reducing the specificity of the topics. With the inclusion of external information, STM improves upon LDA by extracting more exclusive words relevant to the topics, producing results more similar to those from our method. Meanwhile, Tensor-LDA includes many non-typical words such as “mesh”,“talk”, “pinpoint”, “institutions”, and “beginequation” which are less representative of the core topics. This highlights the strength of our method in capturing more relevant and representative words for each topic, especially compared to other decomposition techniques.

To investigate the trends in theoretical and applied research across subject topics from 2007 to 2024, we further examine the core tensor in Figure
9(a)
(with exact values provided in Table
M.3
in the Appendix). Within the theoretical group, we observe that the proportion of statistics research increases over the time slice, although mathematics still holds a significant proportion. However, the proportion of mathematics gradually decreases over time (from 76% to 69% as shown in Table
M.3
), suggesting a shift of focus towards statistics in recent years. Additionally, computer science is categorized purely to application, and its overall proportion continues to increase over the years. This shows the surge of computer science and ongoing technological advancements in recent years. Meanwhile, physics is categorized as purely theoretical and the proportion is stable over the time. Due to space limitations, we include the core values of the benchmarks in Appendix
M.1
.

(b)

\mathbf{A}^{(3)}

4.2
Sub-communities of the vaginal microbiota in non-pregnant women

In this subsection,
we revisit the analysis of women vaginal microbiota presented by Symul et al.
[
51
]
.
This dataset consists of vaginal samples collected daily from 30 nonpregnant women enrolled at the University of Alabama, Birmingham. Each sample is represented here as a vector of counts of rRNA amplicon sequence variants (ASVs), agglomerated by taxonomic assignment (see
[
51
]
).

As in
[
51
]
, we propose to analyze the data using topic models; In this context, each sample from individual
i
on day
t
can be viewed as a document, with ASV corresponding to words (
R=1,338
). We construct the data tensor using a subset of vaginal samples (since many participants do not have a sample for every single day of the cycle) and align the samples based on the patient’s menstrual cycle. Specifically, for each participant, we selected 11 samples corresponding to various stages of their menstrual cycle: 2 days from the menstrual phase, 3 days from the follicular phase, 2 days from the ovulation phase, and 4 days from the luteal phase. Keeping only participants for whom this construction does not produce missing entries, this results in a three-mode tensor of size
24\times 11\times 1,338
, as shown in the left plot of Figure
11
.

Figure 11
:
Visualization of the construction of the vaginal microbiome tensor (
24\times 11\times 1,338
). Left: Menstrual cycle information. Cycles (28 days in total) were then aligned from day
-20
(i.e. 20 days before menses) to day
+7
(i.e. 7 days after the first day of menses). Right: The tensor represents data collected from 24 participants, each with vaginal samples taken across 11 days of the menstrual cycle. The participants include 3 individuals of Hispanic
/
Latino origin, 4 who identify as White, and 17 who identify as African American. The participants’ ages range from 19 to 45.

The first mode of the resulting tensor indexes the participants, the second indexes the cycle stage, and the third mode represents the different ASVs. Additionally, we have demographic information for each participant, including race and age.

TTM-HOSVD provides more accurate representation of vaginal microbiotas.

To evaluate the topics estimated by our method, we propose using two techniques:
(a) the refinement and coherence
of the topic hierarchy computed by the package
alto

[
19
]
as a proxy for topic stability. Developed as a diagnostic and visualization tool to select an appropriate number of topics for LDA,
alto
aligns topics as
K
grows based on the document assignments
\mathbf{W}
in order to visualize the induced topic hierarchy, as well as the progressive splitting or recombination of topics as
K
increases. In this setting, topics with better refinement (few ancestor per topic) and coherence (persistence of the topic as
K
grows) are considered more stable; and
(b) the median topic resolution
[
52
]
, computed by splitting the dataset in half, fitting the model on each half, and evaluating the agreement between the recovered topics using a cosine similarity .

Figure
12
shows the refinement and coherence of the topics recovered by our method as
K^{(3)}
increases, and compares them with those obtained by the hybrid LDA, STM, NTD and Tensor LDA approaches described in the previous section. Our method seems to provide topics with higher refinement (fewer ancestors per topic) and higher coherence. For instance, figure
12(b)
shows that hybrid LDA produces topics that have a tendency of recombining (e.g. topic 7 merges with 4 at
K^{(3)}=6
). The other figures (
12(c)
and
12(d)
) show even worse rates of topic recombination as
K^{(3)}
increases.

Figure
13(a)
shows that our method also exhibits significantly better results in terms of median topic resolution than both LDA and NTD for up to
K^{(3)}=10
topics – at which point NTD begins to outperform all other methods. NTD relies on the minimization of the KL divergence between the observed data and the low-rank approximation — a procedure which could perhaps allow it to better capture fine-grained patterns in the data, particularly when dealing with a larger number of topics. However, this comes at a significant computational cost compared to our method.

The results for STM (Structural Topic Model)
[
44
]
are presented in the Appendix (Section
M.2
). However, this method performs the worst in both the topic refinement and topic resolution plots, which could indicate that the method places too much emphasis on enhancing relationships derived from the meta information.

TTM-HOSVD reveals associations between topic composition and menstrual phases.

We here propose to test whether the recovered latent factors from
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
,
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
and
\mathbf{W}^{(3)}
are associated with relevant covariates, such as demographic characteristics and menstrual phases.
Following the procedure in
[
51
]
, we employed Dirichlet regression to analyze the estimated document-topic matrix
\hat{\mathbf{W}}^{(3)}=\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathcal{\hat{G}})\left(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}\times\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(2)}\right)^{\top}
. Our HOSVD-based approach (Figure
14(c)
) reveals that participants’ race is significantly associated with topic proportions, a result consistent with that of the LDA-based approach of the original paper (shown in Figure 3(c) in
[
51
]
). However, compared to matrix LDA, our method identifies more significant points, demonstrating a more pronounced relationship between vaginal microbiota composition, demographic characteristics, and menstrual phases.
(For comparison, we also reproduced the LDA-based results, which are presented in Appendix Figure
30
.)

To further explore the ability of the data to project participants and cycle days into lower-dimensional representations that reflect demographic characteristics and menstrual phases, we analyzed the heatmaps of
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
. The heatmap of
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
estimated by our method in Figure
15(b)
shows that samples from the same menstrual phase exhibit similar loadings, successfully recovering the underlying menstrual phase structure. Additionally, the heatmap of
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
in Figure
15(a)
indicates that the groups are not solely associated with a single demographic feature. This finding aligns with the Dirichlet regression results, which reveal that the lower-dimensional representation of subjects is significantly associated with both age and racial information.

Figures
14(a)
and
14(b)
further validate the effectiveness of Tucker decomposition in this dataset. Significant associations in
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
are concentrated around their respective covariates, demonstrating that topic composition is conditionally independent given the lower-dimensional factors representing time (menstrual phases) and demographic features. These findings underscore the utility of Tucker decomposition in uncovering topics in multi-dimensional data, providing stronger and more interpretable signals compared to traditional matrix LDA-based approaches.

TTM-HOSVD highlights dynamic changes in microbiome composition:

Figure
16(a)
displays the heatmap of the core tensor derived from TTM-HOSVD, revealing key patterns in microbiome topic activity throughout the menstrual cycle. Notably, Topic 4, which is dominated by Lactobacillus crispatus (as shown in Figure
13(b)

16(b)

The figure also highlights the reduced presence of microbiome Topic 2 in Group 3, as observed in UAB022. Additionally, Microbiome Topic 1 shows a noticeable decline during menstrual phases 3 and 4, which correspond to the luteal and ovulation phases. Figure
16(b)
further reveals that the proportion of Lactobacillus gasseri (the dominant bacterium in microbiome Topic 1) peaks during the menstrual phase for Subject UAB022.

These findings demonstrate that our Tucker decomposition-based method effectively captures meaningful associations between microbiome topic activity.
