4.3
Market-Basket Analysis

Finally, we propose deploying our method to the analysis of market data. More specifically, we consider the analysis of the Dunnhumby dataset
3
3

3

The dataset is publicly available at the following url:
https://www.kaggle.com/datasets/frtgnn/dunnhumby-the-complete-journey
.
, a public dataset tracking the purchases of 2,500 households who frequently shop over a two-year period in a retail store. After a light preprocessing of the data (see details in Appendix
M.3
), we retain a total of 176 households, whose purchases are observed at 26 time points (corresponding to sets of two weeks) in the year. Multiple purchases in a given period of two weeks are aggregated, yielding baskets (our “documents”) consisting of an average 10 items. The dictionary here consists of a list of 2,102 possible retail items. We run our Tensor Topic Modeling method of the dataset to extract
K^{(3)}=10
retail “topics” (i.e. sets of items that are frequently bought together), as well as attempt classifying shoppers in
K^{(1)}=4
categories and time points in
K^{(2)}=4
clusters. The values for
K^{(1)}
and
K^{(3)}
were chosen using the scree plot of the corresponding matricized data, and the value of
K^{(2)}
was chosen to match the 4 seasons.

Analysis of the results.

The market-basket topics recovered by our TTM-HOSVD method are presented in Figure
17
, where we show the top 30 words for each of the 10 topics. Due to space constraints, we present the results for STM, Hybrid-LDA, as well as TopicScore-HOSVD in Appendix
M.3
(Figures
34
,
35
and
36
). We were unfortunately unable to run the Bayesian tensor LDA approach on a problem of this size, as this approach is unfortunately unable to scale to larger dictionaries and core sizes. We summarize the frequency of each category among the top 30 most common items in Table
M.6
of the Appendix.

Overall, our method is able to detect interesting patterns. Topic 4, for instance, is highly enriched in the “baby product” category (with items from this category making up as much as 40% of the topic (Table
M.6
)), as it is enriched in items from the baby product category, as well as items from other categories targeted to children (e.g. baby juices or chocolate milk). Topic 9, on the other hand, is enriched in pet products (dog and cat food), with pet products representing 24% of the topic. Topic 8 is the most enriched in snacks ( 28% of the topic), while Topic 10 is enriched in Eggs & Dairy (20%), Topic 3 and 7 in “fruits and vegetables” (16.5% and 15.8% respectively), and Topic 6 in beverages (16%). By contrast, the hybrid LDA model does not recover any ”baby-product” or ”beverage”-related topic, and most of its topics are either enriched in Candy& Snacks, Meals,or Fruits and Vegetables (Figure
35
of the Appendix) — thereby shedding less light into customer-buying patterns. STM fares better than the Hybrid-LDA approach, recovering a clear pet-product topic, but seems to mix Beverages and Baby product items (Figure
34
).

Turning to the analysis of the latent factors along mode 2 (
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
), we observe that our proposed method returns cluster assignments that seem to be smooth in time (Figure
18(b)
). Topics 3 and 4, in particular, are complementary (first and second half of the year, respectively), while topic 1 seems to be at the midway point, and topic 2 serves as the transition between topic 3 and 4. LDA and STM, however, return patterns in the time dimensions, with Topic 4 spanning most of the year, and topic 2 and 3 spanning the extremities (Figure
18(b)
).

5
Related Literature

5.1
Relationship to existing literature

Before further discussion, we briefly describe related work and emphasize the potential significance of tensor topic modeling approaches for handling modern datasets.

Topic Modeling.

Topic modelling approaches usually fall into on of two categories: Bayesian approaches, which typically involve variations around the Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA) model of
[
9
]
; and its frequentist counterpart, probabilistic latent semantic indexing (pLSI). While the data generation mechanism of (
1
) is the same across methods, the two types of approaches differ mainly in the assumptions that they make on the data and in the tools that they use for inference. Beyond these two main categories, additional methods such as Latent Semantic Analysis and standard Non-negative Matrix Factorization have also been employed to solve the same problem. However, since their outputs are non longer probabilities and do not benefit from the same interpretability, we will not discuss them here.

Latent Dirichlet Allocation assumes that the topic assignment and topic matrices,
\mathbf{W}
and
\mathbf{A}
respectively, are random variables, which need to be endowed with appropriate priors (such as for instance, Dirichlet distributions
[
9
]
). In this setting, the goal of the inference is to adjust the posterior distribution of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{W}
given the observed data. This is typically achieved through a version of the variational Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm, which is known to be fast and scalable. Further refinements of LDA have subsequently been proposed to accommodate additional side information, such as accounting for correlation between documents by the same author, or longitudinal information in the analysis of microbiome data. For instance,
in the matrix setting,

[
44
]
extend the usual LDA setting to structural LDA by incorporating document-level covariates
X_{i}
and propose modeling correlations between documents by replacing the Dirichlet prior on each row of the mixture matrix
\mathbf{W}
with a logistic normal sampling step:
\mathbf{W}_{i\star}=\text{softmax}(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i})
, where
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{i}\sim N(\mathbf{X}_{i}\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
.
However, fewer works have considered the tensor setting. Amongst the latter, most seem to have primarily focused on the supervised setting, where documents are paired with numerical ratings. For example, in the context of reviewer-item rating dyads (a similar context to our reviewer example), FLDA
[
1
]
and FLAME
[
57
]
learn to explain document ratings using a latent variable model, where scores are explained by a combination of latent factors on the items, reviewers and topics. To our knowledge, the only adaptation of LDA to the tensor setting is found in the works by
[
21
]
and
[
3
]
. In
[
21
]
, the authors consider document corpora with a time dimension. The authors propose a method that automatically categorizes similar topics into groups over time using a two-step process, which fits topics to documents using LDA — thereby yielding a topic tensor —, and subsequently refines the topics by grouping them using a canonical polyadic (CP) decomposition.
While this approach allows to understand correlations between topics through time, the two-step procedure does not allow the estimation of the topics to borrow strength across tie points.

[
3
]
, on the other hand, consider the application of LDA on tensor data, using the moments to recover the latent topics using a CP decomposition.
This model consequently does not allow to understand the interactions between topics and latent time variables, Furthermore, to the best of our knowledge, no Bayesian model for tensor topic modeling has yet been suggested in the literature.

Probabilistic Latent Semantic Indexing, on the other hand, can be viewed as a special instance of non-negative matrix factorization (NMF). Its consistency is generally based on a separability condition, also referred as “anchor word” or “anchor document” assumption
[
28
,
8
,
31
]
(definitions
1.1
and
1.2
). In other words, anchor words act as signatures for a topic, while anchor documents act as “archetypes”, in that they correspond to documents that have only a single topic. Either of the anchor assumptions is a sufficient condition for identifiability of parameters
[
13
]
in matrix pLSI. Existing methods
[
28
,
5
,
7
,
52
,
31
]
estimate the parameters of the model by first, identifying the anchors and subsequently using them to recover the remaining parameters, usually by expressing the remaining rows as convex combinations of the anchors.

A major drawback of both types of approaches is that they are inherently designed to analyze data based on two-dimensional interactions between documents and topics. This can oversimplify complex datasets where documents also have temporal or spatial dimensions, or demographic characteristics leading to latent clusters. In other words, simply flattening all dimensions and neglecting the correlations between documents fails to accommodate their unique properties and relationships. This approach can result in a loss of important information that could substantially aid in topic inference. Moreover, while traditional topic models focus solely in reducing the vocabulary space by describing documents as mixture of topics, the automatic clustering of documents and time points might also be of scientific interest. In particular, we might be interested in understanding interactions between all latent components. For example, in our guiding example, reviewer and paper information can be treated as additional information that can be useful to improve the topic and mixed-membership estimation. In microbiome studies, temporal and demographic characteristics of samples may impact microbioa abundances. Understanding the interaction between types of patients (e.g. pregnant vs non-pregnant women) and the different abundances of microbiota might be of scientific significance. In this setting, representing the data as a tensor holds richer promise.

The Nonnegative Matrix Factorization literature has a richer history in accommodating tensor data. Methods such as fixed NMF
[
14
]
and direct NMF
[
41
]
operate by slicing the tensor along one of the dimensions (for instance, time) and estimate the topic proportions over that particular dimension. However, it still fails to fully capture the nuanced information of that dimension since these methods assume a prior distribution of topic proportion over the third mode. For direct NMF, the data are treated as “independent” across the side information, while the data in fixed NMF are assumed to share the same latent topics over the third dimension. It fails to capture the topic changes over third mode.
Therefore, it is crucial to employ a tensor decomposition method that captures the complete information and detects changes in topics across different dimensions.

Tensor Decompositions.

There are two main tensor decompositions: the
CANDECOMP/ PARAPAC
(CP) decomposition and the
Tucker
decomposition. Due to the discrete and non-negative nature of count data, some studies have devised constrained version of these decompositions for the analysis of text data, namely non-negative CP
[
2
]
(NNCPD) or non-negative Tucker decomposition
[
45
]
(NTD) in tensor topic modeling.
CP can be viewed as a special case of Tucker decomposition, which require a super-diagonal core tensor. In tensor topic modeling, CP decomposition finds the analogy sharing by all dimensions, while tucker decomposition allows more complex interactions across modes. However, these tensor decomposition methods still face identifiability issue in tensor topic modeling. The uniqueness of NNCPD is based on Kruskal’s rank condition
[
6
]
. Unlike the separable condition alike anchor assumption, it is hard to interpret its meaning in pLSI model. MFLBM
[
64
]
was proposed to estimate the tucker-based topic modeling by variational EM algorithm but as the matrix LDA, it do not explore the anchor conditions. Therefore, these algorithms face the identifiability issue and do not guarantee to produce consistent estimates of core tensor and three factor matrices.

6
Conclusion

In this paper, we introduce
Tensor Topic modeling
(TTM), a HOSVD-based estimation procedure to incorporate spatial, temporal, and other document-specific information, providing a more nuanced analysis for datasets that inherently span multiple dimensions. Our procedure is also shown to adapt effectively under the
\ell_{q}
-sparsity assumption
2.4
on word-topic matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
, which is exhibited by many real-world text datasets. By thresholding low-frequency words, we reduce the inclusion of specific, potentially identifiable terms, which helps mitigate such privacy risks
[
37
]
. Our threshold HOSVD-based method can be potentially used as a pre-processing step in differentially private (DP) vocabulary selection to improve privacy without significantly impacting model performance.

Based on this paper, some potential research directions emerge. First, investigating core sparsity within tensor decompositions could offer insights, particularly in enhancing computational efficiency and interpretability. For instance, exploring the AL
\ell_{0}
CORE approach
[
23
]
, where the core tensor’s
\ell_{0}
-norm is constrained, allows only a limited number of non-zero entries (up to a user-defined budget
Q^{*}
). This sparse core structure is an adaptation of Tucker decomposition that maintains computational tractability similar to CP decomposition while benefiting from Tucker’s more flexible latent structure.

Also, the minimax-optimal
\ell_{1}
-error rate for the Tucker components with and without
\ell_{q}
-assumption
2.4
remains an open problem.

from the National Science Foundation (NSF grant IIS-2238616). This work was completed in part with resources provided by the University of Chicago’s Research Computing Center.

Appendix A
Appendix: Proofs

Appendix B
Properties of the data

B.1
Tensor Topic Modeling

In this paper, we generalize the usual matrix topic modeling framework assumed by Probabilistic Latent Semantic Indexing (
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{W}
) to accommodate the analysis of tensor count data assumed to have a latent Tucker factor decomposition (
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{G}\cdot(\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)})
). The crux of this approach consists in considering the matricized versions of
\mathcal{D}
(as defined in Equation (
D.1
) of the main text) along each axis:

1.

\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{D})=\mathbf{D}^{(1)}=\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\mathbf{W}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(1)}\times(N^{(2)}R)}
, which we assume to have rank
K^{(1)}
,

2.

\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathcal{D})=\mathbf{D}^{(2)}=\mathbf{A}^{(2)}\mathbf{W}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(2)}\times(N^{(1)}R)}
, which we assume to have rank
K^{(2)}
,

3.

\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathcal{D})=\mathbf{D}^{(3)}=\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\mathbf{W}^{(3)}\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times(N^{(1)}N^{(2)})}
, which we assume to have rank
K^{(3)}
.

Given the inherent structure of the problem, each one of the Tucker factor matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
belong to one of two matrix types. Letting
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times K}
denote a general matrix, the factor matrices can be either considered as instances of either:

Case 1: Row-Wise Stochastic Matrices:

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathbf{A}_{ik}=1\text{ for any }i\in[n]

(C1)

Case 2: Column-Wise Stochastic Matrices:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[n]}\mathbf{A}_{ik}=1\text{ for any }k\in[K]

(C2)

Remark B.1
.

As highlighted in the main text (Equations (
6
) and (
8
)), the matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
,
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{G})/K^{(2)}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}(\mathcal{G})/K^{(1)}
in our proposed tensor topic model are assumed to be row-wise stochastic (Case (
C1
)), while the matrices
\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathcal{G})
and
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
are column-stochastic (Case (
C2
)). This implies that, in the rest of the appendix, the estimation of
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
can be achieved symmetrically (using the same tools and methods), but the estimation of
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
must be tailored to account for its peculiar structure.

B.2
Properties of the one-hot word encoding vectors.

Throughout our theoretical analysis, it will often be easier to analyze the behavior of the observed empirical frequencies
\mathbf{Y}_{ir}
of each term
r
in the vocabulary in document
i
by writing them as averages of one-hot word encoding vectors.

Let
\{\mathbf{T}_{im}:i\in[n],m\in[M]\}
represent the set of independent
R
-dimensional vectors corresponding to the one-hot word encoding vectors for each of the
m
words in in document
i
. Under the pLSI model:

\mathbf{T}_{im}\in\mathbb{R}^{R}\sim\text{Multinomial}(1,d_{i})

(22)

for
\mathbf{d}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{R}
. We have
\sum_{r\in[R]}\mathbf{d}_{i}(r)=1
. For any fixed
r\in[R]
, we define
\mathbf{X}_{im}
to be the centered version of
\mathbf{T}_{im}
:

\mathbf{X}_{im}(r)=\mathbf{T}_{im}(r)-d_{i}(r).

(23)

where
\mathbf{d}_{i}(r)=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{T}_{im}(r)].

It is easy to show that the following properties on the moments of
\mathbf{X}_{im}
hold true:

•

First moment of
\mathbf{X}_{im}
:

|\mathbf{X}_{im}(r)|\leqslant 1,\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{im}(r)]=0\qquad

(24)

•

Second moment of
\mathbf{X}_{im}
and
\mathbf{T}_{im}
:

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{T}_{im}(r)^{2}]=\mathbf{d}_{i}(r),\qquad\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{T}_{im}(r)^{2}]=\mathsf{Var}\left[\mathbf{X}_{im}(r)\right]=\mathbf{d}_{i}(r)-\mathbf{d}_{i}(r)^{2}

(25)

and if
r\neq l
:

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{T}_{im}(r)\mathbf{T}_{im}(l)]=0,\quad\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{im}(r)\mathbf{X}_{im}(l)]=-\mathbf{d}_{i}(r)\mathbf{d}_{i}(l).

(26)

•

Fourth moment of
\mathbf{X}_{im}
and
\mathbf{T}_{im}
:

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{im}(r)^{4}]&\leqslant\mathbf{d}_{i}(r)\text{ and }\mathsf{Var}\left[\mathbf{X}_{im}(r)^{2}\right]\leqslant\mathbf{d}_{i}(r),\\
\text{And if }r\neq l,\hskip 85.35826pt&\\
\quad\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{im}(r)^{2}\mathbf{X}_{im}(l)^{2}]&\leqslant\mathbf{d}_{i}(r)\mathbf{d}_{i}(l),\\
\quad\mathsf{Cov}(\mathbf{X}_{im}(r)^{2},\mathbf{X}_{im}(l)^{2})&=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{im}(r)^{2}\mathbf{X}_{im}(l)^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{im}(r)^{2}]\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{im}(l)^{2}]\leqslant\mathbf{d}_{i}(r)\mathbf{d}_{i}(l)\end{split}

(27)

Remark B.2
.

Recall the model in (
2
). Define
\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)=\mathcal{T}_{ijm}(r)-\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{T}_{ijm}(r)]
where
M\mathcal{T}_{ijm}(\cdot)\in\mathbb{R}^{R}\sim\text{Multinomial}(M,\mathcal{D}_{ij\star})
for any
i\in[N^{(1)}],j\in[N^{(2)}]
and
r\in[R]
. Define:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\quad\quad\text{ for }i\in[N^{(1)}],j\in[N^{(2)}],m\in[M]

(28)

where
\mathcal{Z}:=\mathcal{Y}-\mathcal{D}
with
\mathcal{Y}
the observed tensor corpus, and
\mathcal{D}
its expectation. By the previous remarks (Equations (
25
) and
27
), noting that each document in the tensor
\mathcal{Y}
is indexed by a tuple
(i,j)
, we directly deduce that for each entry of
\mathcal{Z}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Z}_{i_{1}j_{1}r_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{i_{2}j_{2}r_{2}}]=\frac{1}{M}\begin{cases}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}-\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2}&\text{if }i_{1}=i_{2}=i,j_{1}=j_{2}=j,r_{1}=r_{2}=r\\
-\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{2}}&\text{if }(i_{1}=i_{2}=i,j_{1}=j_{2}=j)\text{ but }r_{1}\neq r_{2}\\
0&\text{as soon as }(i_{1}\neq i_{2})\text{ or }(j_{1}\neq j_{2})\\
\end{cases}

(29)

Properties on the concentration and norms of
\mathcal{Z}
are provided in Section
D

Lemma B.1
.

Define
f_{r}:=\sum_{k\in[K^{(3)}]}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{rk}
. For any
i\in[N^{(1)}],j\in[N^{(2)}]
and
r\in[R]
, we have

\mathcal{D}_{ijr}\leqslant f_{r}.

Proof.

Recall that, in our definition of
\mathcal{D}
, for each entry
(i,j,r)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{ijr}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k^{(1)}=1}^{K^{(1)}}\sum_{k^{(2)}=1}^{K^{(2)}}\sum_{k^{(3)}=1}^{K^{(3)}}\mathcal{G}_{k^{(1)}k^{(2)}k^{(3)}}\mathbf{A}^{(1)}_{ik^{(1)}}\mathbf{A}^{(2)}_{jk^{(2)}}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{rk^{(3)}}\qquad\text{ where }\mathcal{G}_{k^{(1)}k^{(2)}k^{(3)}}\leq 1

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k^{(1)}=1}^{K^{(1)}}\sum_{k^{(2)}=1}^{K^{(2)}}\sum_{k^{(3)}=1}^{K^{(3)}}\mathbf{A}^{(1)}_{ik^{(1)}}\mathbf{A}^{(2)}_{jk^{(2)}}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{rk^{(3)}}=\sum_{k^{(3)}=1}^{K^{(3)}}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{rk^{(3)}}=f_{r}

where the last line follows from the fact that
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
are column-wise stochastic (entrywise non-negative) matrices (Case
C1
).
∎

Lemma B.2

(Maximum singular values of the factor matrices)
.

The eigenvalues of the factor matrices defined in Model (
4
) verify the following inequalities:

\sigma_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N^{(1)}}\mathbf{A}^{(1)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\right)\leqslant 1,\qquad\sigma_{1}\left(\frac{1}{N^{(2)}}\mathbf{A}^{(2)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(2)}\right)\leqslant 1,\qquad\sigma_{1}\left(\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\right)\leqslant\sqrt{K^{(3)}}

and

\sigma_{1}\left(\frac{1}{K^{(1)}K^{(2)}}\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathcal{G})^{\top}\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathcal{G})\right)\leqslant 1\text{ for any }a\in[3]

Proof.

For any matrix of the factor matrices
\mathbf{A}
such that each of its entries is less than 1:

\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})\leqslant\text{tr}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{A}_{ik}^{2}\leqslant\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{A}_{ik}

To conclude, we note that this last double sum simplifies given the stochastic nature of the Tucker factor matrices in TTM (with
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
and
\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathcal{G})
belonging to Case (
C1
) and
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
belonging to Case (
C2
)).

∎

Lemma B.3

(Maximum singular values of the matricizations of
\mathcal{D}
)
.

Given any matricization
\mathbf{D}^{(a)}
of
\mathcal{D}
along axis
a\in[3]
, we have for all
k\in[\max_{a\in[3]}K^{(a)}]
,

c^{*}K^{(1)}K^{(2)}K^{(3)}N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\leqslant\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{D}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}^{(a)\top})\leqslant K^{(1)}K^{(2)}K^{(3)}N^{(1)}N^{(2)}.

Proof.

For any matrices
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
for which we can form the Kronecker product
\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}
, the following holds true:

\lambda_{1}[(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})^{\top}]=\lambda_{1}[(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{\top})\otimes(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{B}^{\top})]=\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{\top})\cdot\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{B}^{\top}),

\text{and }\lambda_{\min}[(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})^{\top}]=\lambda_{\min}[(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{\top})\otimes(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{B}^{\top})]=\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{\top})\cdot\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{B}^{\top}),

In the previous equations, the first equality follows by the mixed-product property of the Kronecker product, and the second, by property of the extremal eigenvalues of the Kronecker product
[
48
]
.
Moreover,
\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{A}^{\top})=\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}).

Therefore, for any set of indices
a_{1}\neq a_{2}\neq a_{3}
with
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\in[3]
, the matrix
\mathbf{W}^{(a_{1})}=\mathcal{M}_{a_{1}}(\mathcal{G})\cdot(\mathbf{A}^{(a_{2})}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(a_{3})})
is such that:

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{W}^{(a_{1})}\mathbf{W}^{(a_{1})\top})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}[\mathcal{M}_{a_{1}}(\mathcal{G})\cdot(\mathbf{A}^{(a_{2})}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(a_{3})})(\mathbf{A}^{(a_{2})}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(a_{3})})^{\top}\cdot\mathcal{M}_{a_{1}}(\mathcal{G})^{\top}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{a_{1}}(\mathcal{G})^{\top}\mathcal{M}_{a_{1}}(\mathcal{G}))\cdot\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{(a_{2})\top}\mathbf{A}^{(a_{2})})\cdot\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{(a_{3})\top}\mathbf{A}^{(a_{3})})

Therefore,

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{D}^{(a_{1})}\mathbf{D}^{(a_{1})\top})=\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{(a_{1})}\mathbf{W}^{(a_{1})}\mathbf{W}^{(a_{1})\top}\mathbf{A}^{(a_{1})\top})\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{a_{1}}(\mathcal{G})^{\top}\mathcal{M}_{a_{1}}(\mathcal{G}))\Pi_{a=1}^{3}\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{(a)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(a)})

Combining this with Lemma
B.2
, for any
a\in[3]
, we conclude that :

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{D}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}^{(a)\top})\leqslant K^{(1)}K^{(2)}K^{(3)}N^{(1)}N^{(2)}

Using Assumption
2.1
, we also have

\begin{split}\lambda_{K^{(a)}}(\mathbf{D}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}^{(a)\top})&=\lambda_{K^{(a)}}(\mathbf{A}^{(a_{1})}\mathbf{W}^{(a_{1})}\mathbf{W}^{(a_{1})\top}\mathbf{A}^{(a_{1})\top})\\
&\geq\lambda_{K^{(a)}}(\mathbf{A}^{(a_{1})}\mathbf{A}^{(a_{1})\top})\lambda_{K^{(a)}}(\mathbf{W}^{(a_{1})}\mathbf{W}^{(a_{1})\top})\\
&\geqslant c^{*}K^{(1)}K^{(2)}K^{(3)}N^{(1)}N^{(2)}.\\
\end{split}

(30)

∎

Lemma B.4

(From Lemma F.2 of
[
28
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
be a symmetric matrix such that all entries are lower-bounded by a constant
c_{0}
. If
\|\mathbf{\Theta}\|_{op}\leqslant c^{*}
, then there exists some constant
c>0
such that

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{\Theta})-\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{\Theta})\geqslant c.

Furthermore, the entries of the unit-norm leading positive eigenvector of
\mathbf{\Theta}
are all lower bounded by some constant
c_{1}>0
.

Proof.

To make this manuscript self-contained, we provide here the proof of Lemma
B.4
, which is an extract of the proof of Lemma F.2 in
[
28
]
. The argumentation is essentially a proof by contradiction.

Consider a sequence
\{\mathbf{\Theta}^{(n)}\}
of symmetric matrices such that all the elements are bounded below by
c_{0}
. Suppose that the gap between the first two eigenvalues of each of its elements goes to zero as
n\to\infty
. Since
\|\mathbf{\Theta}\|_{op}\leqslant c^{*}
, by the Bolzano-Weierstrass theorem, there exists a subsequence
\{\mathbf{\Theta}^{(n_{m})}\}_{m=1}^{\infty}
such that
\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbf{\Theta}^{(n_{m})}=\mathbf{\Theta}_{0}
for a fixed matrix
\mathbf{\Theta}_{0}
. By the assumptions on the sequence
\{\mathbf{\Theta}^{(n)}\}
, the matrix
\mathbf{\Theta}_{0}
has entries that are all bounded below by
c_{0}
and the gap between the first two eigenvalues of
\mathbf{\Theta}_{0}
are zero.
By Perron’s theorem
[
24
]
, such a matrix
\mathbf{\Theta}_{0}
does not exist.

To prove the second point, let
\mathbf{\eta}_{1}^{(n)}
be the unit-norm leading positive eigenvector of
\mathbf{\Theta}^{(n)}
. By Perron’s Theorem
[
24
]
, the leading eigenvector of strictly positive matrix is also strictly positive. Note
\mathbf{\eta}^{(n)}_{1}
indicates the dependency of
n
. Assume the contradiction for some
k\in[K]

\lim_{n\to\infty}\inf\mathbf{\eta}_{1}^{(n)}(k)=0.

This also implies that there exists a subsequence
\{n_{m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty}
such that
\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbf{\eta}_{1}^{(n_{m})}(k)=0
and
\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbf{\Theta}^{n_{m}}\to\mathbf{\Theta}_{0}
. By the continuity, we have
\mathbf{\eta}_{1}^{(n_{m})}\to\mathbf{\eta}_{0}
where
\mathbf{\eta}_{0}
is unit norm leading eigenvector of
\mathbf{\Theta}_{0}
up to a multiple of
\pm 1
on
\mathbf{\eta}_{1}^{(n_{m})}
. This would implies
\mathbf{\eta}_{0}(k)=0
but contradicts with the claim that the leading eigenvector of strictly positive matrix
\mathbf{\Theta}_{0}
is also all positive. This ends the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Basic Bernstein Concentration Inequalities

Lemma C.1

(Bernstein inequality (Theorem 2.8.4,
[
55
]
))
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be independent random variables with
|X_{i}|\leqslant b
,
\mathbb{E}[X_{i}]=0
and
\text{Var}[X_{i}]\leq\sigma_{i}^{2}
for all
i
. Let
\sigma^{2}:=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}^{2}
. Then for any
t>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left(n^{-1}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\right|\geq t\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{nt^{2}/2}{\sigma^{2}+bt/3}\right)

Lemma C.2

(Matrix Bernstein Inequality
[
53
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots\mathbf{X}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times r}
be independent random matrices such that
\|\mathbf{X}_{i}\|_{op}\leqslant b
and
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{i}]=0
for all
i
. Let

\sigma^{2}=\max\{\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{i}\mathbf{X}_{i}^{\top}]\|_{op},\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{i}]\|_{op}\}.

Then, for all
t>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{X}_{i}\right\|_{op}\geqslant t\right)\leqslant(p+r)\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}n^{2}/2}{\sigma^{2}+btn/3}\right)

Lemma C.3

(Bernstein Inequality with permutations)
.

Let
X_{1,1},\dots,X_{N_{1},N_{2}}
for all
i\in[N_{1}]
,
j\in[N_{2}]
be independent random variables such that
|X_{i,j}|\leqslant 1
,
\mathbb{E}[X_{i,j}]=0
and
\text{Var}[X_{i,j}]=\sigma^{2}
. Define
Q_{i,M}=Q(X_{i,1},\dots,X_{i,N_{2}})=\sum_{m=1}^{M}X_{i,m}
for some integer
M\leqslant N_{2}
, and for any permutation
\pi
of
[N_{2}]
, define
Q_{i,\pi}=Q(X_{i,\pi(1)},\dots,X_{i,\pi(N_{2})})
. With these notations,
Q_{i,M}
is the identity permutation of
Q_{i,\pi}
.

Let
V=V(X_{1,1},\dots X_{N_{1},N_{2}})
be the random variable defined as
V=\frac{1}{N_{2}!}\sum_{i\in[N_{1}]}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(N_{2})}Q_{i,\pi}
for some permutation
\pi
on set
[N_{2}]
(where
\Pi
denotes all possible permutations of the set
[N_{2}]
).

Then, for all
t>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}(V\geqslant t)\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}/2}{MN_{1}\sigma^{2}+t/3}\right)

Proof.

Consider first the case where
N_{1}=1
(fix
i
), so
V
can simply be rewritten as:
V=\frac{1}{N_{2}!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(N_{2})}Q_{\pi}
.

By Markov’s inequality and the convexity of the exponential function, we have for any
s,t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V\geq t\right)\leqslant e^{-st}\mathbb{E}[e^{sV}]\leqslant e^{-st}\left(\frac{1}{N_{2}!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(N_{2})}\mathbb{E}[e^{sQ_{\pi}}]\right).

(31)

Let
G(x)=\frac{e^{x}-1-x}{x^{2}}
, which is an increasing function of
x
.
Expanding the exponential, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{sX_{m}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[1+sX_{m}+\frac{s^{2}X_{m}^{2}}{2}+\dots\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[1+s^{2}X_{m}^{2}\left(\frac{e^{sX_{m}}-1-sX_{m}}{s^{2}X_{m}^{2}}\right)\right]\quad\quad\quad(\text{ since }\mathbb{E}[X_{m}]=0)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[1+s^{2}X_{m}^{2}G(sX_{m})\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{E}\left[1+s^{2}X_{m}^{2}G(s)\right]\quad\quad\quad(\text{ since }|X_{m}|\leqslant 1)

\displaystyle=1+s^{2}\sigma^{2}G(s)\leqslant e^{s^{2}\sigma^{2}G(s)}

This inequality holds for any
m\in[M]
and permutation
{\pi}
. Since the variables
X_{m}
for
m\in[M]
are independent,
\mathbb{E}[e^{sQ_{\pi}}]=\prod_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}[e^{sX_{\pi(m)}}]
and we are able to rewrite (
31
) as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V\geqslant t\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\exp(-st+Ms^{2}\sigma^{2}G(s))=\exp\left\{-st+M\sigma^{2}\left(e^{s}-1-s\right)\right\}

(32)

Let
s=\log\left(1+\frac{t}{M\sigma^{2}}\right)>0
, then:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(V\geqslant t\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\exp\left\{-t\log\left(1+\frac{t}{M\sigma^{2}}\right)+M\sigma^{2}\left(\frac{t}{M\sigma^{2}}-\log\left(1+\frac{t}{M\sigma^{2}}\right)\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{-M\sigma^{2}\left[\left(1+\frac{t}{M\sigma^{2}}\right)\log\left(1+\frac{t}{M\sigma^{2}}\right)-\frac{t}{M\sigma^{2}}\right]\right\}

Let
H(x)=(1+x)\log(1+x)-x
and note that
H(x)\geqslant\frac{3x^{2}}{6+2x}
. Evaluating the function
H(x)
at
x=\frac{t}{M\sigma^{2}}
, the previous bound guarantees:

\mathbb{P}\left(V\geqslant t\right)\leqslant\exp\left\{-M\sigma^{2}\left(\frac{3t^{2}}{M^{2}\sigma^{4}(6+2t/(M\sigma^{2}))}\right)\right\}=\exp\left\{-\frac{t^{2}/2}{M\sigma^{2}+t/3}\right\}.

We now consider the case where
N_{1}>1
. Since all
\{X_{i,m}\}_{i\in[N^{(1)}],m\in[M]}
are independent, for any value of the tuple
(i,m)
, (
31
) can be rewritten as:

\mathbb{P}[V\geq t]\leq e^{-st}\mathbb{E}[e^{sV}]=e^{-st}\mathbb{E}[e^{s\sum_{i\in[N_{1}]}\frac{1}{N_{2}!}\sum_{\pi}Q_{i\pi}}]=e^{-st}\prod_{i\in[N_{1}]}\mathbb{E}[e^{s\frac{1}{N_{2}!}\sum_{\pi}Q_{i\pi}}]\leq e^{-st}e^{N_{1}Ms^{2}\sigma^{2}G(s)},

where the last inequality follows by applying Equations (
31
) and (
32
) to each of the
i
terms in the product. Therefore, the result follows by a similar reasoning to the case where
N_{1}=1
by replacing
M
with
MN_{1}
.
∎

Appendix D
Properties of the matricized noise
\mathbf{Z}^{(a)}=\mathcal{M}_{(a)}(\mathcal{Y}-\mathcal{D})

D.1
Properties of the moments of the matricized noise

Let
N_{R}=\max\{N^{(1)},N^{(2)},R\}
.

Lemma D.1
.

Assuming the multinomial model of TTM in Equation (
4
) and letting
\mathbf{Z}^{(a)}=\mathcal{M}_{(a)}(\mathcal{Y}-\mathcal{D})
denote the matricization of the noise
\mathcal{Z}
along each axis
a\in[3]
, the following equalities hold:

•

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\mathbf{Z}^{(1)\top}]=\frac{1}{M}\text{diag}\left(N^{(2)}-\|\mathbf{D}^{(1)}_{1\star}\|_{2}^{2}\dots,N^{(2)}-\|\mathbf{D}^{(1)}_{N^{(1)}\star}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(1)}\times N^{(1)}}

•

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(2)}\mathbf{Z}^{(2)\top}]=\frac{1}{M}\text{diag}\left(N^{(1)}-\|\mathbf{D}^{(2)}_{1\star}\|_{F}^{2}\dots,N^{(1)}-\|\mathbf{D}^{(2)}_{N^{(2)}\star}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(2)}\times N^{(2)}}

•

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\mathbf{Z}^{(3)\top}]=\frac{1}{M}\left[\text{diag}\left(\mathbf{D}^{(3)}\mathbf{1}_{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\right)-\mathbf{D}^{(3)}\mathbf{D}^{(3)\top}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times R}

Proof.

•

Consider first the case of mode-1 matricization, with
\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}=\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{Z})
. By definition,
\forall i_{1},i_{2}\in[N^{(1)}]
:

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[(\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{Z})\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{Z})^{\top})_{i_{1}i_{2}}]&=\mathbb{E}[\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\sum_{r\in[R]}\mathcal{Z}_{i_{1}jr}\mathcal{Z}_{i_{2}jr}]\\
&=\frac{1}{M}\begin{cases}0\text{ if }i_{1}\neq i_{2}\\
\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\sum_{r\in[R]}\left(\mathcal{D}_{i_{1}jr}-\mathcal{D}^{2}_{i_{1}jr}\right)\qquad\text{ }\end{cases}\\
&=\frac{1}{M}\begin{cases}0\text{ if }i_{1}\neq i_{2}\\
N^{(2)}-\|\mathcal{D}^{2}_{i_{1},\cdot}\|_{2}^{2}\qquad\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}\\
\end{split}

where the penultimate equality follows by applying Remark A.1 (Equation (
29
)), and the last line follows from the properties of
\mathcal{D}
established in Equation (
6
).

•

Since for mode-2 matricization, the structure of the problem is identical to that of mode-1 matricization, it suffices to replace
N^{(1)}
by
N^{(2)}.

•

For mode-3 matricization, for any
r_{1},r_{2}\in[R]
, we note that by Equation (
29
):

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[(\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathcal{Z})\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathcal{Z})^{\top})_{r_{1}r_{2}}]&=\mathbb{E}[\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr_{1}}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr_{2}}]\\
&=\frac{1}{M}\begin{cases}-\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{2}}\text{ if }r_{1}\neq r_{2}\\
\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\left(\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}-\mathcal{D}^{2}_{ijr_{1}}\right)\qquad\text{ if }r_{1}=r_{2}\end{cases}\\
\end{split}

(33)

Which concludes the proof.

∎

Lemma D.2
.

Under the same assumptions as Lemma
D.1
, we have:

\displaystyle c^{*}\frac{N^{(2)}}{M}\leqslant

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\mathbf{Z}^{(1)\top}]\|_{op}\leqslant\frac{N^{(2)}}{M},\quad c^{*}\frac{N^{(1)}}{M}\leqslant\|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(2)}\mathbf{Z}^{(2)\top}]\|_{op}\leqslant\frac{N^{(1)}}{M},

\displaystyle\quad\text{and }c^{*}\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}{M}\leqslant\|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\mathbf{Z}^{(3)\top}]\|_{op}\leqslant\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}{M}

Proof.

The proof is a direct consequence of Lemma
D.1
, and follows trivially for the mode 1 and 2 matricizations of
\mathcal{Z}
given the diagonal nature of the covariance matrix, with
c^{*}
any constant such that
c^{*}\leq N^{(a)}-\max_{i}\|\mathcal{D}^{(a)}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}
for
a\in\{1,2\}.

For our mode-3 matricization of
\mathcal{Z}
, we note that:

\mathbb{E}[(\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathcal{Z})\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathcal{Z})^{\top})]=\frac{1}{M}\text{diag}\left(\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\right)-\frac{1}{M}\mathcal{M}^{(3)}(\mathcal{D})\mathcal{M}^{(3)}(\mathcal{D})^{T}

For any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{R}
such that
\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}=1:

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{T}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}]\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{r_{1}\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\left(\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\mathbf{u}_{r_{1}}^{2}\right)-\sum_{r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{2}}\mathbf{u}_{r_{1}}\mathbf{u}_{r_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{r_{1}\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\mathbf{u}_{r_{1}}^{2}-\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\left(\sum_{r_{1}\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\mathbf{u}_{r_{1}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{r_{1}\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\mathbf{u}_{r_{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{r_{1}\in\mathbb{R}}\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\quad\text{ since }\sum_{r_{1}}\mathbf{u}_{r_{1}}^{2}=1

\displaystyle\leq\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}{M}

Therefore:

\|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\mathbf{Z}^{(3)\top}]\|_{op}\leq\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}{M}

We also have, for any
r_{0}\in[R]
, letting
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}
be the indicator vector of
r_{0}
(ie
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}_{r_{0}}=1
, and
\tilde{\mathbf{u}}_{r}=0
for any
r\neq r_{0}

):

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\mathbf{Z}^{(3)\top}]\|_{op}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{r_{1}\in R}\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}_{r_{1}}^{2}-\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\left(\sum_{r_{1}\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\tilde{\mathbf{u}}_{r_{1}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{0}}-\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\left(\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{0}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq c^{*}\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}{M}

with
c^{*}=\min_{r}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}(1-\mathcal{D}_{ijr}).

∎

D.2
Concentration bounds of the matricized noise

Lemma D.3

(Concentration of the maximum of the entries in
\mathcal{Z}.
)
.

Suppose
\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}\leq c_{0}
for some constant
c_{0}
.
Then with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
, for every
i\in[N^{(1)}],j\in[N^{(2)}]
and
r\in[R]
, we have

|\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}|\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}}

Proof.

Recall that, in Remark
B.2
, we defined
\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}
as the average of
M
i.i.d centered variables:
\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m\in[M]}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)
where
|\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)|\leqslant 1
and
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)]=0
.
We further note that by direct application of Equation (
25
),
\text{Var}[\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)]\leqslant\mathcal{D}_{ijr}\leqslant 1
. By Bernstein’s inequality
[
55
]
(Lemma
C.1
), we then have, for any
t>0
:

\mathbb{P}\left(|\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}|\geqslant t\right)\leqslant 2\exp\left(-\frac{Mt^{2}/2}{1+t/3}\right)

Taking
t=C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}}
for some constant
C^{*}
, we have, by a simple union bound:

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}[\exists(i,j,r):|\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}|\geqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}}]&\leq 2N^{(1)}N^{(2)}R\exp\left(-\frac{\frac{(C^{*})^{2}}{2}\log(N_{R})}{1+\frac{C^{*}}{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}}}\right)\\
&\leq\exp\left(3\log(N_{R})-\frac{\frac{(C^{*})^{2}}{2}\log(N_{R})}{1+\frac{C^{*}}{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}}}\right)\\
&\leq\exp(-(C_{0}-3)\log(N_{R})).\end{split}

(34)

for some constant
C_{0}
, assuming that the ratio
\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}
is bounded above by a constant
c_{0}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma D.4

(Concentration of the operator norm of the matricized noise)
.

Suppose
\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}\leq c_{0}
for some constant
c_{0}
. Then, with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
:

\begin{split}&\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\|_{op}\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\log(N_{R})}{M}}\\
&\|\mathbf{Z}^{(2)}\|_{op}\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\log(N_{R})}{M}}\\
&\|\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\|_{op}\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\log(N_{R})}{M}}.\end{split}

(35)

Moreover, with the same probability:

\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}_{i\star}\|_{2}\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{N^{(2)}\log(N_{R})}{M}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\|\mathbf{Z}^{(2)}_{j\star}\|_{2}\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}\log(N_{R})}{M}}

(36)

Proof.

Analysis of Mode-1 and Mode-2 matricization:

Consider first the random matrix
\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(1)}\times(N^{(2)}R)}
. By Remark
B.2
, the rows of the matrix
\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}
are independent, and, as before, we write
\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}
as an average of
M
variables:

\mathbf{Z}^{(1)\top}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}

where the set of matrices
\{\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}\}_{m\in[M]}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(2)}R\times N^{(1)}}
are independent matrices such that
\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}(jr,i)=\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)
. The product
\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(1)\top}_{m}
has entries
\left(\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(1)\top}_{m}\right)(j_{1}r_{1},j_{2}r_{2})=\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\mathcal{X}_{ij_{1}m}(r_{1})\mathcal{X}_{ij_{2}m}(r_{2})
. By (
29
):

\mathbb{E}[\left(\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(1)\top}_{m}\right)(j_{1}r_{1},j_{2}r_{2})]=\begin{cases}-\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{2}}&\text{ if }j_{1}=j_{2}=j,r_{1}\neq r_{2}\\
\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}(\mathcal{D}_{ijr}-\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2})&\text{ if }j_{1}=j_{2}=j,r_{1}=r_{2}=r\\
0&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}

Therefore:

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{m}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(1)\top}_{m}]\right\|_{op}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(1)\top}_{m}]\right\|_{op}\leqslant\left\|\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(1)\top}_{m}]\right\|_{F}\leqslant\sum_{m=1}^{M}\left\|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(1)\top}_{m}]\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leqslant M\sqrt{\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}],r\in[R]}\left(\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}(1-\mathcal{D}_{ijr})\right)^{2}+\sum_{j\in[N^{(2)}]}\sum_{r_{1}\neq r_{2}}\left(\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{2}}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant M\sqrt{N^{(1)}}\sqrt{\sum_{i,j,r}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2}(1-\mathcal{D}_{ijr})^{2}+\sum_{i,j}\sum_{r_{1}\neq r_{2}}(\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{2}})^{2}}\text{ by Cauchy Schwarz}

\displaystyle\leqslant M\sqrt{N^{(1)}}\sqrt{\sum_{i,j,r}(\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2}-2\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{3}+\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{4})+\sum_{i,j}\sum_{r_{1}\neq r_{2}}(\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{2}})^{2}}

\displaystyle=M\sqrt{N^{(1)}}\sqrt{\sum_{i,j,r}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2}(1-2\mathcal{D}_{ijr})+\sum_{i,j}\sum_{r_{1},r_{2}}(\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{2}})^{2}}

\displaystyle=M\sqrt{N^{(1)}}\sqrt{\sum_{i,j,r}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2}(1-2\mathcal{D}_{ijr})+\sum_{i,j}(\sum_{r_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq M\sqrt{N^{(1)}}\sqrt{\sum_{i,j,r}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}+\sum_{i,j}(\sum_{r_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant M\sqrt{N^{(1)}}\sqrt{\sum_{i,j}1+\sum_{i,j}1}\qquad\text{since }\sum_{r}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}=1

\displaystyle\leqslant MN^{(1)}\sqrt{2N^{(2)}}\leqslant MN^{(1)}N^{(2)}.

On the other hand, we have
[\mathbf{X}^{(1)\top}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}]_{i_{1}i_{2}}=\sum_{j,r}\mathcal{X}_{i_{1}jm}(r)\mathcal{X}_{i_{2}jm}(r)
. We have, by (
29
):

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{(1)\top}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}]=\text{diag}\left(\sum_{jr}(\mathcal{D}_{1jr}-\mathcal{D}_{1jr}^{2}),\dots,\sum_{jr}(\mathcal{D}_{N^{(1)}jr}-\mathcal{D}_{N^{(1)}jr}^{2})\right).

Thus

\|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{(1)\top}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}]\|_{op}\leqslant N^{(2)}
and
\|\sum_{m}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{(1)\top}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}]\|_{op}\leqslant N^{(2)}M
.

We further note that for any
i\in[N^{(1)}]
,

\|(\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m})_{i\star}\|^{2}_{2}=\sum_{j,r}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)^{2}=\sum_{j,r}(\mathbf{T}_{ijm}(r)-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{T}_{ijm}(r)])^{2}\leqslant 2N^{(2)}.

Thus, for any unit norm vector
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(1)}},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(2)}R}
,

\mathbf{u}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m})\mathbf{v}=\sum_{i}\sum_{j,r}\mathbf{u}_{i}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathbf{v}_{(j_{r})}\leqslant\sqrt{\sum_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i}^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i}((X_{m})_{i\star}\mathbf{v})^{2}}\leqslant\sqrt{2N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}.

It follows that

\|\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}\|_{op}^{2}=\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{S}^{N^{(1)}-1},\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{S}^{N^{(2)}R-1}}\|\mathbf{u}^{\top}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}\mathbf{v}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant 2N^{(2)}N^{(1)}

By the Matrix Bernstein inequality (Lemma
C.2
), we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}\right\|_{op}\geqslant t\right)\leqslant(N^{(1)}+N^{(2)}R)\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}M^{2}}{2(\sigma^{2}+LtM/3)}\right)

where
\sigma^{2}=N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M
and
\mathbf{L}=\sqrt{2N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}
.

Choosing
t=C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\log(N_{R})}{M}}
for some constant
C^{*}
, and noting that
N^{(1)}+N^{(2)}R\leq 2N_{R}^{2}
, the previous inequality becomes:

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{m}\right\|_{op}\geqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\log(N_{R})}{M}}\right)&\leqslant 2N_{R}^{2}\exp\left(-\frac{(C^{*})^{2}MN^{(1)}N^{(2)}\log(N_{R})}{2(N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3}\sqrt{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M\log(N_{R})}})\right)\\
&=\exp\left(2\log(N_{R})+\log(2)-\frac{\frac{(C^{*})^{2}}{2}\log(N_{R})}{1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}}\right).\\
\end{split}

Therefore, for
C^{*}
large enough, the result in the first line of Equation (
35
) holds.

We now turn to the bound on the row norm
\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}_{i\star}\|_{2}
(Equation (
36
)). In this case, we note that for any tuple
i,j,r
, since
|\mathcal{X}_{imj}(r)|\leq 1
and
\text{Var}(\mathcal{X}_{imj}(r))\leq 1
(see Remark
B.2
), by Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
C.1
), for a choice of
t=C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}}
with
C^{*}
a constant:

\mathbb{P}[|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)|>C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}}]\leq 2\exp(-\frac{(C^{*})^{2}\log(N_{R})/2}{1+\frac{C^{*}}{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}}})

Equation (
36
) follows by a simple union bound, with a choice of
C^{*}
large enough and provided that the ratio
\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}\leq c_{0}
.

The results for
\mathbf{Z}^{(2)}
follow directly from those on
\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}
, due to the symmetry of the roles played by the first two dimensions.

Analysis of Mode-3 matricization:

We now adapt the proof to show the results for
\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}
, due to its row-stochastic properties (matrix of type
C2
). In this case, the matrix
\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times(N^{(1)}N^{(2)})}
has independent column vectors. We set define matrix
\mathbf{X}^{(3)}_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times N^{(1,2)}}
such that
[\mathbf{X}^{(3)}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(3)\top}_{m}](r_{1},r_{2})=\sum_{ij}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r_{1})\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r_{2})
. Then, by Remark
B.2
, Equation (
29
):

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{(3)}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(3)\top}_{m}]=\text{diag}(\mathbf{D}^{(3)}\mathbf{1}_{N^{(1,2)}})-\mathbf{D}^{(3)}\mathbf{D}^{(3)\top},

which, Lemma
B.3
, implies
\|\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{(3)}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(3)\top}_{m}]\|_{op}\leqslant M\|(\mathbf{D}^{(3)}\mathbf{D}^{(3)\top}\|_{op}\leq K^{(1,2,3)}N^{(1,2)}M.

Furthermore,

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{(3)\top}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(3)}_{m}]=\text{diag}\left(\{1-\sum_{r\in[R]}\mathcal{D}^{2}_{ijr}\}_{(i,j)\in[N^{(1)}]\times[N^{(2)}]}\right)

By the same arguments as above, we have
\|\sum_{m\in[M]}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{X}^{(3)}_{m}\mathbf{X}^{(3)\top}_{m}]\|\leqslant M
. We then define:

\sigma^{2}=K^{(1)}K^{(2)}K^{(3)}N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\lesssim N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M
.
To apply the matrix Bernstein, we now need to bound the operator norm
\|\mathbf{X}^{(3)}_{m}\|
. Since, by definition of
\mathcal{X}
, for each column
(i,j)
,

\|(\mathbf{X}^{(3)}_{m})_{\star(i,j)}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{r}(\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r))^{2}\leqslant 2
, it is easy to see that
\|\mathbf{X}^{(3)}_{m}\|_{op}^{2}\leq\|\mathbf{X}^{(3)}_{m}\|_{F}^{2}\leqslant 2N^{(1)}N^{(2)}
as well. The result on the operator norm of
\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}
in Equation (
35
) thus follows by similar arguments to mode-1 matricization, using the Matrix Bernstein’s inequality.

∎

Lemma D.5
.

Suppose
f_{r}\geqslant{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}
. Then with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
, the following inequality holds:

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N^{(1)},N^{(2)}}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\right|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}\text{ for all }r\in[R]

(37)

Proof.

We note again that
\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)
where
\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)
is independent across
i\in[N^{(1)}]
,
j\in[N^{(2)}]
and
m\in[M]
. Moreover, each
\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)
is such that
|\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)|\leqslant 1
and
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)]=0
,
\text{Var}[\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)]=\mathcal{D}_{ijr}-\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2}\leqslant\mathcal{D}_{ijr}\leqslant f_{r}
.

Therefore, by Berstein’s inequality (Lemma
C.1
), for any
t>0
:

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\left|\sum_{i,j,m}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\right|\geq t\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}Mt^{2}/2}{f_{r}+t/3}\right)

Choosing
t=C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}
, the previous equation becomes:

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\left|\sum_{i,j,m}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\right|\geq C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}\right)&\leq 2\exp\left(-(C^{*})^{2}\frac{\log(N^{(r)})/2}{1+\frac{C^{*}}{3}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{f_{r}N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}}\right)\\
&\leq\exp(-(C^{*}\log(N_{R}))\end{split}

(38)

as long as
f_{r}\geqslant\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}
. By application of a simple union bound along the
R\leq N_{R}
words, we prove equation (
37
).

∎

Lemma D.6
.

Suppose
f_{r}\geqslant\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}
for all
r\in[R]
. Then, with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N^{(2)}}\sum_{j,r=1}^{N^{(2)},R}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}^{2}-\frac{1}{M}(1-\frac{1}{N^{(2)}}\sum_{j,r}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2})\right|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(2)}M}}

(39)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N^{(1)}}\sum_{i,r=1}^{N^{(1)},R}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}^{2}-\frac{1}{M}(1-\frac{1}{N^{(1)}}\sum_{i,r}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2})\right|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}M}}

(40)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N^{(1)},N^{(2)}}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}^{2}-\frac{1}{M}\cdot\frac{1}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\sum_{i,j}(\mathcal{D}_{ijr}-\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2})\right|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}+C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}^{2}\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}

(41)

Proof.

We note that for the squared entries
\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}^{2}
, the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{m}\sum_{m^{\prime}}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ijm^{\prime}}(r)

(42)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{m}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)^{2}+\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{m\neq m^{\prime}}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ijm^{\prime}}(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{m}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)^{2}+\frac{M-1}{M}\frac{1}{M(M-1)}\sum_{m\neq m^{\prime}}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ijm^{\prime}}(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{m}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)^{2}+\frac{M-1}{M}\frac{1}{M!}\sum_{\pi\in{\Pi}[M]}\frac{1}{\lfloor M/2\rfloor}\sum_{m\in\lfloor M/2\rfloor}\mathcal{X}_{ij\pi(2m-1)}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ij\pi(2m)}(r)

We first note that by Remark
B.2
Equation (
29
),

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}^{2}]=\frac{1}{M}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}(1-\mathcal{D}_{ijr}).\qquad\eqref{eq: prob v in bernstein permute}

Moreover, for the first term, leftmost in the sum above, we propose using Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
C.1
), while we will consider Lemma
C.3
to bound the second summand. The use of Bernstein inequalities requires characterizing the means and variances of the corresponding random variables
\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)^{2}
.

Consequently, we split the analysis depending on the matricization mode:

•

Mode 1 & 2 matricization.

As highlighted in Remark
B.1
, Modes 1 and 2 exhibit similar behaviours. We analyse here mode 2, and the results for mode 1 follow by replacing
N^{(2)}
with
N^{(1)}
.

1.

Expectation:
In this case, given observation (
31
) above, the expectation
\frac{1}{N^{(1)}}\sum_{i,r}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}^{2}
simplifies to:

\begin{split}\frac{1}{N^{(1)}}\sum_{i,r}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}^{2}]&=\frac{1}{N^{(1)}}\sum_{i,r}\frac{1}{M}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}(1-\mathcal{D}_{ijr})=\frac{1}{M}-\frac{1}{MN^{(1)}}\sum_{i,r}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2}.\end{split}

2.

First summand:

Define
\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{ijm}=\sum_{r\in[R]}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}^{2}(r)
. It is easy to see that
|\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{ijm}|\leqslant 2
. Indeed, letting
r_{0}
denote the index for which
\mathcal{T}_{ijm}(r_{0})=1
, we have:

\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{ijm}=\sum_{r\in[R]}\big(\mathcal{T}_{ijm}(r)-2\mathcal{T}_{ijm}(r)\mathcal{D}_{ijr}+\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2})\leq 1-2\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{0}}+1\leq 2.

Furthermore, we have:

\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{ijm}]=\sum_{r}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}(1-\mathcal{D}_{ijr})
, and

\begin{split}\text{Var}[\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{ijm}]&=\sum_{r,r^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}^{2}_{ijm}(r)\mathcal{X}^{2}_{ijm}(r^{\prime})]-\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Q}_{ijm}]^{2}\\
&\leq\sum_{r}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}(1-\mathcal{D}_{ijr})(1-3\mathcal{D}_{ijr}(1-\mathcal{D}_{ijr}))\\
&+\sum_{r\neq r^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}\mathcal{D}_{ijr^{\prime}}\left(-7\mathcal{D}_{ijr}\mathcal{D}_{ijr^{\prime}}+3(\mathcal{D}_{ijr}+\mathcal{D}_{ijr^{\prime}})-1\right)\\
&\leq\sum_{r}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}+2\sum_{r,r^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}\mathcal{D}_{ijr^{\prime}}\\
&\leqslant 3\end{split}

Therefore, by Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
C.1
), for any
t>0
:

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{N^{(1)}M}\sum_{i,m}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{ijm}-\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{N^{(1)}M}\sum_{i,m}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{ijm}]\right|\geq t\right)\leqslant 2\exp\left(-\frac{N^{(1)}Mt^{2}/2}{3+2t/3}\right)

Choosing
t=C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}M}}
for some constant
C^{*}
, we obtain:

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{N^{(1)}M}\sum_{i,m}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{ijm}-\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{N^{(1)}M}\sum_{i,m}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{ijm}]\right|\geq C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}M}}\right)\leqslant 2\exp\left(-C\log(N_{R})\right).

(43)

Therefore, by a simple union bound, provided that
c
is large enough, there exists a constant
{C}
such that:

\mathbb{P}\left(\forall j\in[N^{(2)}]:\left|\frac{1}{N^{(1)}M}\sum_{i,m}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{ijm}-\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{N^{(1)}M}\sum_{i,m}\tilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{ijm}]\right|\geq c\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}M}}\right)\leqslant e^{-{C}\log(N_{R})}.

3.

Second summand:
For the terms involving
m\neq m^{\prime}
, we define
\mathcal{Q}_{ijmm^{\prime}}=\sum_{r}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)X_{ijm^{\prime}}(r)
. In this case, it is also possible to verify that, for fix
i,j,m,m^{\prime}
,

|\mathcal{Q}_{ijmm^{\prime}}|\leqslant 2,\qquad\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Q}_{ijmm^{\prime}}]=0,

\begin{split}\text{Var}[{\mathcal{Q}}_{ijmm^{\prime}}]&=\sum_{r,r^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ijm^{\prime}}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r^{\prime})\mathcal{X}_{ijm^{\prime}}(r^{\prime})]-\mathbb{E}[{\mathcal{Q}}_{ijmm^{\prime}}]^{2}\\
&=\sum_{r,r^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r^{\prime})]\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}_{ijm^{\prime}}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ijm^{\prime}}(r^{\prime})]\\
&=\sum_{r}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2}(1-\mathcal{D}_{ijr})^{2}-\sum_{r\neq r^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{ijr^{\prime}}^{2}\\
&\leqslant 1.\end{split}

Applying Lemma
C.3
, we obtain that, with probability
1-\mathbf{O}(\frac{1}{N_{R}})

\left|\frac{1}{N^{(1)}M!}\sum_{i,\pi}\sum_{m}\mathcal{Q}_{ij,\pi}\right|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{M\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}}}.

(44)

By a simple union bound argument, we thus have:

\mathbb{P}[\forall m,m^{\prime}:\quad\left|\frac{1}{N^{(1)}M!}\sum_{i,\pi}\sum_{m}\mathcal{Q}_{ij,\pi}\right|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{M\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}}}]\leq\exp(-C\log(N_{R})).

Adding Equations (
43
) and (
44
) yields the results in (
39
) and subsequently, by substitution of
i
to
j
and
N^{(1)}
by
N^{(2)}
, in (
40
).

•

Mode 3 matricization

1.

First summand:
we have, for any tuple
i,j,r
:
|\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)^{2}|\leqslant 2
,
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)^{2}]=\mathcal{D}_{ijr}-\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2}
and
\text{Var}[\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)^{2}]\leqslant\mathcal{D}_{ijr}\leqslant f_{r}
(see Appendix
B.2
, noting that here documents are indexed by the tuple
(i,j)
).

By direct application of the Bernstein Inequality (Lemma
C.1
), as long as
f_{r}\geqslant\frac{\log(N_{R})}{MN^{(1)}N^{(2)}}
, then with probability at least
1-\mathbf{O}(\frac{1}{N_{R}}):

\left|\frac{1}{MN^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\sum_{ijm}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\frac{1}{MN^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\sum_{ijm}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)^{2}]\right|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}\log(N_{R})}{MN^{(1)}N^{(2)}}}.

(45)

2.

Second summand:
For
m\neq m^{\prime}
, we consider
\mathcal{Q}^{r}_{ijm}=\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m-1)}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m)}(r)
.
It is easy to see that for any fixed
r
, the
\mathcal{Q}^{r}
s are such that, for any tuple of indices
(i,j,m)
:

|\mathcal{Q}^{r}_{ijm}|=|\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m-1)}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m)}(r)|\leqslant 1
,
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Q}^{r}_{ijm}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m-1)}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m)}(r)]=0
and
\text{Var}[\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m-1)}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m)}(r)]=(\mathcal{D}_{ijr}-\mathcal{D}_{ijr}^{2})^{2}\leqslant\mathcal{D}_{ijr}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}\leqslant f_{r}^{2}
. Let
\tilde{M}=\lfloor M/2\rfloor
. Applying Lemma
C.3
, for any
t>0
:

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{N^{(1,2)}}\frac{1}{M!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi[M]}\sum_{i,j}\sum_{m=1}^{\tilde{M}}\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m-1)}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m)}(r)\right|\geqslant t\right)\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{N^{(1,2)}t^{2}/2}{\tilde{M}f_{r}^{2}+t/3}\right)

Choosing
t=C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}^{2}\tilde{M}\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1,2)}}}
as long as
f_{r}\geqslant c^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{MN^{(1)}N^{(2)}}}
, we obtain a bound on the second term that is similar to (
45
), and adding the two, we obtain result (
41
).

∎

Lemma D.7
.

Suppose
f_{r}\geqslant\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}
for all
r\in[R]
. Then, with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
:

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N^{(2)}}\sum_{j,r=1}^{N^{(2)},R}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathcal{Z}_{i^{\prime}jr}\right|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(2)}M}}\text{ for all }i\neq i^{\prime}

(46)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N^{(1)}}\sum_{i,r=1}^{N^{(1)},R}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathcal{Z}_{ij^{\prime}r}\right|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}M}}\text{ for all }j\neq j^{\prime}

(47)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N^{(1)},N^{(2)}}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{M}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}\mathcal{D}_{ijr^{\prime}}\right|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}f_{r^{\prime}}\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}\text{ for all }r\neq r^{\prime}

(48)

Proof.

We rewrite the product
\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathcal{Z}_{i^{\prime}jr}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathcal{Z}_{ij^{\prime}r}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr^{\prime}}
for
i\neq i^{\prime}
,
j\neq j^{\prime}
, and
r\neq r^{\prime}
in a similar way to Equation (
42
).
Like in the proof of Lemma
D.6
, we use Bernstein inequalities (Lemma
C.1
and
32
) to link two terms involved in (
42
).

•

Mode 1 & 2 matricization

Since mode-1 and mode-2 matricizations (Equations (
46
) and (
47
) respectively) have similar behaviors, we provide here the proof for mode-1 matricization.

1.

Adapting Equation (
42
), we have for all
i\neq i^{\prime}
:

\begin{split}\left|\frac{1}{N^{(2)}}\sum_{j,r=1}^{N^{(2)},R}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathcal{Z}_{i^{\prime}jr}\right|&=\left|\frac{1}{M^{2}N^{(2)}}\sum_{j,r=1}^{N^{(2)},R}\sum_{m,m^{\prime}}^{M}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathcal{X}_{i^{\prime}jm^{\prime}}(r)\right|\end{split}

For a fixed tuple
i,i^{\prime}
such that
i\neq i^{\prime}
, let
\mathcal{Q}_{jmm^{\prime}}=\sum_{r\in[R]}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathcal{X}_{i^{\prime}jm^{\prime}}(r)
.
Then:

\begin{split}\left|\frac{1}{N^{(2)}}\sum_{j,r=1}^{N^{(2)},R}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathcal{Z}_{i^{\prime}jr}\right|&=\left|\frac{1}{M^{2}N^{(2)}}\sum_{j}^{N^{(2)}}\sum_{m,m^{\prime}=1}^{M}\mathcal{Q}_{jmm^{\prime}}\right|\\
&=\left|\frac{1}{M^{2}N^{(2)}}\sum_{j}^{N^{(2)}}\sum_{m}^{M}\mathcal{Q}_{jmm}+\frac{1}{M^{2}N^{(2)}}\sum_{j}^{N^{(2)}}\sum_{m\neq m^{\prime}}^{M}\mathcal{Q}_{jmm^{\prime}}\right|\end{split}

2.

By direct application of Cauchy-Schwarz:

|\mathcal{Q}_{jmm}|\leq\sqrt{\sum_{r}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{r}\mathcal{X}_{ijm^{\prime}}(r)^{2}}\leq\sqrt{\sum_{r}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)}\sqrt{\sum_{r}\mathcal{X}_{ijm^{\prime}}(r)}=1.

By independence of the variables
\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)
for
i\neq i^{\prime}
, we have:

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Q}_{jmm}]=0
, and

\displaystyle\text{Var}[\mathcal{Q}_{jmm}]=

\displaystyle\sum_{r_{1}}\sum_{r_{2}}\text{Cov}\left(\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r_{1})\mathcal{X}_{i^{\prime}jm}(r_{1}),\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r_{2})\mathcal{X}_{i^{\prime}jm}(r_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\sum_{r_{1}}\sum_{r_{2}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{ijr_{2}}\mathcal{D}_{i^{\prime}jr_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{i^{\prime}jr_{2}}\leqslant c

By Bernstein’s inequality Lemma
C.1
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{N^{(2)}M}\sum_{j,m}\mathcal{Q}_{ii^{\prime}jm}\right|\geq t\right)\leqslant 2\exp\left(-\frac{N^{(2)}Mt^{2}/2}{c+t/3}\right)

We then choose
t=c\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(2)}M}}
to bound the first term.

3.

For
m\neq m^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{jmm^{\prime}}=\sum_{r}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)X_{i^{\prime}jm^{\prime}}(r)
also verifies
|\mathcal{Q}_{jmm^{\prime}}|\leqslant 1
.
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Q}_{jmm^{\prime}}]=0
,
\text{Var}[\mathcal{Q}_{jmm^{\prime}}]\leqslant c
.
By application of Lemma
C.3
, we obtain that with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
:

\left|\frac{1}{N^{(2)}M!}\sum_{j,\pi}\sum_{m}Q_{i^{\prime}ij,\pi}\right|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{M\log(N_{R})}{N^{(2)}}}

•

Mode 3 matricization
. We treat the case of Mode 3 matricization separately, due to its row-stochastic structure.

1.

|\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r^{\prime})|\leqslant 1
,
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r^{\prime})]=-\mathcal{D}_{ijr}\mathcal{D}_{ijr^{\prime}}
and
\text{Var}[\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r^{\prime})]\leqslant f_{r}f_{r^{\prime}}
. By Lemma
C.1
, if
f_{r}\geqslant\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{MN^{(1)}N^{(2)}}}
, with high probability,

\left|\frac{1}{MN^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\sum_{ijm}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r^{\prime})-\mathbb{E}[\star]\right|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{f_{r^{\prime}}f_{r}\log(N_{R})}{MN^{(1)}N^{(2)}}}

2.

For
m\neq m^{\prime}
, consider
\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m-1)}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m)}(r^{\prime})
such that

|\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m-1)}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m)}(r^{\prime})|\leqslant 1
, and
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m-1)}(r)X_{ij(2m)}(r^{\prime})]=0
and
\text{Var}[\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m-1)}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m)}(r^{\prime})]\leqslant\mathcal{D}_{ijr}\mathcal{D}_{ijr^{\prime}}\leqslant f_{r}f_{r^{\prime}}
. Let
\tilde{M}=\lfloor M/2\rfloor
. Applying Lemma
C.3
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{N^{(1,2)}}\frac{1}{M!}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi[M]}\sum_{i,j}\sum_{m=1}^{\tilde{M}}\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m-1)}(r)\mathcal{X}_{ij(2m)}(r^{\prime})\right|\geqslant t\right)\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{N^{(1,2)}t^{2}/2}{\tilde{M}f_{r}f_{r^{\prime}}+t/3}\right)

Let
t=C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}f_{r^{\prime}}\tilde{M}\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1,2)}}}
if
f_{r}\geqslant c^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{MN^{(1)}N^{(2)}}}
to bound the second term.

∎

Lemma D.8
.

Under the condition in Lemma
D.6
, with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\mathbf{Z}^{(1)\top}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\mathbf{Z}^{(1)\top}]\|_{F}\leqslant C^{*}\left(N^{(1)}\sqrt{\frac{N^{(2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}\right)

(49)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{Z}^{(2)}\mathbf{Z}^{(2)\top}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(2)}\mathbf{Z}^{(2)\top}]\|_{F}\leqslant C^{*}\left(N^{(2)}\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}\log N_{R}}{M}}\right)

(50)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\mathbf{Z}^{(3)\top}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\mathbf{Z}^{(3)\top}]\|_{F}\leqslant C^{*}\left(\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1,2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}\right)

(51)

Proof.

We first start with Equation (
49
). We have:

\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\mathbf{Z}^{(1)\top}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\mathbf{Z}^{(1)\top}]\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{i,i^{\prime}\in[N^{(1)}]}\left(\sum_{j,r}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathcal{Z}_{i^{\prime}jr}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\mathbf{Z}^{(1)\top}]\right)^{2}.

By Lemmas
D.6
and
D.7
, with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\mathbf{Z}^{(1)\top}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\mathbf{Z}^{(1)\top}]\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}]}\frac{N^{(2)}\log N_{R}}{M}+\sum_{i\neq i^{\prime}}\frac{N^{(2)}\log N_{R}}{M}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(N^{(1)}\right)^{2}\frac{N^{(2)}\log N_{R}}{M}.

Equation (
50
) is obtained in a similar fashion, due to the symmetry in the roles of
\mathbf{Z}^{(2)}
and
\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}.

Finally, for Equation (
51
), by Lemmas
D.6
and
D.7
:

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\mathbf{Z}^{(3)\top}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\mathbf{Z}^{(3)\top}]\|_{F}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{r\in[R]}\frac{(f_{r}+f_{r}^{2})N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\log(N_{R})}{M}+\sum_{r\neq r^{\prime}}\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}f_{r}f_{r^{\prime}}\log N_{R}}{M}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\frac{\log(N_{R})}{M}

∎

Proof.

The proof of this lemma follows directly from Lemma
D.8

∎

Lemma D.9
.

Given
f_{r}\geqslant\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}
for all
r
, with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N^{(1)},N^{(2)}}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathbf{W}^{(3)}_{k,(ij)}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}\text{ for all }r\in[R],k\in[K^{(3)}]

Proof.

Note

\sum_{ij}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathbf{W}^{(3)}_{k,(ij)}=\frac{1}{N^{(1,2)}M}\sum_{ijm}N^{(1,2)}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathbf{W}^{(3)}_{k,(ij)}

We have
|N^{(1,2)}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathbf{W}^{(3)}_{k,(ij)}|\leqslant N^{(1,2)}
,
\mathbb{E}[N^{(1,2)}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathbf{W}^{(3)}_{k,(ij)}]=0
and
\text{Var}[N^{(1,2)}\mathcal{X}_{ijm}(r)\mathbf{W}^{(3)}_{k,(ij)}]\leqslant(N^{(1,2)})^{2}D_{ijr}\leqslant(N^{(1,2)})^{2}f_{r}
. Applying Berstein’s inequality again, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\sum_{ij}Z_{ijr}\mathbf{W}^{(3)}_{k,ij}\right|\geqslant t\right)\leqslant 2\exp\left(-\frac{Mt^{2}}{N^{(1,2)}f_{r}+t/3}\right)

Let
t=C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}f_{r}\log(N_{R})}{M}}
if
f_{r}\geqslant\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}
.
∎

Appendix E
Analysis of the SVD Procedure

Let
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
be matrix as defined in the standard pLSI model provided in Equation (
1
), and denote its SVD as
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{{\Xi}}\mathbf{{\Lambda}}\mathbf{\mathbf{B}}^{\top}
, with
\mathbf{{\Xi}}^{\top}\mathbf{{\Xi}}=\mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{I}_{K}
and
\mathbf{{\Lambda}}=\text{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots\lambda_{K})
. Let
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
be the matrix defined as
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}=\mathbf{W}\mathbf{B}{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{-1}
.

Lemma E.1

(Adaptation of Lemma D.2 in
[
28
]
)
.

Suppose the Assumption
2.1
is satisfied. The following properties hold true for
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}
:

1.

The matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}=\mathbf{W}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
is invertible, unique and such that
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}=(\mathbf{\Xi}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}
.

2.

\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{\top}=(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}
, and thus the singular values of
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}
are the inverses of the singular values of
\mathbf{A}
.

3.

\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\star 1},\dots\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\star K}
are right eigenvectors of
\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
associated with eigenvalues
\lambda^{2}_{k}
(these eigenvectors are not necessarily orthogonal with each other)

4.

Finally, we have
\mathbf{\mathbf{\Xi}}=\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}
, and
\|\mathbf{\mathbf{\Xi}}_{j\star}\|_{2}\leqslant\|\mathbf{A}_{j\star}\|_{2}\sigma_{K}(\mathbf{A})^{-1}
.

Proof.

The proof for the previous lemma is essentially a readaptation of Lemma D.2 in
[
28
]
, which we provide here to make this manuscript self-contained, but we emphasize that the original proof in a more general setting is in
[
28
]
.

1.

By definition:

\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{\mathbf{\Xi}}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{B}^{\top},

so we write:

\mathbf{\Xi}=\mathbf{D}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{-1}=\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{W}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{-1})=\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}.\qquad\eqref{eq: prob v in bernstein permute}

Since:

\mathbf{\Xi}^{\top}\mathbf{\Xi}=\mathbf{I}_{K}=\mathbf{\Xi}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}=(\mathbf{\Xi}^{\top}\mathbf{A})\tilde{\mathbf{V}},

we deduce that
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}
is unique and non singular, and that it is the inverse of

\mathbf{\Xi}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
.

2.

In this case, plugging in
\mathbf{\Xi}=\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}
in
\mathbf{\Xi}^{\top}\mathbf{\Xi}=\mathbf{I}_{K}
, we obtain:

\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}=I_{K}

Multiplying left and right by
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{\top}
respectively:

(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{\top})\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{\top})=\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{\top}\implies\tilde{\mathbf{V}}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{\top}=(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})^{-1}.

3.

Let
\lambda_{k}
denote the singular values of
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{W}
. Then, for any column
\mathbf{\xi}_{k}
of the matrix
\mathbf{\Xi}
, by definition:

\mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}^{T}\xi_{k}=\lambda_{k}^{2}\mathbf{\xi}_{k}

where by (
31
),
\mathbf{\xi}_{k}=\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\cdot k}.

Therefore:

\mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}^{T}\xi_{k}=\mathbf{A}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{T}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\cdot k}=\lambda_{k}^{2}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\cdot k}

\implies\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{T}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\cdot k}=\lambda_{k}^{2}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\cdot k}

\implies\mathbf{W}^{T}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\cdot k}=\lambda_{k}^{2}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\cdot k}

where the last line follows from the fact that
\mathbf{A}^{T}\mathbf{A}
is assumed to be non-singular and invertible.

4.

By (
31
),
\mathbf{\Xi}=\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}
. The inequality follows by definition of the operator norm of
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}
, and by application of point 2 in which we noted that the singular values of
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}
are the inverses of the singular values of
\mathbf{A}
.

∎

E.1
Properties of the SVD

Proposition E.1

(Lemma 5.1 in
[
33
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a symmetric matrix with rank
K
, and
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be any symmetric matrix. Let
\hat{\mathbf{U}},\mathbf{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times K}
be
K
leading left singular vector matrices of
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
and
\mathbf{Q}
respectively. Then there exists a
K\times K
orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{O}
such that

\|\hat{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{O}-\mathbf{U}\|_{F}\leqslant\frac{2\sqrt{2K}\|\hat{\mathbf{Q}}-\mathbf{Q}\|_{op}}{\lambda_{K}(\mathbf{Q})}

Lemma E.2

(Lemma F.1 of
[
28
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}
and
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
be
n\times n
symmetric matrices with
\text{rank}(\mathbf{Q})=K
, and assume
\mathbf{Q}
is positive semi-definite. For
1\leq k\leq K
, let
\lambda_{k}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{k}
be the
k^{\text{th}}
largest eigenvalues of
\mathbf{Q}
and
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
respectively, and let
\mathbf{u}_{k}
and
\mathbf{\hat{u}}_{k}
be the
k^{\text{th}}
eigenvectors of
\mathbf{Q}
and
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
. Fix
1\leq s\leq k\leq K
. For some
c\in(0,1)
, suppose that:

\min(\lambda_{s-1}-\lambda_{s},\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{k+1},\min_{l\in[K]}|\lambda_{l}|)\geq c\|\mathbf{Q}\|_{2},\quad\|\hat{\mathbf{Q}}-\mathbf{Q}\|_{2}\leq(c/3)\|\mathbf{Q}\|_{2},

where by convention, if
s=1
, we take
\lambda_{s-1}-\lambda_{s}=\infty
.
Write
\mathbf{U}=[\mathbf{u}_{s},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{k}],\hat{\mathbf{U}}=[\mathbf{\hat{u}}_{s},\dots,\mathbf{\hat{u}}_{k}]
and
\mathbf{U}^{*}=[\mathbf{u}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{K}]
. There exists an orthonormal matrix
\mathbf{O}
such that for all
1\leq j\leq n
,

\displaystyle\|(\hat{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{O}-\mathbf{U})_{j*}\|_{2}\leq\frac{5}{c\|\mathbf{Q}\|_{2}}(\|\hat{\mathbf{Q}}-\mathbf{Q}\|_{2}\|\mathbf{U}^{*}_{j*}\|_{2}+\sqrt{K}\|(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}-\mathbf{Q})_{j*}\|_{2})

(52)

E.2
Accuracy of the HOSVD decomposition

Given the mode-
a
matricization
\mathbf{D}^{(a)}=\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathcal{D})\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
for arbitrary dimensions
n_{1}\in\{N^{(1)},N^{(2)},R\}
and
n_{2}\in\{N^{(1)}R,N^{(2)}R,N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\}
, define the matrix
\mathbf{L}^{(a)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{1}}
and the scalar
\alpha
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}^{(a)}:=\begin{cases}0_{N^{(1)}\times N^{(1)}}&\text{ if }a=1\\
0_{N^{(2)}\times N^{(2)}}&\text{ if }a=2\\
\frac{1}{M}\text{diag}(\mathbf{Y}^{(3)}\mathbf{1}_{N^{(1,2)}})&\text{ if }a=3\end{cases}\quad\quad\alpha^{(a)}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }a\in\{1,2\}\\
\frac{1}{M}&\text{ if }a=3\end{cases}

Next, for each mode of matricization, we define a pair of
(\mathbf{Q}^{(a)},\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{(a)})
to adapt Proposition
E.1
to our tensor setting. Define

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}^{(a)}=(1-\alpha^{(a)})\mathbf{D}^{(a)}(\mathbf{D}^{(a)})^{\top}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{(a)}=\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}(\mathbf{Y}^{(a)})^{\top}-\mathbf{L}^{(a)}

(53)

Recall that the
\mathbf{\Xi}_{\star k}
is the
k
-th left singular vector of
\mathbf{D}
, and
\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{\star k}
is the
k
-th left singular vector of the observed frequency matrix
Y
. Equivalently,
\mathbf{\Xi}_{\star k}
and
\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{\star k}
are the
k
-th eigenvectors of
\mathbf{Q}
and
\hat{\mathbf{Q}}
, respectively.

To apply Proposition
E.1
, we need to study in particular the difference
\|\mathbf{Q}^{(a)}-\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{(a)}\|_{op}
. Since
\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}=\mathbf{D}^{(a)}-\mathbf{Z}^{(a)}
, we re-write the difference as:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{(a)}-\mathbf{Q}^{(a)}=

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}(\mathbf{Y}^{(a)})^{\top}-\mathbf{L}^{(a)}-(1-\alpha^{(a)})\mathbf{D}^{(a)}(\mathbf{D}^{(a)})^{\top}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathbf{D}^{(a)}+\mathbf{Z}^{(a)})(\mathbf{D}^{(a)}+\mathbf{Z}^{(a)})^{\top}-\mathbf{L}^{(a)}-(1-\alpha^{(a)})\mathbf{D}^{(a)}(\mathbf{D}^{(a)})^{\top}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{(a)}\mathbf{Z}^{(a)\top}+\mathbf{Z}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}^{(a)\top}+\mathbf{Z}^{(a)}\mathbf{Z}^{(a)\top}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(a)}\mathbf{Z}^{(a)\top}]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(a)}\mathbf{Z}^{(a)\top}]-\mathbf{L}^{(a)}+\alpha\mathbf{D}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}^{(a)^{\top}}

(54)

Lemma E.3
.

Under the Assumption
2.1
, if
M\geqslant\log N_{R}/c_{0}
for some constant
c_{0}>0
, then with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(1)}\mathbf{O}^{(1)}-\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)}\|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}

(55)

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(2)}\mathbf{O}^{(2)}-\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}\|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}

(56)

If we further assume
f_{r}\geqslant\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(3)}\mathbf{O}^{(3)}-\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}\|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1,2)}M}}

(57)

Proof.

The proof idea is to control data error
\|\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{(a)}-\mathbf{Q}^{(a)}\|
and apply the results in Proposition
E.1
.

•

Mode 1 & 2 matricization:
Since the analyses of mode 1 and 2 are identical, we only provide a detailed analysis of mode 1 here. Note that
\alpha=0
and
\mathbf{L}=0
in mode 1 and mode 2 matricization. Therefore, we re-write
(
54
) as

\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{Q}^{(1)}=(\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}-\mathbf{D}^{(1)})\mathbf{Y}^{(1)\top}+\mathbf{D}^{(1)}(\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}-\mathbf{D}^{(1)})^{\top}

By Lemma
D.4
, with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
:

\displaystyle\|\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{Q}^{{(1)}}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\|\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}\|_{op}+\|\mathbf{D}^{(1)}\|_{op}\right)\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\|_{op}\leqslant\left(\|\mathbf{D}^{(1)}\|_{op}+\|\mathbf{D}^{(1)}\|_{op}+\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\|_{op}\right)\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{N^{(1,2)}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1,2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}=C^{*}N^{(1,2)}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}

Therefore, using proposition
E.1
, under assumption
2.1
, we then obtain the results in (
55
) and (
56
).

•

Mode-3 matricization:
We use the decomposition explicited in (
54
) to get an upper bound of
\|\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{(3)}-\mathbf{Q}^{(3)}\|
. With probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
, the following holds true:

1.

Analysis of
\mathbf{D}^{(3)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(3)})^{\top}+\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}(\mathbf{D}^{(3)})^{\top}
: We have:

\mathbf{D}^{(3)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(3)})^{\top}=\sum_{k}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{\star k}(\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}(\mathbf{W}^{(3)}_{k\star})^{\top})^{\top}.

Using Lemma
D.9
and the fact that
\sum_{k}\|\mathbf{A}_{\star k}\|_{2}\leqslant\sum_{k}\|\mathbf{A}_{\star k}\|_{1}=K^{(3)}
and
\sum_{r}f_{r}\leqslant K^{(3)}

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{D}^{(3)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(3)})^{\top}+\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}(\mathbf{D}^{(3)})^{\top}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\|\mathbf{D}^{(3)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(3)})^{\top}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\sum_{k}\|\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{\star k}\|_{2}\|\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}(\mathbf{W}^{(3)}_{k\star})^{\top}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{k}\|\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{\star k}\|_{2}\sqrt{\sum_{r}(\sum_{ij}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathbf{W}^{(3)}_{k,ij})^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\log(N_{R})}{M}}

2.

Analysis of
\|\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(3)})^{\top}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(3)})^{\top}]\|_{op}
: We use the upper bound given by Lemma
D.8
:

\|\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(3)})^{\top}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(3)})^{\top}]\|\leqslant C^{*}\left(\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1,2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}\right)

3.

Analysis of
\|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(3)})^{\top}]-\mathbf{L}^{(3)}+\frac{1}{M}\mathbf{D}^{(3)}(\mathbf{D}^{(3)})^{\top}\|
: We use Lemmas
D.1
and
D.5
:

\|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}(\mathbf{Z}^{(3)})^{\top}]-\mathbf{L}^{(3)}+\frac{1}{M}\mathbf{D}^{(3)}(\mathbf{D}^{(3)})^{\top}\|=\max_{r}\frac{1}{M}|\sum_{ij}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}|\leqslant\max_{r}C^{*}\left(\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}N^{(1,2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}\right)

Using the fact that
f_{r}\leqslant K^{(3)}
, we have

\|\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{(3)}-\mathbf{Q}^{(3)}\|\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1,2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}.

Applying proposition
E.1
, we immediately obtain (
57
)

∎

E.3
Accuracy of the HOOI procedure

For the purpose of keeping this appendix clear and (almost) self-contained, we summarize the HOOI procedure as follows.
Let
\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{0}=\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{\text{hosvd}}
. Calculate and repeat until convergence:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\textbf{SVD}_{K_{1}}\left(\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t-1}^{(2)\top}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t-1}^{(3)\top}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\textbf{SVD}_{K_{2}}\left(\mathbf{Y}^{(2)}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t-1}^{(1)\top}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t-1}^{(3)\top}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\textbf{SVD}_{K_{3}}\left(\mathbf{Y}^{(3)}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t-1}^{(1)\top}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t-1}^{(2)\top}\right)\right)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\leftarrow t+1

Lemma E.4
.

Define
L_{t}:=\max_{a=1,2,3}\left\|\sin\Theta(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(a)}_{t},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)})\right\|,t=0,1,2,\dots
. Whenever

\lambda_{K}(\mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}^{\top})\geqslant c^{*}N^{(1,2)},

after at most
t_{\max}=C^{*}\log\left(\frac{N^{(1,2)}}{\lambda_{K}(\mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}^{\top})}\vee 1\right)
iterations in algorithm, the following upper bounds hold with high probability,

\displaystyle\max_{a\in[3]}\|\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(a)}_{\text{hooi}}\mathbf{O}^{(a)}-\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}\|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}

Proof.

Define

\mathbf{Z}_{t}^{(1)}:=\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\left(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(2)\top}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(3)\top}_{t}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(1)}\times(K^{(2)}K^{(3)})},

(58)

and
\mathbf{Z}_{t}^{(2)}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{t}^{(3)}
