in a similar fashion:

\mathbf{Z}_{t}^{(2)}:=\mathbf{Z}^{(2)}\left(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(1)\top}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(3)\top}_{t}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(2)}\times(K^{(1)}K^{(3)})},

\mathbf{Z}_{t}^{(3)}:=\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\left(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(1)\top}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(2)\top}_{t}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times(K^{(1)}K^{(2)})}.

We follow the proof of Theorem 1 and inequality (31) in
[
63
]
. Similar to
[
63
]
, we define:

\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}_{t}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\left(\mathcal{Y}\times_{2}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(2)}_{t}\times_{3}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(3)}_{t}\right)=\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(2)\top}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(3)\top}_{t}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(1)}\times(K^{(2)}K^{(3)})}

\mathbf{D}^{(1)}_{t}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\left(\mathcal{D}\times_{2}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(2)}_{t}\times_{3}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(3)}_{t}\right)=\mathbf{D}^{(1)}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(2)\top}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(3)\top}_{t}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{(1)}\times(K^{(2)}K^{(3)})}.

By Wedin’s
\sin\Theta
theorem
[
56
]
, we know that:

\|\sin\Theta(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(t+1)},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)})\|\leq\frac{\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}^{(1)}\|_{op}}{\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{D}_{t}^{(1)})}.

(59)

We thus need to bound the numerator and denominator in the previous equation.

Bound on
\|\mathbf{Z}_{t+1}^{(1)}\|_{op}
.

We have, by definition of
\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}_{t+1}
(Equation (
58
)):

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}_{t+1}\|_{op}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\cdot(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(2)}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(3)}_{t})\|_{op}

(60)

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\|_{op}\|\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(2)}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(3)}_{t}\|_{op}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\|_{op}\|(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(2)}_{t}-\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)})\otimes\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(3)}_{t}+\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}\otimes(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(3)}_{t}-\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)})+\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\|_{op}\left(2L_{t}+1\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\|_{op}(1+L_{t}).

By the proof of Equation (31) in
[
63
]
, we have:

\begin{split}\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}_{t+1}\|_{op}&\leq\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)})\|_{op}+\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\cdot(P_{\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}_{\perp}}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(2)}\otimes P_{\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(3)})\|_{op}\\
&+\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\cdot(P_{\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(2)}\otimes P_{\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{\perp}}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(3)})\|_{op}+\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\cdot(P_{\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}_{\perp}}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(2)}\otimes P_{\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{\perp}}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(3)})\|_{op}\\
&\leq\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)})\|_{op}+\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\cdot(P_{\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}_{\perp}}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(2)}\otimes P_{\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(3)})\|_{op}\\
&+\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\cdot(P_{\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(2)}\otimes P_{\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{\perp}}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(3)})\|_{op}+\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\cdot(P_{\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}_{\perp}}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(2)}\otimes P_{\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{\perp}}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t}^{(3)})\|_{op}\\
\end{split}

(61)

Bound on
\sigma_{r}(\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(t)})
.

To this end, by the same arguments as the proof of Equation 30 in
[
63
]
, we obtain:

\sigma_{K^{(1)}}(\mathbf{D}_{t}^{(1)})\geq\sigma_{K^{(1)}}(\mathbf{D}^{(1)})\cdot(1-L_{t}^{2}).

(62)

Therefore, combining Equations
60
and
62
, equation
59
becomes:

L_{t+1}\leq\frac{2\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\|_{op}(1+L_{t})}{\sigma_{K}(\mathbf{D}^{(1)})(1-L_{t}^{2})}=\frac{2\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\|}{\sigma_{K}(\mathbf{D}^{(1)})(1-L_{t})}.

Using inequality (32) in
[
63
]
and
L_{0}\leqslant 1/2
from HOSVD, we apply Lemmas
B.3
and
D.4
with sintheta theorem
By induction, we can sequentially prove that for all
t\geqslant 0
,

L_{t+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{w.h.p}}}{{\lesssim}}(1+L_{t})\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}\leqslant 1/2

This then yields, with high probability, let
\epsilon=\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}

\displaystyle L_{t+1}-\epsilon\leqslant 4\epsilon(L_{t}-\epsilon)\text{ as }\epsilon\leqslant 1/2

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\quad

\displaystyle L_{t_{\max}}-\epsilon\leqslant(4\epsilon)^{t_{\max}}(L_{0}-\epsilon)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\quad

\displaystyle L_{t_{\max}}\leqslant\epsilon+\frac{L_{0}}{2^{t_{\max}}}

From the results in HOSVD, we have with high probability,

\displaystyle|L_{0}|\leqslant C^{*}\epsilon

Thus,

L_{t_{\max}}\leqslant\epsilon+\cdot\frac{C^{*}}{2^{t_{\max}}}\cdot\frac{N^{(1,2)}\sqrt{\log N_{R}/M}}{\lambda_{K}(\mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}^{\top})}.

Let
t_{\max}\geqslant C^{*}\left(\log(\frac{N^{(1,2)}}{\lambda_{K}(\mathbf{D}\mathbf{D}^{\top})}\vee 1)\right)
. Therefore we have the following upper bound for
\ell_{2}
distance for
\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{t_{\max}}
,

\max_{a\in[3]}\|\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(a)}_{\text{hooi}}\mathbf{O}^{(a)}-\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}\|\leqslant L_{t_{\max}}\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}

∎

E.4
Row-wise HOSVD bound

Lemma E.5
.

Suppose the assumption
2.1
is satisfied and
M\geqslant\log N_{R}/c_{0}
for some constant
c_{0}>0
, then with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
, for every
i\in[N^{(1)}]
,
j\in[N^{2}]
, there exist orthogonal matrices
\mathbf{O}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{O}_{K^{(1)},K^{(1)}}
,
\mathbf{O}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{O}_{K^{(2)},K^{(2)}}

\displaystyle E_{1}:=

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(1)}\mathbf{O}^{(1)}-\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)})_{i\star}\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}M}}

(
E1
)

\displaystyle E_{2}:=

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(2)}\mathbf{O}^{(2)}-\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)})_{j\star}\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(2)}M}}

(
E2
)

Furthermore, if the assumption
2.3
is further satisfied, and
f_{r}\geqslant c^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}
for all
r\in[R]
, we have probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
, there exists an orthogonal matrices
\mathbf{O}^{(3)}=\text{diag}(o,\mathbf{O}^{*})
where
o\in\{\pm 1\}
and orthonormal matrix
\mathbf{O}^{*}\in\mathbb{O}_{K-1,K-1}
,

\displaystyle E_{3}(r):=\|(\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(3)}\mathbf{O}^{(3)}-\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)})_{r\star}\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}\text{ for all }r\in[R]

(
E3
)

Proof.

To obtain the bound above with high probability, we use Lemma
E.2
and need to study
\|\mathbf{U}^{*(a)}_{j\star}\|
and
\|\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{(a)}_{j\star}-\mathbf{Q}^{(a)}_{j\star}\|
for each
a
.
Given the results from Section
E.2
, we have with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
,

\displaystyle\max_{a\in[3]}\|\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{(a)}-\mathbf{Q}^{(a)}\|\lesssim N^{(1,2)}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}\leqslant c^{*}(1-\alpha)N^{(1,2)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemma \ref{lemma: singular value D}}}}{{\leqslant}}\lambda_{K}(\mathbf{Q}^{(a)})

(63)

•

Mode 1 & 2 matricization:
We here provide the analysis of mode 1, and that of mode 2 alike. Let
s=1
and
k=K
in both mode 1 and mode 2. From the Lemma
B.3
and (
63
), we verify the conditions in Lemma
E.2
with
\hat{\mathbf{U}}^{(1)}=\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(1)}
, and
\mathbf{U}^{(1)}=\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)}
.

1.

Let
\hat{\mathbf{U}}^{(1)}=\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(1)}
,
\mathbf{U}^{*(1)}=\mathbf{U}^{(1)}=\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)}
. We have

\|\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)}_{i\star}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\|\mathbf{A}^{(1)}_{i\star}\|_{2}^{2}\sigma_{K}(\mathbf{A}^{(1)})^{-2}\leqslant\|\mathbf{A}^{(1)}_{i\star}\|_{1}\sigma_{K}(\mathbf{A}^{(1)})^{-2}\leqslant 1/N^{(1)}

2.

For
\|(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{Q}^{(1)})_{i\star}\|
, we have

\displaystyle\|(\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{Q}^{(1)})_{i\star}\|=

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}(\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}-\mathbf{D}^{(1)})_{i\star}+(\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}-\mathbf{D}^{(1)})\mathbf{D}^{(1)}_{i\star}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}\|\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}_{i\star}\|+\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}\|\|\mathbf{D}^{(1)}_{i\star}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\sqrt{N^{(1,2)}}\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}_{i\star}\|+\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1,2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}\|\mathbf{D}^{(1)}_{i\star}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle N^{(2)}\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}\log N_{R}}{M}}

where the last second inequality is given by Wely’s inequality and Lemmas
B.3
,
D.4
and the last inequality is from the results of Lemma
D.4
and the fact that

\|\mathbf{D}^{(1)}_{i\star}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{jr}P(r|i,j)^{2}\leqslant N^{(2)}

We then use Lemma
E.2
with two bounds above, with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
,

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(1)}\mathbf{O}^{(1)}-\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)})_{i\star}\|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{(1)}}}+N^{(2)}\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}\log N_{R}}{M}}\cdot\frac{1}{N^{(1,2)}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}M}}

•

Mode 3 matricization:
We divide the eigenvectors of
\mathbf{Q}^{(3)}
into two groups. The first group only includes the leading eigenvector
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{\star 1}
as
s=1,k=1
, while the second group contains the left
K-1
eigenvectors as
s=2
and
k=K^{(3)}
in lemma
E.2
. We need to figure out the lower bound of the gap of the first two eigenvalues of
\mathbf{Q}^{(3)}
. Let
n=N^{(1)}N^{(2)}
. Define

\mathbf{\Theta}=\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A}
, where
\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{W}^{(3)}
and
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
.
Note that
\mathbf{W}=\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathcal{G})(\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(2)})^{\top}
, we have

\sigma_{K}(\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\top})\geqslant\frac{1}{N^{(1)}}\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{A}^{(1)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(1)})\cdot\frac{1}{N^{(2)}}\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{A}^{(2)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(2)})\cdot\sigma_{K}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{G})^{\top}\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{G}))\geqslant c^{*}K^{(1)}K^{(2)}

On the other side, by lemma
B.2
, we have

\sigma_{1}(\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\top})\leqslant K^{(1)}K^{(2)}

By assumptions
2.1
and
2.3
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Theta}_{k_{1}k_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{n}(\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\top})_{k_{1}k}\cdot(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})_{kk_{2}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\min_{s,s^{\prime}}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})_{s,s^{\prime}}\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{n}(\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\top})_{k_{1}k}

\displaystyle\geqslant\min_{s,s^{\prime}}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})_{s,s^{\prime}}\cdot\frac{1}{n}(\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\top})_{k_{1}k_{1}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\min_{s,s^{\prime}\in[K^{(3)}]}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})_{s,s^{\prime}}\cdot\sigma_{K}(\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\top})\geqslant c^{*}

(64)

Also,

\|\mathbf{\Theta}\|\leqslant\|\mathbf{A}\|_{op}^{2}\|\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\|_{op}\leqslant\prod_{i=1}^{3}K^{(i)}

Applying lemma
B.4
, we have

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{\Theta})-\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{\Theta})\geqslant c^{*}

Note that

\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{Q}^{(3)})=\left(1-\frac{1}{M}\right)\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\top})=\left(1-\frac{1}{M}\right)\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{A})

so we have

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{Q}^{(3)})-\lambda_{2}(\mathbf{Q}^{(3)})\geqslant c^{*}N^{(1,2)}

and thus

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{Q}^{(3)})\geqslant c^{*}N^{(1,2)}+\max_{k\in[K^{(3)}]}\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{Q}^{(3)})

By lemma
B.3
and
\mathbf{Q}^{(3)}=\left(1-\frac{1}{M}\right)\mathbf{D}^{(3)}\mathbf{D}^{(3)\top}
, we have

c^{*}N^{(1,2)}\leqslant\lambda_{k}(\mathbf{Q}^{(3)})\leqslant N^{(1,2)}\text{ for all }k\in[K^{(3)}]

Plugging these values into lemma
E.2
with matrix
\mathbf{O}^{(3)}=\text{diag}(o,\mathbf{O}^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
where
o\in\{\pm 1\}
and orthonormal matrix
\mathbf{O}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{(K-1)\times(K-1)}
, we achieve the result with high probability. Note that we set
o\in\{\pm 1\}
because the singular vector is determined up to a sign flip.

We then satisfy the conditions in Lemma
E.2
, and need to study the terms left in (
52
).

1.

Let
\mathbf{U}^{*(3)}=\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}
, we have

\|\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{r\star}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\|\mathbf{A}_{r\star}\|_{2}^{2}\sigma_{K}(\mathbf{A})^{-2}\leqslant\|\mathbf{A}_{r\star}\|_{1}\sigma_{K}(\mathbf{A})^{-2}\leqslant f_{r}/K

2.

The upper bound
\|(\mathbf{Q}^{(3)}-\hat{\mathbf{Q}}^{(3)})_{r\star}\|_{2}\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\log(N_{R})}{M}}
. w.h.p. is given by

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{D}^{(3)}\mathbf{Z}^{(3)\top}+\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\mathbf{D}^{(3)\top})_{r\star}\|_{2}\leqslant

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\mathbf{A}_{rk}\|\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\mathbf{W}_{k\star}\|+\sum_{k}\left|\sum_{ij}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathbf{W}_{k,ij}\right|\|\mathbf{A}_{\star k}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemma \ref{lemma: dz}}}}{{\lesssim}}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}N^{(1,2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\mathbf{Z}^{(3)\top}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\mathbf{Z}^{(3)\top}]\right)_{r\star}\right\|\leqslant

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sum_{r^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{ij}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr^{\prime}}-\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Z}_{ijr}\mathcal{Z}_{ijr^{\prime}}]\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemmas \ref{lemma: zz},\ref{lemma: zijr square}}}}{{\lesssim}}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}N^{(1,2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}\quad\text{ with }f_{r}\leqslant K

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M}\|\text{diag}(\mathbf{Z}^{(3)}\mathbf{1}_{n})_{r\star}\|_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemma \ref{lemma: sumij zij}}}}{{\lesssim}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M}\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}N^{(1,2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}

Combined the two points above, we apply Lemma
E.2
for two groups of eigenvectors to get the bound in (

⁢
E
3

)

∎

Appendix F
Analysis of the Vextex Hunting Algorithm

In our analysis, we will make the following assumption on the efficiency the vertex hunting algorithm (similar to Assumption 4 in
[
52
]
).

Assumption F.1
.

[Efficiency of the VH algorithm]
Given
K
and a set of data points
\{\mathbf{y}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
\mathbf{y}_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{ik}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}
satisfying the ideal simplex condition
2.1
, the vertex hunting function
\mathcal{V}
that outputs estimates
\mathcal{V}(\{\mathbf{y}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})=\{\hat{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{k}\}
for the vertices of the point cloud
\{\mathbf{y}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
satisfies, for some absolute constant
C>0
:

\displaystyle\max_{k\in[K]}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{\pi(k)}-\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}\|\leqslant C^{*}\max_{i\in[n]}\|\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{\hat{y}}_{i}\|

(65)

subject to a label permutation
\pi
.

This assumption is commonly used to control vertex estimation error by requiring to have points near each vertex
[
28
,
52
]
. The Successive Projection (SP) method
[
4
]
has been demonstrated to satisfy this assumption (see Section 2.2 in
[
28
]
). Another suitable vertex hunting (VH) algorithm, Sketched Vertex Search (SVS)
[
27
]
, is more robust to noise in the simplex under mild conditions, although it is computationally slower. Additionally, the Archetype Analysis (AA) vertex hunting procedure can achieve the bound in (
65
) under conditions that are more relaxed than the ideal simplex condition described in
2.1
(see Theorem F.1 in
[
25
]
).

Remark that as long as the bound in (
65
) is satisfied with high probability, our main results remain valid even under these relaxed conditions, which do not require the anchor conditions outlined in Assumption
2.2

. The anchor conditions are primarily used to satisfy the ideal simplex condition in Assumption
F.1
. We retain these anchor conditions to help the readers better understand the concepts and to keep the proofs straightforward and concise.

Next, we demonstrate how the anchor conditions ensure that the simplex formed by the rows of
S
in step 2 of the Post-SVD process satisfies the ideal simplex condition in Assumption
F.1
. Recall that in oracle procedure, we use
\mathbf{V}
to denote the matrix of vertices returned by the VH algorithm, while
\mathbf{V}^{*}
denotes the vertices matrix used in the recovery procedure (Algorithm
2.1.2
). Define
\Omega
as the weight matrix with
\mathbf{V}
returned by the VH algorithm in the oracle procedure, and
\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}
its counterpart in the recovery procedure.

Lemma F.1

(Mode 1 & 2 matricization)
.

Given
a\in\{1,2\}
,
suppose we define the simplex

\mathbf{S}^{(a)}=\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}=\mathbf{A}^{(a)}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}

with vertex matrix
\mathbf{V}^{(a)}=\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
and weight matrix
\mathbf{\Omega}^{(a)}=\mathbf{A}^{(a)}
. Under the conditions of Lemma
E.5
, we have probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
,

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{(1)}\mathbf{O}^{(1)}-\mathbf{S}^{(1)})_{i\star}\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1)}M}}

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{(2)}\mathbf{O}^{(2)}-\mathbf{S}^{(2)})_{j\star}\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(2)}M}}

If
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}
follows (
C1
) such that
\sum_{k\in K^{(a)}}\mathbf{A}_{ik}=1
for all
i\in[N^{(a)}]
and satisfies the anchor document condition
1.1
, then the simplex
\mathbf{S}^{(a)}
is an ideal simplex.

Proof.

The SVD of
\mathbf{D}^{(a)}
in lemma
E.1
yields

\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}_{i\star}=\sum_{k=1}^{K^{(a)}}\mathbf{A}^{(a)}_{ik}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}_{k\star}

Fix
a\in\{1,2\}
, by observation (
6
), for any
i\in[N^{(a)}]
,
\sum_{k\in[K^{(a)}]}\mathbf{A}^{(a)}_{ik}=1
. Furthermore, by the anchor document assumption on the matrix
\mathbf{A}
(Assumption
1.1
), for each
k
, there exists an index
i
such that
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}_{ik}=1
and
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}_{ik^{\prime}}=0
for
k\neq k^{\prime}
. Thus, the linear equation
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}=\mathbf{A}^{(a)}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
defines an ideal simplex (Definition
2.1
), with the matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(a)}
playing the role of the weights
\mathbf{\Omega}^{(a)}
. Consequently, we run the VH algorithm on the rows of the matrix
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}
to estimate the corresponding vertices.
∎

Lemma F.2

(Mode 3 matricization)
.

Suppose the simple
\mathbf{S}^{(3)}
is given by

[\mathbf{1}_{R},\mathbf{S}^{(3)}]=\left(\text{diag}(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{\star 1})\right)^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}

with vertex matrix
\mathbf{V}^{(3)}
and weight matrix
\mathbf{\Omega}^{(3)}
such that

[\mathbf{1}_{K^{(3)}},\mathbf{V}^{(3)}]=[\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})]^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{\Omega}^{(3)}=[\text{diag}(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{\star 1})]^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}[\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})]

If we assume the same conditions in Lemma
E.5
, we have probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{O}^{*\top}\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{(3)}_{r\star}-\mathbf{S}^{(3)}_{r\star}\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{f_{r}N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}\text{ for all }r\in[R]

(66)

where
\mathbf{O}^{*}\in\mathbb{O}_{K^{(3)}-1,K^{(3)}-1}
comes from the orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{O}^{(3)}=\text{diag}(o,\mathbf{O}^{*})
.
Observed that
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
follows (
C2
) such that
\sum_{r\in[R]}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{rk}=1
, if
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
further satisfies the anchor word condition
1.2
, then the point cloud of the rows of
\mathbf{S}^{(3)}
forms an ideal simplex.

Proof.

For notation simplicity, let
\mathbf{O}=\mathbf{O}^{(3)}=\text{diag}(o,\mathbf{O}^{*})
where
o\in\{\pm 1\}
. Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
,
\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{W}^{(3)}
,
\mathbf{\Xi}=\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}
, etc. Do SCORE normalization
2.3
on
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}
, we get

[\mathbf{1}_{R},\mathbf{S}]=\left(\text{diag}(\mathbf{\Xi}_{\star 1})\right)^{-1}\mathbf{\Xi}=\left(\text{diag}(\mathbf{\Xi}_{\star 1})\right)^{-1}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}

where
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times(K-1)}
works data point cloud in VH algorithm.

To find an ideal simplex for VH algorithm, the equation above is not enough. Consider

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}^{*}=[\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\star 1})]^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}.

(67)

The entries of the first column of
\mathbf{V}^{*}
are all equal to 1. To make
\mathbf{V}^{*}
and
\mathbf{S}
well-defined, we need to prove that the entries of
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\star 1}
and
\mathbf{\Xi}_{r1}
are all nonzero. We will show later that for some constant
c^{*},C^{*}>0
such that

\displaystyle\frac{c^{*}}{\sqrt{K^{(3)}}}\leqslant\min_{k\in[K^{(3)}]}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{k1}^{(3)}\leqslant\max_{k\in[K^{(3)}]}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{k1}^{(3)}\leqslant\frac{C^{*}}{\sqrt{K^{(3)}}}

(68)

\displaystyle\frac{c^{*}f_{r}}{\sqrt{K^{(3)}}}\leqslant\mathbf{\Xi}_{r1}^{(3)}\leqslant\frac{C^{*}f_{r}}{\sqrt{K^{(3)}}}\text{ for all }r\in[R]

(69)

Therefore, all entries in
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\star 1}
and
\mathbf{\Xi}_{\star 1}
are positive. By the definition of
\mathbf{V}^{*}
, it is of form

\mathbf{V}^{*\top}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\dots&1\\
\mathbf{V}_{1\star}&\dots&\mathbf{V}_{K\star}\end{pmatrix}

where
\mathbf{V}_{1\star},\dots,\mathbf{V}_{K\star}
are supposed to be the simplex vertices of the point cloud generated by the rows of
S
. We could rewrite

\mathbf{S}^{*}=[\mathbf{1}_{R},\mathbf{S}]=\left(\text{diag}(\mathbf{\Xi}_{\star 1})\right)^{-1}\mathbf{A}[\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\star 1})]\mathbf{V}^{*}=\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}\mathbf{V}^{*}

To prove
\mathbf{S}^{*}=\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}\mathbf{V}^{*}
(
\mathbf{S}^{*}_{r\star}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\Omega_{rk}^{*}\mathbf{V}_{k\star}^{*}
) and
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}\mathbf{V}
(
\mathbf{S}_{r\star}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbf{\Omega}_{rk}^{*}\mathbf{V}_{k\star}
) are indeed of form of ideal Simplex, we need to prove
\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}
is non negative and the rows of it sum up to 1. Remark that

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}:=\left[\text{diag}(\mathbf{\Xi}_{\star 1})\right]^{-1}\mathbf{A}[\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\star 1})]

we have

\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}_{rk}=\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{k1}\mathbf{A}_{rk}}{\mathbf{\Xi}_{r1}}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}_{rk}=1

Since the entries of the matrices
\text{diag}(\mathbf{\Xi}_{\star 1}),\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}_{\star 1})
and
\mathbf{A}
are all nonnegative, the entries of
\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}
is then also nonnegative. If
r
is an anchor word index, then
\mathbf{A}_{rk}\neq 0
but
\mathbf{A}_{rk^{\prime}}=0
for all
k^{\prime}\neq k
. Since
\sum_{k=1}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}_{rk}=1
,
\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}_{rk}
can only be equal to 1 at the anchor word index
r
. Thus the property of
\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}
guarantees that
\mathbf{S}^{*}=\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}\mathbf{V}^{*}
and
\mathbf{S}=\mathbf{\Omega}^{*}\mathbf{V}
are of form of ideal simplex. To get the bound of the error given the VH, we need to first bound the error between point cloud of
\mathbf{S}
and the data
\mathbf{\hat{S}}
.

By the definition, we have

\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\mathbf{S}_{r\star}\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{\Xi}_{r\star}/(\mathbf{\Xi}_{r1}),\quad\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\mathbf{O}^{*\top}\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{r\star}\end{pmatrix}=\mathbf{O}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r\star}/(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r1})

(70)

To make (
70
) to be well-defined, we also need
\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r1}
for all
r\in[R]
are nonzero. By (

⁢
E
3

), we know that with high probability when
f_{r}\geqslant c^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1,2)}M}}
, for all
r\in[R]
,

|o\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r1}-\mathbf{\Xi}_{r1}|\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{f_{r}\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1,2)}M}}\leqslant C^{*}f_{r}\left(\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1,2)}M}\right)^{1/4}

and by (
69
), we have

\mathbf{\Xi}_{r1}\geqslant c^{*}f_{r}>0.

We can see that
|o\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r1}-\mathbf{\Xi}_{r1}|\ll\mathbf{\Xi}_{r1}
with high probability as
N^{(1,2)}M
is sufficiently large. Thus we have
|o\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r1}|\geqslant\mathbf{\Xi}_{r1}/2
. Therefore, we have
\mathbf{\Xi}_{\star 1}
is strictly positive and thus
\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{\star 1}
is strictly positive with high probability. WLOG, we set
o=1
. By (
70
), we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{O}^{*\top}\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{r\star}-\mathbf{S}_{r\star}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r1}}\mathbf{O}^{\top}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r\star}-\frac{1}{\mathbf{\Xi}_{r1}}\mathbf{\Xi}_{r\star}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{1}{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r1}}\left(\mathbf{O}^{\top}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r\star}-\mathbf{\Xi}_{r\star}\right)-\frac{\mathbf{S}_{r\star}}{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r1}}\left(\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r\star}-\mathbf{\Xi}_{r\star}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant|\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r1}|^{-1}\left(\|\mathbf{O}\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r\star}-\mathbf{\Xi}_{r\star}\|+\|\mathbf{S}_{r\star}\|\cdot|\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}_{r1}-\mathbf{\Xi}_{r1}|\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant c^{*}\frac{1}{f_{r}}\cdot E_{3}(r)\left(1+\|\mathbf{S}_{r\star}\|_{2}\right)\text{ using the bound from \eqref{eq: E 3}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{f_{r}N^{(1,2)}M}}\left(1+\|\mathbf{S}_{r\star}\|_{2}\right)

(71)

Since each row
\mathbf{S}_{r\star}
is in the simplex, it follows that for some constant
C^{*}>0

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{S}_{r\star}\|_{2}\leqslant\max_{k\in[K]}\|\mathbf{V}_{k\star}\|_{2}\leqslant\max_{k\in[K]}\|\mathbf{V}_{k\star}^{*}\|_{2}\leqslant\|\mathbf{V}^{*}\|_{2}\leqslant C^{*}

(72)

where the last inequality comes from the results of Lemma
F.4
.

Proving inequalities (
68
) and (
69
) under the Assumption
2.1

By Assumption
2.1
, we have
\sigma_{K^{(3)}}(\mathbf{A}^{(3)})\geqslant c^{*}\sqrt{K^{(3)}}
. Then

\max_{k\in[K^{(3)}]}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{k1}\leqslant\|\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1}\|_{2}\leqslant\sigma_{1}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)})=\sigma_{K^{(3)}}(\mathbf{A}^{(3)})^{-1}\leqslant\frac{C^{*}}{\sqrt{K^{(3)}}}

Similarly, we have

\|\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1}\|_{2}\geqslant\sigma_{K^{(3)}}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)})=\sigma_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{(3)})^{-1}\geqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{K^{(3)}}}

By inequality (
64
), we have
\mathbf{\Theta}=\frac{1}{N^{(1,2)}}\mathbf{W}^{(3)}\mathbf{W}^{{(3)}\top}\mathbf{A}^{(3)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
such that each entry of
\mathbf{\Theta}
is lower bounded by a constant.
\mathbf{\Theta}
is a strictly positive matrix. By lemma
B.4
, the leading eigenvector of
\mathbf{\Theta}
, which is
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1}
by item 3 in Lemma
E.1
, is a strictly positive vector.

\min_{k\in[K^{(3)}]}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{k1}=\|\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1}\|_{2}\min_{k}\left\{\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{k1}}{\|\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1}\|_{2}}\right\}\geqslant\frac{c^{*}}{\sqrt{K^{(3)}}}

We end the proof for (
68
). Next, by the definition
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}=\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}
, we have
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{\star 1}=\sum_{k=1}^{K^{(3)}}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{\star k}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{k1}
. For any
r\in[R]
, we have

c^{*}\frac{f_{r}}{\sqrt{K^{(3)}}}\leqslant\min_{k}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{k1}f_{r}\leqslant\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{r1}\leqslant f_{r}\max_{k}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{k1}\leqslant C^{*}\frac{f_{r}}{\sqrt{K^{(3)}}}

∎

Corollary F.0.1
.

Under the conditions in Lemma
E.5
, if there exists an efficient VH algorithm such that the bound in (
65
) could be achieved with high probability, then we have probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
, subject to label permutations
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3}
,

\displaystyle\max_{k\in[K^{(1)}]}\|\mathbf{O}^{(1)\top}\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(1)}_{\pi_{1}(k)\star}-\mathbf{V}^{*(1)}_{k\star}\|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}M}}

\displaystyle\max_{k\in[K^{(2)}]}\|\mathbf{O}^{(2)\top}\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(2)}_{\pi_{2}(k)\star}-\mathbf{V}^{*(2)}_{k\star}\|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(2)}M}}

\displaystyle\max_{k\in[K^{(3)}]}\|\mathbf{O}^{(3)\top}\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(3)}_{\pi_{3}(k)\star}-\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}_{k\star}\|\lesssim\max_{r}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{f_{r}N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}

where
\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}=\mathbf{V}^{(a)}
for
a\in\{1,2\}
,
\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}=[\mathbf{1}_{K^{(3)}},\mathbf{V}^{(3)}]
, and
\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}=\mathbf{\Omega}^{(a)}
for all
a\in[3]
.

Lemma F.3
.

If
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
satisfy Assumption
2.1
and
\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}=\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
for
a\in\{1,2\}
, then we have for some constants
c^{*},C^{*}>0

\displaystyle\frac{c^{*}}{N^{(1)}}\leqslant\lambda_{K^{(1)}}(\mathbf{V}^{*(1)}\mathbf{V}^{*(1)\top})\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{*(1)}\mathbf{V}^{*(1)\top})\leqslant\frac{C^{*}}{N^{(1)}}

\displaystyle\frac{c^{*}}{N^{(2)}}\leqslant\lambda_{K^{(2)}}(\mathbf{V}^{*(2)}\mathbf{V}^{*(2)\top})\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{*(2)}\mathbf{V}^{*(2)\top})\leqslant\frac{C^{*}}{N^{(2)}}

Proof.

Applying the results from Lemma
F.1
and corollary
F.0.1
, we have

\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}=\mathbf{V}^{(a)}=\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
for
a\in\{1,2\}
. Item 2 in Lemma
E.1
yields that
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)\top}=(\mathbf{A}^{(a)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(a)})^{-1}
is non-degenerate, we have

\lambda_{K^{(a)}}(\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}\mathbf{V}^{{*(a)}\top})=\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{(a)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(a)})^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemma \ref{lemma: max singular values}}}}{{\geqslant}}\frac{1}{N^{(a)}}>0

Using Assumption
2.1
, we have the upper bound

\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}\mathbf{V}^{*{(a)}\top})\leqslant\frac{C^{*}}{N^{(a)}}

∎

Lemma F.4
.

Under the Assumptions
2.1
, let
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{*(3)}=[\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})]^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}
, for some constants
c^{*},C^{*}>0
, we have

c^{*}\leqslant\lambda_{K^{(3)}}(\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}\mathbf{V}^{*(3)\top})\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}\mathbf{V}^{*(3)\top})\leqslant C^{*}.

Proof.

As
\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}=[\mathbf{1}_{K^{(3)}},\mathbf{V}^{(3)}]=[\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})]^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}
, we could write

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}\|_{2}^{2}=

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}\mathbf{V}^{*(3)\top})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}([\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})]^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)\top}[\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})]^{-1})

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}([\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})]^{-1}[\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})]^{-1})\lambda_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)\top})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lemma \ref{lemma:v_tilde_properties}}}}{{\leqslant}}

\displaystyle\left[\min_{k}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{k1}\right]^{-2}[\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{A}^{(3)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(3)})]^{-1}\quad\text{ as }\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)\top}=(\mathbf{A}^{(3)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(3)})^{-1}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{K^{(3)}}}{c^{*}}\cdot c^{*}K^{(3)}\text{ by Assumption \ref{ass: min singular value} and inequality \eqref{eq: bound on first value of tilde V}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle c^{*}\sqrt{K^{(3)}}\leqslant C^{*}

On the other side, Lemma
B.2
and (
68
) enable

\lambda_{K^{(3)}}(\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}\mathbf{V}^{*(3)\top})\geqslant\left(\frac{1}{\max_{k}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{k1}}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{A}^{(3)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(3)})}\geqslant c^{*}

∎

Appendix G
Recovery Error for weight matrix
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{*}

Recall that
\mathbf{S}^{*}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{*}\mathbf{V}^{*}
.
To get the recovery error, the first key step is to prove
\mathbf{V}^{*}
and
\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*}
is non-degenerate.

Lemma G.1
.

Suppose we have matrix
\mathbf{V}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
is non-degenerate such that
\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{V}^{*})\geqslant\epsilon_{n}>0
. Given a permutation matrix
\Pi
and orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{O}
, if
\|\Pi\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*}\mathbf{O}-\mathbf{V}^{*}\|\leqslant\epsilon_{1}
for some
\epsilon_{n}>\epsilon_{1}>0
,
then
\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*}
is non-degenerate with
\sigma_{\min}(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*})\geqslant\epsilon_{n}-\epsilon_{1}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{V}^{*}
is non-degenerate,
\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{V}^{*})>0
.
By Wely’s inequality, we have

\sigma_{\min}(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*})=\sigma_{\min}(\Pi\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*}\mathbf{O})\geqslant\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{V}^{*})-\|\Pi\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*}\mathbf{O}-\mathbf{V}^{*}\|\geqslant\epsilon_{n}-\epsilon_{1}>0

∎

Lemma G.2
.

Suppose the VH algorithm is efficient as in Assumption
F.1
. Under the conditions in Lemma
E.5
, we have with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
, for every
i\in[N^{(1)}]
,
j\in[N^{(2)}]
and
r\in[R]

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(1)}-\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(1)}\Pi^{(1)}\|_{F}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}\log N_{R}}{M}}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(1)}_{i\star}-\Pi^{(1)\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(1)}_{i\star}\|_{1}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}\log N_{R}}{M}}

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(2)}-\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(2)}\Pi^{(2)}\|_{F}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{N^{(2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(2)}_{j\star}-\Pi^{(2)\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(2)}_{j\star}\|_{1}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{N^{(2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(3)}-\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(3)}\Pi^{(3)})_{r\star}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)

for some permutation matrices
\Pi^{(1)}
,
\Pi^{(2)}
, and
\Pi^{(3)}
which are consistent as before.

Proof.

Recall that the estimator
\mathbf{\tilde{\Omega}}^{*(a)}
is obtained by
\mathbf{\tilde{\Omega}}^{*(a)}=\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(a)}\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}
. Lemmas
F.1
and
F.2
control the error between
\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(a)}\mathbf{O}^{(a)}
and
\mathbf{S}^{*(a)}
, while an efficient VH algorithm enables then to control the discrepancy between
\Pi^{(a)}\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\mathbf{O}^{(a)}
and
\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}
with a label permutation
\Pi^{(a)}
and orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{O}^{(a)}
. Thereby,
\mathbf{\tilde{\Omega}}^{*(a)}\left(\Pi^{(a)}\right)^{-1}=\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(a)}\mathbf{O}^{(a)}\mathbf{O}^{(a)\top}\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\left(\Pi^{(a)}\right)^{-1}
.

We will prove later that for any
a\in[3]
,
\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}
is non-degenerate with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
.
Here we assume it is non-degenerate. For simplicity of notation, let
\mathbf{O}
,
\Pi
be general matrice for
\mathbf{O}^{(a)}
, and
\Pi^{(a)}
respectively. Then

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{\tilde{\Omega}}^{*(a)}-\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}\Pi^{(a)}\right\|_{F}=\left\|\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(a)}\mathbf{O}\mathbf{O}^{\top}\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}-\mathbf{S}^{*(a)}\left(\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\Pi\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|(\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(a)}\mathbf{O}-\mathbf{S}^{*(a)})\mathbf{O}^{\top}\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}+\mathbf{S}^{*(a)}(\mathbf{O}^{\top}\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\Pi-\left(\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\Pi\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left\|(\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(a)}\mathbf{O}-\mathbf{S}^{*(a)})\right\|_{F}\cdot\left\|\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\mathbf{O}^{\top}\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\Pi^{-1}-\left(\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(a)}\mathbf{O}-\mathbf{S}^{*(a)}\right\|_{F}\cdot\left\|\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\right\|+\left\|\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\right\|\left\|\left(\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\right\|\left\|\Pi\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\mathbf{O}-\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}\right\|_{F}

(73)

Similarly, we also have for any abstract row index
r
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\tilde{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{r\star}-\Pi^{(a)\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}_{r\star}\|_{2}=\|\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-\top}\mathbf{O}(\mathbf{O}^{\top}\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(a)}_{r\star})-\Pi^{\top}\left(\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}\right)^{-\top}\mathbf{S}^{*(a)}_{r\star}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{O}\left(\mathbf{O}^{\top}\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(a)}_{r\star}-\mathbf{S}^{*(a)}_{r\star}\right)+\left\{\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-\top}\mathbf{O}-\Pi^{-1}\left(\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}\right)^{-\top}\right\}\mathbf{S}^{*(a)}_{r\star}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\right\|\cdot\|\mathbf{O}^{*}\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(a)}_{r\star}-\mathbf{S}^{*(a)}_{r\star}\|_{2}+\|\mathbf{O}^{\top}\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\Pi^{-1}-\left(\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\|\cdot\|\mathbf{S}_{r\star}^{*(a)}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\right\|\|\mathbf{O}^{*}\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(a)}_{r\star}-\mathbf{S}^{*(a)}_{r\star}\|+\left\|\left(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\right\|\left\|\left(\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}\right)^{-1}\right\|\left\|\Pi\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\mathbf{O}-\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}\right\|_{F}\|\mathbf{S}_{r\star}^{*(a)}\|

(74)

•

Mode 1 & 2 matricization:
with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
, we have

1.

\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}
for
a\in\{1,2\}
is non-degenerate with
\sigma_{\min}(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{c^{*}}{N^{(a)}}}
: Since Corollary
F.0.1
yields that

\left\|\Pi^{(a)}\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}\mathbf{O}^{(a)}-\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}\right\|_{F}\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(a)}M}}\quad\text{for any }a\in\{1,2\},

and Lemma
F.3
gives
\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{V}^{*(a)})\geqslant\sqrt{\frac{c^{*}}{N^{(a)}}}
, we can use the assumption
M\geqslant\log N_{R}/c_{0}
with
c_{0}<\frac{c^{*}}{4C^{*2}}
to derive

\sqrt{\frac{c^{*}}{N^{(a)}}}-C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(a)}M}}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{c^{*}}{N^{(a)}}}>0
. Therefore,
we have the non-degeneracy of
\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(a)}
by lemma
G.1
.

2.

Lemma
F.1
can further admit that

\left\|\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(a)}\mathbf{O}-\mathbf{S}^{*(a)}\right\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{i}\left\|(\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(a)}\mathbf{O}-\mathbf{S}^{*(a)})_{i\star}\right\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}.

Then, (
73
) gives

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{\tilde{\Omega}}^{*(a)}-\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}\Pi^{(a)}\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}\sqrt{N^{(a)}}+N^{(a)}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(a)}M}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{N^{(a)}\log N_{R}}{M}}\quad\text{ for }a\in\{1,2\}

•

Mode 3 matricization:
with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
,

1.

\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(3)}
is non-degenerate with
\sigma_{\min}(\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(3)})\geqslant c^{*}/2
:
Lemma
F.4
gives
\sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{V}^{*(3)})\geqslant c^{*}
. By the assumption
f_{r}\geqslant\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1,2)}M}}
, Corollary
F.0.1
gives that
\left\|\Pi^{(3)}\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(3)}\mathbf{O}^{(3)}-\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}\right\|_{F}\lesssim\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1,2)}M}\right)^{1/4}
. As
c^{*}-C^{*}\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1,2)}M}\right)^{1/4}\geqslant c^{*}-o(1)\geqslant c^{*}/2>0
, Lemma
G.1
gives the results.

2.

As
\mathbf{S}^{*(3)}=[1_{R},\mathbf{S}^{(3)}]
, and
\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(3)}=[1_{R},\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{(3)}]
, Lemma
F.2
also yields that

\left\|(\mathbf{\hat{S}}^{*(3)}\mathbf{O}^{(3)}-\mathbf{S}^{*(3)})_{r\star}\right\|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{f_{r}N^{(1,2)}M}}.
with
\mathbf{O}^{(3)}=\text{diag}(\pm 1,\mathbf{\Omega}^{*})
.
By (
72
), we have
\|\mathbf{S}^{*(3)}_{r\star}\|_{2}\leqslant\sqrt{1+\|\mathbf{S}^{(3)}_{r\star}\|_{2}^{2}}\leqslant C^{*}
. Then, (
74
) gives

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\tilde{\Omega}}^{*(3)}_{r\star}-\Pi^{(3)\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(3)}_{r\star}\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{f_{r}N^{(1,2)}M}}+\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1,2)}M}\right)^{1/4}\lesssim\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1,2)}M}\right)^{1/4}

Note the final weight matrix
\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(a)}
is given by
\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}=\frac{\mathbf{\check{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}}{\left\|\mathbf{\check{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\right\|_{1}}
where
\mathbf{\check{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{ik}=\max\{\mathbf{\tilde{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{ik},0\}
. The weight matrix
\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}=\mathbf{\Omega}^{(a)}
for all
a\in[3]
has property
\left\|\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\right\|_{1}=1=\left\|\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\right\|_{1}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}-\Pi^{\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}-\mathbf{\check{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{1}+\|\mathbf{\check{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}-\Pi^{\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{1}

(75)

\displaystyle=\|\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{1}\cdot\left|1-\|\mathbf{\check{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{1}\right|+\|\mathbf{\check{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}-\Pi^{\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{1}\text{ since }\mathbf{\check{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}=\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|\mathbf{\check{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left|\|\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{1}-\|\mathbf{\check{\Omega}}_{i\star}^{*(a)}\|_{1}\right|+\|\mathbf{\check{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}-\Pi^{\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\Pi^{\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}_{i\star}-\mathbf{\check{\Omega}}_{i\star}^{*(a)}\|_{1}+\|\mathbf{\check{\Omega}}_{i\star}^{*(a)}-\Pi^{\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{1}\quad\text{as}\quad\|\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{1}=\|\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\|\mathbf{\check{\Omega}}_{i\star}^{*(a)}-\Pi^{\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\|\mathbf{\tilde{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}-\Pi^{\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{1}

Therefore,

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(a)}-\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}\Pi^{(a)}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{N^{(a)}}\|\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}-\Pi^{(a)\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\sqrt{K^{(a)}}\sum_{i}\|\mathbf{\tilde{\Omega}}^{*(a)}_{i\star}-\Pi^{(a)\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(a)}_{i\star}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{N^{(a)}\log N_{R}}{M}\text{ for }a\in\{1,2\}

and

\|(\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(3)}-\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(3)}\Pi^{(3)})_{r\star}\|_{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)

∎

Appendix H
Matrix A estimation error

Lemma H.1

(Mode 1 & 2 matricization)
.

Fix
a\in\{1,2\}
, we obtain
\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(a)}=\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(a)}
. Under the conditions in
G.2
, we have with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
,

\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\Pi^{(1)}\|_{1}\lesssim N^{(1)}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}},\quad\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\Pi^{(1)}\|_{F}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}\log N_{R}}{M}},

and

\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(2)}-\mathbf{A}^{(2)}\Pi^{(2)}\|_{1}\lesssim N^{(2)}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}},\quad\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(2)}-\mathbf{A}^{(2)}\Pi^{(2)}\|_{F}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{N^{(2)}\log N_{R}}{M}},

Proof.

This can be easily obtained from the results of Lemma
G.2

\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\Pi^{(1)}\|_{1}\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{N^{(1)}}\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\Pi^{(1)}\|_{F}\lesssim N^{(1)}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}

∎

Lemma H.2

(Mode 3 matricization)
.

Suppose we get
\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}
from the column-normalized
\text{diag}({\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(3)}}_{\star 1})\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(3)}
. Under conditions in Lemma
G.2
hold, then we have with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
,

\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}-\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\Pi^{(3)}\|_{1}\lesssim\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}

Proof.

Define

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}=\text{diag}({{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(3)}}_{\star 1})\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(3)}\text{ and }\mathbf{A}^{*}=\text{diag}({\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{\star 1})\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(3)}}.

(76)

Note
|\mathbf{{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(3)}_{r1}|\leqslant 2|\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{r1}|\leqslant C^{*}f_{r}
by (
69
). Then with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
, for any
r\in[R]
, if
f_{r}\geqslant\sqrt{\frac{\log(N_{R})}{N^{(1,2)}M}}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{r\star}-\Pi^{(3)\top}\mathbf{A}^{*}_{r\star}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(3)}_{r1}\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(3)}_{r\star}-\Pi^{(3)\top}\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{r1}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(3)}_{r\star}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant|\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}^{(3)}_{r1}|\|\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(3)}_{r\star}-\Pi^{(3)\top}\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(3)}_{r\star}\|_{1}+|\mathbf{{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(3)}_{r1}-\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}_{r1}|\|\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(3)}_{r\star}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim f_{r}\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}\text{ from Lemma \ref{lem: hat omega bound} and }\|\mathbf{\Omega}^{*(3)}_{r\star}\|_{1}=1

Note that the final estimator
\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}
is defined as column-normalized
\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}
.
Also,
\mathbf{A}^{*}=\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\cdot\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})
(by (
10
) and (
76
)).
We have for each
r\in[R]
and
k\in[K^{{(3)}}]
,

\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}_{rk}=\frac{\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{rk}}{\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star k}\|_{1}}\text{ and }\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{rk}=\frac{\mathbf{A}^{*}_{rk}}{\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{k1}}

Given that
\|\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{\star k}\|_{1}=1
, we have
\|\mathbf{A}^{*}_{\star\pi(k)}\|_{1}=\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{k1}
. Consequently,
|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}_{r\pi(k)}-\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{rk}|\leqslant\frac{1}{\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star\pi(k)}\|_{1}}\left|\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{r\pi(k)}^{*}-\mathbf{A}_{rk}^{*}\right|+\frac{\left|\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star\pi(k)}\|_{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{k1}\right|}{\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star\pi(k)}\|_{1}}\left|\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{rk}\right|
. Then,

\displaystyle\left|\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star\pi(k)}\|_{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{k1}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star\pi(k)}\|_{1}-\|\mathbf{A}^{*}_{\star k}\|_{1}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star\pi(k)}-\mathbf{A}^{*}_{\star k}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{r=1}^{R}|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{r\pi(k)}-\mathbf{A}^{*}_{rk}|\leqslant\sum_{r=1}^{R}\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{r\star}-\Pi^{(3)\top}\mathbf{A}^{*}_{r\star}\|_{1}

(77)

Combining this with
\sum_{r}f_{r}=K
,
\|\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{r\star}\|_{1}=f_{r}
and
\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{\star k}^{*}\|_{1}\geqslant\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{k1}/2\geqslant c^{*}
by (
68
), we have

\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}_{r\star}-\Pi^{(3)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{r\star}\|_{1}\leqslant C^{*}\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{r\star}^{*}-\Pi^{(3)\top}\mathbf{A}_{r\star}^{*}\|_{1}\lesssim f_{r}\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}

Therefore, we have

\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}-\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\Pi^{(3)}\|_{1}\leqslant\sum_{r}\|\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{r\star}\|_{1}\cdot\max_{r}\left\{\frac{\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}_{r\star}-\Pi^{(3)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{r\star}\|_{1}}{\|\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{r\star}\|_{1}}\right\}\lesssim\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}

∎

Appendix I
Core Tensor Recovery

Lemma I.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{G}\cdot(\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)})
be a tensor following the Tensor Topic Model explicited in equation (
4
).
Given its HOSVD decomposition
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{S}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}\right)
such that
\mathcal{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{K^{(1)}\times K^{(2)}\times K^{(3)}}
, if Assumption
2.1
is satisfied, then there exist some invertible matrices
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)}
, and
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}
such that

\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{S}\cdot\left(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)},\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)},\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\right).

Proof.

Note
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}
is the matrix of
K^{(a)}
leading right singular vectors from SVD of mode-a matricization
\mathcal{M}_{a}(\mathcal{D})
(so
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)\top}\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}=I_{K^{(a)}}
), and
where the
K^{(1)}\times K^{(2)}\times K^{(3)}
core tensor
\mathcal{S}
is defined as

\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{D}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)\top},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)\top},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)\top}\right)

Recall for any
a\in[3]
, by Lemma
E.1
(bullet point 4), since
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}
corresponds to the left singular vectors of the matricized data
\mathbf{D}^{(a)}
, we have
\mathbf{\Xi}^{(a)}=\mathbf{A}^{(a)}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
where
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
is invertible. Considering in particular the mode-1 matricization of
\mathcal{D}
, we note that:

\displaystyle\qquad\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{S}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}\right))=\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{S}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}\right))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{S})\left(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)\top}\otimes\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)\top}\right)\qquad\text{(Lemma 4 of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{zhang2018tensor}{}{}]})}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{S})\left[\left(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(2)\top}\right)\otimes\left(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(3)\top}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{S})\left(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)\top}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)\top}\right)\left(\mathbf{A}^{(2)\top}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(3)\top}\right)

By (
4
), we also have:

\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{D})=\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{G}\cdot(\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)}))=\mathbf{A}^{(1)}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{G})(\mathbf{A}^{(2)\top}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(3)\top})\\

Therefore, since
\mathbf{A}^{(1)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
is invertible (Assumption
2.1
), and since the minimum eigenvalue of

(\mathbf{A}^{(2)\top}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(3)\top})(\mathbf{A}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(3)})=\mathbf{A}^{(2)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{A}^{(3)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
is
\sigma_{K^{(2)}}(\mathbf{A}^{(2)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(2)})\sigma_{K^{(3)}}(\mathbf{A}^{(3)\top}\mathbf{A}^{(3)})\geq c^{*}>0
(Assumption
2.1
) and the matrix is thus invertible, we simply take:

\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{G})=\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{S})\left(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)\top}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)\top}\right)

to get the result.
∎

Lemma I.2
.

Define
\hat{\mathcal{S}}:=\mathcal{Y}\cdot\left(\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(1)\top},\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(2)\top},\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(3)\top}\right)
, Under the conditions of Lemma
E.3

then we have with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})

\|\hat{\mathcal{S}}-\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbf{O}^{(1)},\mathbf{O}^{(2)},\mathbf{O}^{(3)}\right)\|_{F}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}

Proof.

Given the HOSVD form
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{S}\cdot(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)},\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)})
,

\displaystyle\|\hat{\mathcal{S}}-\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbf{O}^{(1)},\mathbf{O}^{(2)},\mathbf{O}^{(3)}\right)\|_{F}=\|\mathcal{M}_{1}(\hat{\mathcal{S}})-\mathbf{O}^{(1)}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{S})\left(\mathbf{O}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{O}^{(3)}\right)^{\top}\|_{F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(1)\top}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})\left(\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(3)}\right)-\mathbf{O}^{(1)}\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)\top}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{D})\left(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}\right)\left(\mathbf{O}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{O}^{(3)}\right)^{\top}\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)}\mathbf{O}^{(1)\top}\right)^{\top}\left(\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})-\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{D})\right)\left(\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(3)}\right)\right\|_{F}

(S.1)

\displaystyle+\left\|\left(\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)}\mathbf{O}^{(1)}\right)^{\top}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{D})\left(\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(3)}\right)\right\|_{F}

(S.2)

\displaystyle+\left\|\mathbf{O}^{(1)}\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)\top}\left(\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{Y})-\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{D})\right)\left(\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(3)}\right)\right\|_{F}

(S.3)

\displaystyle+\left\|\mathbf{O}^{(1)}\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)\top}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{D})\left[\left(\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(3)}\right)-\left(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}\right)\left(\mathbf{O}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{O}^{(3)}\right)^{\top}\right]\right\|_{F}.

(S.4)

We use the property of sub-multiplicative matrix norm to bound equations (
S.1
) to (
S.4
), respectively.

•

Applying Lemmas
E.3
,
D.4
and the fact that for any matrix
\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
such that
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|\mathbf{A}_{j\star}\|^{2}_{2}
, we have

\eqref{eq:S.1}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}

•

Use the fact that
\|\mathbf{D}^{(1)}\|_{F}^{2}\leqslant\|\mathbf{D}^{(1)}\|_{1}\leqslant N^{(1)}N^{(2)}
, we apply (
55
) again to have

\eqref{eq:S.2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}\cdot\sqrt{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}

•

It is easily observed that
\eqref{eq:S.3}\leqslant\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N^{(1)}}\|\mathbf{Z}^{(1)}_{i\star}\|_{2}^{2}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}
.

•

Using the fact that
\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\hat{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}\|\leqslant\|(\hat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A})\otimes\hat{\mathbf{B}}\|+\|\mathbf{A}\otimes(\hat{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B})\|
, we then have

\eqref{eq:S.4}\lesssim\sqrt{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\cdot\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}\right)\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}

∎

Lemma I.3
.

Define
\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}:=\text{diag}(\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star 1}\|_{1},\dots,\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star K}\|_{1})\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(3)}
, where
\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}=\text{diag}(\mathbf{\mathbf{\hat{\Xi}}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})\mathbf{\hat{\Omega}}^{*(3)}
. Under the settings in Lemma
G.2
we have probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
,

\displaystyle\|\Pi^{(3)}\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\mathbf{O}^{(3)}-\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\|_{F}\lesssim\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}

Proof.

Recall the definition in (
67
),
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}=\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}
. By (
77
) and the fact that
\sum_{r}f_{r}=K^{(3)}
, we have for each
k\in[K^{(3)}]

\left|\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star\pi(k)}\|_{1}-\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{k1}\right|\lesssim\sum_{r}f_{r}\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}\leqslant\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}

Then, let the label permutation
\pi\leftarrow\Pi^{(3)}
,

\displaystyle\left\|\text{diag}(\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star\pi(1)}\|_{1},\dots,\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star\pi(K)}\|_{1})-\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})\right\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k\in[K^{(3)}]}\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/2}

By Corollary
F.0.1
and
f_{r}\geq\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/2}
, we have

\|\Pi^{(3)}\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(3)}\mathbf{O}^{(3)}-\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}\|_{F}^{2}\leqslant\sum_{k\in[K^{(3)}]}\|\mathbf{O}^{(3)\top}\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(3)}_{\pi(k)\star}-\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}_{k\star}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/2}

Note
\|\mathbf{V}^{*}\|\leqslant c^{*}
by Lemma
F.4
, and
\|\text{diag}(\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star\pi(1)}\|_{1},\dots,\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star\pi(K)}\|_{1})\|_{2}\leqslant\max_{k}\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star k}\|_{1}\leqslant\sum_{r}f_{r}=K^{(3)}
.
Combining these results above, we use the fact
\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}\hat{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}\|\leqslant\|(\hat{\mathbf{A}}-\mathbf{A})\hat{\mathbf{B}}\|+\|\mathbf{A}(\hat{\mathbf{B}}-\mathbf{B})\|
again. By definitions,
\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}=\text{diag}(\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star 1}\|_{1},\dots,\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{*}_{\star K}\|_{1},\dots)\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(3)}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}=\text{diag}(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}_{\star 1})\mathbf{V}^{*(3)}
, we have

\|\Pi^{(3)}\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\mathbf{O}^{(3)}-\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\|_{F}\lesssim\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}

∎

Lemma I.4
.

Let
\mathcal{\tilde{G}}=\mathcal{\mathbf{\hat{S}}}\cdot\left(\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)},\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)},\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\right)
where
\mathcal{\mathbf{\hat{S}}},\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}
are defined in lemmas
I.2
and
I.3
, respectively. Let
\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}=\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(1)}
and
\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)}=\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(2)}
. Define
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
such that
\mathcal{M}_{3}(\hat{\mathcal{G}})
is the column-normalized of
\mathcal{M}_{3}(\tilde{\mathcal{G}})
.
Under the condition in Lemma
G.2
, with probability at least
1-o(N_{R})
, we have

\|\mathcal{G}\cdot\left(\Pi^{(1)\top},\Pi^{(2)\top},\Pi^{(3)\top}\right)-\hat{\mathcal{G}}\|_{1}\leqslant C^{*}\max\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}},\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1,2)}M}\right)^{1/4}\right\}

Proof.

By the definitions of
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{S}\cdot\left(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)},\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)},\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\right)
in Lemma
I.1
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}\cdot\left(\Pi^{(1)\top},\Pi^{(2)\top},\Pi^{(3)\top}\right)-\tilde{\mathcal{G}}\|_{F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\Pi^{(1)\top}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{S})\left(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)\top}\Pi^{(2)}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)\top}\Pi^{(3)}\right)-\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\hat{\mathcal{S}})\left(\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)\top}\otimes\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)\top}\right)\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\Pi^{(1)\top}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}-\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\mathbf{O}^{(1)}\right)\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{S})\left[\left(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)\top}\Pi^{(2)}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)\top}\Pi^{(3)}\right)-\left(\mathbf{O}^{(2)\top}\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)\top}\otimes\mathbf{O}^{(3)\top}\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)\top}\right)\right]\right\|_{F}

(G.1)

\displaystyle+\left\|\left(\Pi^{(1)\top}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}-\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\mathbf{O}^{(1)}\right)\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{S})\left(\mathbf{O}^{(2)\top}\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)\top}\otimes\mathbf{O}^{(3)\top}\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)\top}\right)\right\|_{F}

(G.2)

\displaystyle+\left\|\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\mathbf{O}^{(1)}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{S})\left[\left(\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)\top}\Pi^{(2)}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)\top}\Pi^{(3)}\right)-\left(\mathbf{O}^{(2)\top}\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)\top}\otimes\mathbf{O}^{(3)\top}\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)\top}\right)\right]\right\|_{F}

(G.3)

\displaystyle+\left\|\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\left(\mathbf{O}^{(1)}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{S})\left(\mathbf{O}^{(2)\top}\otimes\mathbf{O}^{(3)\top}\right)-\mathcal{M}_{1}(\hat{\mathcal{S}})\right)\left(\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)\top}\otimes\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)\top}\right)\right\|_{F}

(G.4)

Our plan is to bound the four terms (
G.1
) to (
G.4
) separately as follows. By the definitions of
\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}
,
\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)}
and
\mathbf{V}^{*(a)}=\mathbf{V}^{(a)}=\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(a)}
for
a\in\{1,2\}
, we apply Corollary
F.0.1
to have
\left\|\Pi^{(1)\top}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}-\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\mathbf{O}^{(1)\top}\right\|_{F}\leqslant\|\mathbf{V}^{*(1)}-\Pi^{(1)}\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(1)}\mathbf{O}^{(1)}\|_{F}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}M}}
. Similarly,
\left\|\Pi^{(2)\top}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)}-\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)}\mathbf{O}^{(2)\top}\right\|_{F}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(2)}M}}
. Lemma
I.3
gives
\left\|\Pi^{(3)\top}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}-\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\mathbf{O}^{(3)\top}\right\|_{F}\lesssim\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}
. By the definition of
\mathcal{S}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}\|_{F}=\|\mathbf{\Xi}^{(1)\top}\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{D})\left(\mathbf{\Xi}^{(2)}\otimes\mathbf{\Xi}^{(3)}\right)\|_{F}\leqslant\|\mathbf{D}^{(1)}\|_{F}\leqslant\sqrt{N^{(1,2)}}

Note
\|\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\|_{F}=K^{(1)}\|\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\|_{2}\leqslant K^{(1)}\frac{1}{\sigma_{K}(\mathbf{A}^{(1)})}\leqslant\frac{c^{*}}{\sqrt{N^{(1)}}}\leqslant c^{*}
by assumption
2.1
. Using Corollary
F.0.1
, we have

\displaystyle\|\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\mathbf{O}^{(1)\top}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\|_{F}+\|\mathbf{V}^{*(1)}-\Pi^{(1)}\hat{\mathbf{V}}^{*(1)}\mathbf{O}^{(1)}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leqslant C^{*}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{(1)}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}M}}\right)\leqslant C^{*}\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{(1)}}}

Similarly,
\|\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)}\|_{F}\leqslant c^{*}
and
\|\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)}\mathbf{O}^{(2)\top}\|_{F}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{(2)}}}
.
\|\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\|_{F}\leqslant K^{(3)}\|\tilde{V}^{(3)}\|_{2}\leqslant K^{(3)}\frac{1}{\sigma_{K}(\mathbf{A}^{(3)})}\leqslant C^{*}
and then
\|\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\mathbf{O}^{(3)}\|_{F}\leqslant C^{*}
by lemma
I.3
.

•

For (
G.1
):

\displaystyle\eqref{eq: G.1}\leqslant

\displaystyle\left\|\Pi^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}-\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(1)}\mathbf{O}^{(1)\top}\right\|_{F}\cdot\|\mathcal{S}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\left\|\Pi^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)}-\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)}\mathbf{O}^{(2)\top}\right\|_{F}\|\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\|_{F}+\left\|\Pi^{\top}\tilde{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}-\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(3)}\mathbf{O}^{(3)\top}\right\|_{F}\|\check{\mathbf{V}}^{(2)}\mathbf{O}^{(2)\top}\|_{F}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}M}}\cdot\sqrt{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\cdot\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(2)}M}}+\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{(2)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}+\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}\right)^{3/4}\left(\frac{1}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\right)^{1/4}

•

For (
G.2
):

\eqref{eq: G.2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}M}}\sqrt{N^{(1,2)}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{(2)}}}\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}

•

For (
G.3
):

\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{(1)}}}\cdot\sqrt{N^{(1,2)}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(2)}M}}+\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{(2)}}}\right)\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}+\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}

•

For (
G.4
):
Lemma
I.2
gives

\eqref{eq: G.4}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{N^{(1,2)}\log N_{R}}{M}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{N^{(1,2)}}}\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}}

Combined all of these and
\|\mathbf{A}\|_{1}\leqslant\sqrt{K}\|\mathbf{A}\|_{F}
,
we have

\|\mathcal{G}\cdot\left(\Pi^{(1)\top},\Pi^{(2)\top},\Pi^{(3)\top}\right)-{\mathcal{\tilde{G}}}\|_{1}\leqslant C^{*}\max\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}},\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1,2)}M}\right)^{1/4}\right\}

Using the techniques in (
75
), we have

\|\mathcal{G}\cdot\left(\Pi^{(1)\top},\Pi^{(2)\top},\Pi^{(3)\top}\right)-\hat{\mathcal{G}}\|_{1}\leqslant C^{*}\max\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{M}},\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1,2)}M}\right)^{1/4}\right\}

∎

Appendix J
Proof of Theorem
2.4
and Lemma
2.2

Theorem
2.4
is easy to obtain by the results from Lemmas
H.1
,
H.2
and
I.4
. For the sparse tensor topic modeling, the proof is readaptation of the Section E in
[
52
]
.
Consider the oracle sets such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\pm}:=\left\{r\in[R]:\frac{1}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}}\sum_{i\in[N^{(1)}],j\in[N^{(2)}]}\mathcal{D}_{ijr}\right\}>\alpha_{\pm}c^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}

where
c^{\prime}
is a constant defined as in set
\mathcal{J}
and
\alpha_{-}>1
and
\alpha_{+}\in(0,1)
for some suitably chosen constants.

Proposition J.1

(Lemma E.1 in
[
52
]
)
.

Suppose the matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
follows the column-wise
\ell_{q}
sparsity in Assumption
2.4
. If
M\gtrsim\log N_{R}
and assumption
2.1
holds, then with probability at least
1-o(N_{R}^{-1})
,
the event
\mathcal{E}:=\{\mathcal{J}_{-}\subseteq\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{J}_{+}\}
occurs,
\min_{r\in[\mathcal{J}]}f_{r}>\alpha_{+}c^{\prime}\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}
, and

\|\mathbf{A}^{(3)}_{\mathcal{J}^{c}\star}\|_{1}\lesssim\frac{1}{1-q}s_{0}\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{\frac{1-q}{2}}

We apply this proposition to adapt the proof for sparse tensor topic modeling. Observe that:

\displaystyle\|\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}-\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\Pi^{(3)}\|_{1}=\sum_{r\in\mathcal{J}}\|(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}-\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\Pi^{(3)})_{r\star}\|_{1}+\sum_{r\in\mathcal{J}^{c}}\|(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}-\mathbf{A}^{(3)}\Pi^{(3)})_{r\star}\|_{1}.

For the first part, Proposition
J.1
indicates that
\min_{r\in[\mathcal{J}]}f_{r}\gtrsim\sqrt{\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}}
which meets the assumption in Lemma
H.2
. Consequently, the first term has the same rate as before,
\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/4}
. For the second part, where we estimate the rows of
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
corresponding to indices in
\mathcal{J}^{c}
as zero. Proposition
J.1
provides the corresponding bound for the second term as well. With this, we complete the proof for the
\ell
-1 error of the estimator
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
in the context of sparse tensor topic modeling.

Remark that the subsequent estimation procedures, including HOSVD and core recovery, rely on the set
\mathcal{J}
where
\min_{r\in\mathcal{J}}f_{r}\gtrsim\left(\frac{\log N_{R}}{N^{(1)}N^{(2)}M}\right)^{1/2}
. Consequently, the error rates for

\mathbf{A}^{(1)}

\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
and
\mathcal{G}

remain consistent with previous results.

Appendix K
Benchmarks

K.1
Tensor LDA

To compare our method with an extension of LDA to the tensor setting, we implement its Bayesian counterpart – that is, we endow each latent variable with a Dirichlet prior. The full model is detailed below:

\phi_{k}\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\beta),\quad\forall k\in\{1,\dots,K_{3}\}

\theta_{n_{1}}\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\alpha_{1}),\quad\forall n_{1}\in\{1,\dots,N_{1}\}

\theta_{n_{2}}\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\alpha_{2}),\quad\forall n_{2}\in\{1,\dots,N_{2}\}

G_{k_{1},k_{2},\cdot}\sim\text{Dirichlet}(\alpha_{3}),\quad\forall k_{1}\in\{1,\dots,K_{1}\},\,k_{2}\in\{1,\dots,K_{2}\}

\theta_{n_{1},n_{2},k_{3}}=\sum_{k1=1}^{K_{1}}\sum_{k2=1}^{K_{2}}G_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}\cdot\theta_{n_{1}k_{1}}\cdot\theta_{n_{2}k_{2}},\quad\forall k_{3}\in\{1,\dots,K\}

\mathcal{X}_{n_{1},n_{2},\star}\sim\text{Multinomial}(M,\theta_{n_{1},n_{2},\star}\phi).

\alpha_{3}

\alpha_{2}

\theta_{n_{1},n_{2},k_{3}}

Plate Diagram for the tensor LDA model

In practice, we set the hyperparameters
\beta,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
and
\alpha_{3}
to 1. To fit this model, we use the variational Bayes provided in RStan
[
20
]
. This provides a fast and scalable solution to the model specified above. Throughout the paper, we report the average of the posterior estimated from the data using this model.

K.2
Hybrid LDA

The Hybrid LDA model for tensor topic modeling is a three-step algorithm that relies on the traditional Latent Dirichlet allocation algorithm to estimate the topics. The different latent variables are fitted as follows:

•

Step 1: Estimation of
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
through LDA
. The first step consists in the estimation of the topic matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
using the matricized matrix
\mathbf{D}^{(3)}=\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathcal{D})\in\mathbb{R}^{R\times N_{1}N_{2}}
. To this end, we use the traditional Latent Dirichlet Allocation model, and recover a posterior distribution for the topic matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
as well as the topic proportions
\mathbf{W}^{(3)}
. We take
\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}
to be the posterior mean of the LDA topic matrix. We note that this step is performed blindly of any existing correlation between documents corresponding to the same entry in mode 1 or 2 (i.e. for each
i
and each
j
, the rows of the matrices
\mathcal{D}_{i\star\star}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\star j\star}
are treated independently).

•

Step 2: Estimation of
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
. Having estimated
\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{(3)}
, we then estimate
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
from the estimated mixture matrix
\mathbf{W}^{(3)}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{3}\times N_{1}N_{2}}
.

•

Step 3: Estimation of the core
\mathcal{G}
through constrained regression.
Having estimated all the latent factor matrices, we propose estimating the core through the following estimation procedure:

\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{G}}\in\text{argmin}\|\mathcal{Y}-\mathcal{G}\cdot(\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{2},\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{3})\|^{2}\end{split}

(78)

\alpha
\theta_{n_{1}n_{2}}
z_{m,n}
w_{m,n}
\beta

N_{m}

\forall n_{1}\in[N_{1}],n_{2}\in[N_{2}]

L.1
Tucker decomposition: Illustration and Comparison

In this section, we provide a detailed discussion and illustration of why Tucker decomposition is our preferred approach for tensor topic modeling in multivariate data applications, rather than matrix decomposition or the
CANDECOMP/PARAFAC
(CP) decomposition. Using the tensor data shown in Figure
21
, we compare our Tucker decomposition-based method with Nonnegative CP decomposition implemented in the
rTensor
package and structural topic modeling (STM) utilizing supervised matrix decomposition from the
stm
package.
The two key questions we aim to address are:
(i) Can we identify the presence of two distinct core groups along mode 1 (the document axis)?
(ii) Can we detect the topic shift within the mixed membership group between time periods 5 and 6 along mode 2 (the time axis)?

To address the questions above, we first apply our method to the entire tensor using an input rank of
(2,2,3)
, obtaining the Tucker factor matrices
\mathbf{A}^{(1)},\mathbf{A}^{(2)},\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
and the core tensor
\mathcal{G}
. The results for this example are presented in Figure
22
.

The factor matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
effectively identifies two primary groups along the first mode (document axis): group 1 and group 2, corresponding to the first and last ten indices of the first dimension, respectively. Additionally, the middle 10 rows of
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
represent documents with mixed membership between the two groups. The matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
captures interactions reflecting topic evolution along the second dimension (e.g., time).

The core tensor, illustrated in Figure
22
, reveals interactions between topics and latent clusters across dimensions. Notably, the topic proportions for document cluster 1, especially those aligned with topic 2, remain stable despite a significant event occurring between time periods 5 and 6. In contrast, documents in cluster 2 exhibit a marked shift in preferences during this period, transitioning from topic 3 to topic 1.

Tucker decomposition offers greater flexibility by allowing different ranks for each mode of the tensor, making it well-suited for datasets with varying levels of complexity across modes such as customer segments, time periods, and product categories. In this synthetic dataset, Tucker decomposition effectively captures these diverse structures.

In contrast, CP decomposition enforces the same rank across all modes, which can be overly restrictive in scenarios where document clusters (first mode), time (second mode), and product categories (third mode) exhibit distinct underlying patterns. For example, in NNCPD, the matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}
represents clusters associations,
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
captures latent time clusters, and
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
reflects word-topic associations. While Figure
23(a)
shows that topic 2 is associated primarily with cluster 1 during time periods 6–10 and topic 1 is linked to cluster 2 in time periods 1–5, it fails to clearly isolate the topic change occurring exclusively within the mixed membership group between time periods 5 and 6. Increasing the rank to 4, as shown in Figure
23(b)
, does not resolve this limitation, underscoring that higher ranks alone cannot fully capture such nuanced changes, and highlighting the need for more sophisticated decompositions that incorporate additional interactions.

Matrix decomposition is inherently limited to two modes (e.g., customers at a given time and products), which fails to preserve the multidimensional relationships in the data. Post-processing is often required to extract interactions between modes, but this approach introduces challenges and may not always yield meaningful insights. Figure
24(a)
illustrates results from standard Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA)
[
9
]
, while Figure
24(b)
presents supervised LDA results from Structural Topic Modeling (STM). The document-topic matrix
\mathbf{W}^{(3)}
in STM demonstrates clearer stripe patterns, reflecting the benefits of incorporating structural information like time stamps and document groups, leading to more accurate reconstructions. However, proper post-processing of
\mathbf{W}^{(3)}
is essential for analyzing interactions between observed changes and customer groups. Figure
25(a)
shows a failed case where Hybrid-LDA’s core tensor illustrates topic changes over time but fails to capture the transition between time points 5 and 6 in
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
.

By contrast, STM effectively identifies the timing of the event and aligns its core tensor with the expected model behavior, albeit with slightly higher reconstruction error. While STM is robust, as it employs multiple EM algorithm runs to ensure convergence, it requires external information, limiting its applicability in real-world scenarios where such data might be unavailable. In comparison, our method is unsupervised, offering greater flexibility and applicability without relying on additional inputs. For further details on its application to real-world datasets, we refer readers to Section
4
.

Appendix M
Real experiments: additional results

This appendix supplements the synthetic experiments presented in the main text.

M.1
Arvix’s Paper

Preprocessing:

The raw repository consists of 1.7 million articles from the vast branches of physics to the many subdisciplines of computer science to everything in between, including math, statistics, electrical engineering, quantitative biology, and economics. The metadata included information such as paper identifiers, titles, abstracts, publication dates, categories, and author information. Each paper in the arXiv repository is assigned one or more categories that follow a hierarchical structure, typically in the format
\text{main}\_\text{category}
.subcategory (e.g., cs.AI for Artificial Intelligence in computer science). For our analysis, we pre-processed the arXiv dataset by randomly selecting up to 500 article abstracts from four distinct categories: mathematics, physics, computer science, and statistics. We then categorized these articles into two main clusters: theoretical and application. The mapping was based on the nature of the research topics.

•

The theoretical categories included, but were not limited to:

–

Mathematics: Algebraic Geometry (math.AG), Analysis of PDEs (math.AP), and Combinatorics (math.CO)

–

Statistics: Statistics
Theory (stat.TH).

–

Computer Science: Computational Complexity (cs.CC), Formal Languages and Automata Theory (cs.FL), and Logic in Computer Science (cs.LO).

–

Physics: High Energy Physics - Theory (hep-th), General Relativity and Quantum Cosmology (gr-qc), and Quantum Physics (quant-ph).

•

The application categories encompassed areas such as:

–

Computer Science Applications: Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI), Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (cs.CV), Machine Learning (cs.LG), and Robotics (cs.RO).

–

Applied Physics and Engineering: Applied Physics (physics.app-ph), Medical Physics (physics.med-ph).

–

Statistics: Statistics Applications (stat.AP), Computation (stat.CO), and Machine Learning (stat.ML).

The selected data has been summarized in the following two tables:

M.2
Sub-communities of the vaginal microbiota in non-pregnant women

Additional results for (i):

Here, we include the
alto
plot to visualize topic refinement and coherence, as well as topic resolution for STM (Structural Topic Model). However, STM performs the worst in both the topic refinement and topic resolution plots, suggesting that it places too much emphasis on enhancing relationships derived from external information. This over-reliance on external covariates makes STM overly sensitive to both the selection of data and changes in the number of topics, ultimately affecting its performance and robustness in recovering
\mathbf{A}^{(3)}
.

(a)

Topic refinement

(b)

Topic resolution

Figure 29
:
Topic refinement and resolution as a function of
K^{(3)}
on the vaginal ecosystem Data
[
51
]
given from STM. Vertical error bars represent the interquartile range for the average topic resolution scores over 25 trials.

Additional results for (ii):

Compared to the post-processing matrix methods, such as STM and Hybrid-LDA, the Tucker-based decomposition methods (as shown in Figure
30
), including our method (TTM-HOSVD), Tensor LDA, and NTD, show more significant points in the document-topic matrix
\mathbf{W}^{(3)}
. This indicates that the proportion of vaginal microbiota is strongly related to demographic characteristics and menstrual phases, and that these relationships could clearly identified through the menstrual phases and the group of subjects, respectively. In this data example, using Tucker decomposition facilitates the detection of this significant relationship, as it could further provides how different phases and subject characteristics influence the vaginal microbiota composition.

Upon closer examination of matrix component
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
, we observed that STM extracts more significant points than LDA by utilizing external information, as shown in Figures
30(k)
and
30(h)
. Other methods, including ours, also perform well in Dirichlet regression on

\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
. However, when we further investigate the heatmap of proportions in
\mathbf{A}^{(2)}
in Figure
31
, our method outperforms the others in recovering the theoretical menstrual phases. Both Hybrid-LDA and our method show similar patterns, but in Hybrid LDA, day ‘-6’ appears closer to the phases on days ‘-7’ and ‘-8’, which should correspond to ovulation. In contrast, our method captures a mixed pattern on day ‘-6’, reflecting a transition between the ovulation phase (days ‘-7’ and ‘-8’) and the luteal phase (days ‘-2’ to ‘-5’), as expected. In comparison, STM provides a more mixed distinction between the luteal, menstrual, and follicular phases, making it harder to differentiate between these phases clearly. On the other hand, Tensor LDA tends to assign too much weight to a single phase, causing a disproportionate focus on one menstrual phase, which deviates from the expected patterns. NTD places more weight on the time after menstruation, and associates it more closely with the luteal phase instead of the follicular phase, which is contradiction to the phase progression.

Matrix component

Additional results for (iii)

From Figure
33
, in group 3 of subjects (which aligns with White) during time phase 1 (the theoretical follicular phase), microbiome topic 1 (dominated by L. gasseri) as characterized by Hybrid-LDA appears more active than microbiome topic 5 (dominated by L. iners). However, this pattern contradicts Figure
32
, where the proportion of topic 5 (v5) in White subjects from day 2 to day 6 is clearly higher than that of topic 1 (v1). It is also inconsistent with other Tucker-decomposition-based methods, which further suggests that the post-processing method can distort the results if the intermediate step of estimating
\mathbf{W}^{(3)}
is not sufficiently close to the ground truth. This highlights the importance of Tucker-decomposition-based methods for date with tensor structure. Additionally, Tensor-LDA produces the most sparse core proportion, as the Bayesian framework promotes sparse solutions through priors. In contrast, NTD results in the least sparse core, as KL minimization tends to distribute data more evenly across components, capturing a broader range of interactions. Our HOSVD-based method lies between the two. It captures the change in mixed-topic dynamics, allowing us to model both the prominent topics and the subtle transitions between them.

M.3
Market-basket analysis

Preprocessing

The original Dunnhumby dataset consists of a list of 2 million transactions corresponding to the shopping baskets of 2,500 households at a retailer over a period of 2 years.
We pre-process this dataset using the following steps.

•

We process item names to remove content in parentheses (e.g. ”tomatoes (vine)” becomes ”tomatoes”).

•

We aggregate items according to their cleaned name and category, thereby eliminating all information pertaining quantities (e.g. “canned tomatoes 16 oz” and “canned tomatoes 32 oz” are both equivalent to “tomatoes”).

•

We discard items that appear less than 10 times in the dataset, as well as items that are too frequent to be informative (defined here as items occurring in 15% of all baskets).

•

We keep baskets that are big enough to determine topics (e.g. bigger than 1 purchase)

•

We keep households who appear in the dataset at least once every two weeks. We aggregate baskets if they appear several times in a two-week window.
